Constrained Systems, Generalized Hamilton–Jacobi Actions, and Quantization Thanks: This research was (partly) supported by the NCCR SwissMAP, funded by the Swiss National Science Foundation. A.S.C. and K.W. acknowledge partial support of SNF Grant No. 200020_192080. K. W. also acknowledges support from a BMS Dirichlet postdoctoral fellowship and the SNF Postdoc.Mobility grant P2ZHP2_184083, and would like to thank the Humboldt-Universität Berlin, in particular the group of Dirk Kreimer, and the university of Notre Dame for their hospitality.
Alberto S. Cattaneo, Pavel Mnev, Konstantin Wernli

Abstract
. Mechanical systems (i.e., one-dimensional field theories) with constraints are the focus of this paper. In the classical theory, systems with infinite-dimensional targets are considered as well (this then encompasses also higher-dimensional field theories in the hamiltonian formalism). The properties of the Hamilton–Jacobi (HJ) action are described in details and several examples are explicitly computed (including nonabelian Chern–Simons theory, where the HJ action turns out to be the gauged Wess–Zumino–Witten action). Perturbative quantization, limited in this note to finite-dimensional targets, is performed in the framework of the Batalin–Vilkovisky (BV) formalism in the bulk and of the Batalin–Fradkin–Vilkovisky (BFV) formalism at the endpoints. As a sanity check of the method, it is proved that the semiclassical contribution of the physical part of the evolution operator is still given by the HJ action. Several examples are computed explicitly. In particular, it is shown that the toy model for nonabelian Chern–Simons theory and the toy model for 7D Chern–Simons theory with nonlinear Hitchin polarization do not have quantum corrections in the physical part (the extension of these results to the actual cases is discussed in the companion paper [ 21 ] ). Background material for both the classical part (symplectic geometry, generalized generating functions, HJ actions, and the extension of these concepts to infinite-dimensional manifolds) and the quantum part (BV-BFV formalism) is provided.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
81T70, 53D22, 70H20, 53D55, 53D50 (Primary); 81T13, 81S10, 70H15, 57R56, 81T45 (Secondary).

1.
Introduction

The Hamilton–Jacobi (HJ) action for a nondegenerate system is a generating function for the graph of the hamiltonian flow of the system and is obtained by substituting a solution of the Euler–Lagrange (EL) equations into the action functional. We extend this result to degenerate systems (i.e., systems with constraints). More precisely, we consider one-dimensional field theories with constraints in involution (a.k.a. first-class constraints), i.e., theories constrained to live in a coisotropic submanifold of the (possibly infinite-dimensional) target. In the finite-dimensional case, we also study their perturbative quantization.

This paper is mainly based on a discussion of examples. The two main general results, on the role of the HJ action, are presented in Theorem
4.2
on page
4.2

at the classical level and in Theorem
11.4
on page
11.4
at the quantum level.

Among the many examples, we discuss in particular Chern–Simons theory and its finite-dimensional toy model versions, get the gauged WZW action as
the generalized generating function of the evolution relation via
the HJ action, and, in the finite-dimensional version, show that there are no quantum corrections in the physical sector.
We will prove the latter result in the infinite-dimensional case in the companion paper
[
21
]
.

We start with a recollection of the known results for a regular mechanical system (i.e., a one-dimensional field theory with regular lagrangian). In this case, the EL equations define a hamiltonian flow and therefore its graph, which we call the evolution relation, is a lagrangian submanifold of the product of the symplectic manifolds associated to the two endpoints (in the framework of this paper the symplectic manifold associated to an endpoint is the target symplectic manifold, up to the sign of the symplectic form which depends on the orientation of the endpoint). The HJ action, defined as the evaluation of the classical action on solutions of the EL equation for a given choice of endpoint polarizations
1
1

1

By polarization we mean, in this paper, an explicit realization of a symplectic manifold as a cotangent bundle. Generating functions, wave functions and integral kernels of operators will always be understood as functions on the base of the cotangent bundle.
(boundary conditions), is then a generating function
2
2

2

A generating function for a lagrangian submanifold
L
of a cotangent bundle is a function on the base that yields
L
as the graph of its differential.
for the evolution relation. At the quantum level, the HJ action appears as the dominant contribution in the semiclassical expansion of Feynman’s path integral (simply as the evaluation of the action on a background classical solution).

In a constrained system, the following differences occur:

(1)

In the framework of this paper,
3
3

3

with the exception of the first part of Section
7.4
which portrays a more general situation
the EL equations split into two classes which we call the evolution equations (those that involve a time derivative) and the constraints (those that do not involve time derivatives). The fields actually split into two classes: maps to the target symplectic manifolds and Lagrange multipliers; the evolution equations arise from variations with respect to the former and the constraints from variations with respect to the latter.

(2)

Solutions of the EL equations still define a lagrangian submanifold---which we keep calling the evolution relation---of the product of the symplectic manifolds associated to the two endpoints. Yet, this submanifold is no longer a graph.
4
4

4

Geometrically, the constraints define a coisotropic submanifold
C
, and the evolution equations describe a motion along a leaf ot the characteristic distribution. The evolution relation then consists of pairs of points of
C
that lie on the same leaf.

(3)

The HJ action, defined as the evaluation of the classical action on solutions of the evolution equation for a given choice of endpoint polarizations, is then a generalized generating function
5
5

5

A generalized generating function is a generating function, see footnote
2
, that depends on additional parameters. It defines the lagrangian submanifold of the given cotangent bundle as the intersection of the graph of its differential with respect to the
base variables
and the vanishing locus of the differential with respect to the additional parameters.
for the evolution relation. Note that the constraints are not used in the definition of the HJ action but are recovered from it (as they appear in the definition of the evolution relation).

(4)

The HJ action depends parametrically on the Lagrange multipliers but is invariant under a (Lie algebra or, more generally, Lie algebroid) gauge transformation thereof that is trivial at the endpoints.

(5)

At the quantum level, the path integral requires a gauge-fixing procedure one has to keep track of. The formalism we use in this paper is the one due to Batalin and Vilkovisky, in particular in its version coupled to the boundary, referred to as the BV-BFV formalism. The HJ action then appears as the physical (i.e., ghost independent) part of
the dominant contribution of the perturbative expansion of the path integral for certain choices of endpoint polarizations and of residual fields (the latter being, in physical parlance, a choice of infrared components, not to be integrated out, of the fields). The HJ action now arises from the resummation of tree diagrams and not from the evaluation of the action on a background classical solution, which is not at hand in the BV-BFV formalism.

Points (3) and (4) are the content of Theorem
4.2
and point (5) of Theorem
11.4
—see also equations (
101
) and (
117
).

One topic we are interested in is the change of (say, final) endpoint polarization. This is realized by a generating function of the corresponding symplectomorphism. At the classical level, the HJ action for the new polarization is simply the composition of the HJ action for the old polarization with the generating function for the change of polarization. At the quantum level, one has to find an appropriate quantization of the latter generating function. There are two, in general irreconcilable, wishes: unitarity and compatibility with the BV-BFV formalism. In this paper we focus on the latter and show that a BV-BFV quantization is possible if the constraints are linear in the momenta of both endpoint polarizations. This is a serious limitation, yet it applies to some very interesting examples.

1.1.
Structure of the paper

This paper consists of three parts.

In the first part (Sections
2
to
6
), we discuss the HJ action for constrained systems (after briefly reviewing the regular, unconstrained case). We discuss several examples (in particular constraints that are linear or affine in both the positions and the momenta), prove Theorem
4.2
, i.e., point (2) of the above list, describe how to incorporate a nontrivial evolution in addition to the constraints, and extend the discussion of generating functions and HJ actions to some bad endpoint conditions.

In the second part (Section
7
), we
compute the HJ action for
several examples with infinite-dimensional target (Chern–Simons theory,
BF
theory, 2D Yang–Mills theory, abelian Yang–Mills theory in any dimension).

In the third part (Sections
8
to
12
), we discuss the BV-BFV quantization. We start with a review of BV and BFV, tailored to the theories we consider in this paper (which are ultimately one-dimensional AKSZ models). Next we discuss the quantization in the case of finite-dimensional targets, both with linear and nonlinear endpoint polarizations, proving in particular Theorem
11.4
, i.e., point (5) of the above list, and show that in the linear and affine examples (the toy models for Chern–Simons theory) no quantum corrections arise in the physical sector: see equations (
108
), (
109
) and (
118
).

In Appendix
A
we review several results on symplectic geometry and on (generalized) generating functions, both in the finite- and in the infinite-dimensional case.

In Appendix
B
we prove a technical result that ensures that the BV-BFV quantization works in the case at hand.

We defer the BV-BFV quantization with infinite-dimensional target (mainly, Chern–Simons theories) to the companion paper
[
21
]
.

1.2.
How to read this paper

The first part is necessary for the whole paper, with the exception of Section
5
which is relevant only for
Sections
7.4
,
11.5
and
11.6
.

The second and the third part can be read independently from each other, as they focus on different developments of the first part.

Appendix
A
contains additional material that completes the discussion in the first part and provides a rigorous foundation for the second part. Appendix
B
contains results for the foundation of the third part.

1.3.
Notations

To simplify the reading of the paper, we collect here some of the main notations and some terminology.

Arguments (e.g., of an action, a HJ action, a partition function) are written in square brackets if they are fields (i.e., if they depend on the variable on the source interval) and in round brackets otherwise.

We will use the subscript HJ to denote a HJ action. We will have several versions thereof, with notations like
S_{\text{HJ}}
,
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
or
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
referring to different incarnations (change of polarization by
f
; dependency on gauge classes of the parameters). This pedantic notation is important to avoid confusion, but the only important point to focus on is that these are all HJ actions in their own way.
In the arguments of HJ actions, or more generaly of generalized generating functions, we will consistenly separate the generalized position variables from the parameters by a semicolon.

In the BFV formalism, the classical master equation (CME) is the equation
\{S,S\}=0
, where
\{\ ,\ \}
is the given even Poisson bracket and

S
is an odd functional. By
\Omega
we will denote a coboundary operator that quantizes
S
.

In the BV formalism, the CME is the equation
(S,S)=0
, where
(\ ,\ )
is the given odd Poisson bracket and

S
is an even functional. By
\Delta
we will denote the BV Laplacian (on functions or on half-densities, depending on the context). The quantum master equation (QME) is the equation
\Delta\psi=0
, where
\psi
is an even function or half-density. For
\psi
of the form
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S}
, under the condition
\Delta S=0
(which we usually assume), the QME for
\psi
is equivalent to the CME for
S
.

In the BV-BFV formalism, where we have both
\Delta
and
\Omega
, the modified quantum master equation (mQME) is the equation
(\Omega+\hbar^{2}\Delta)\psi=0
. We often use the abbreviation
\Delta^{\Omega}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Delta+\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\Omega
.

For a partition function (or a state), we will use the notation
Z
, possibly with some label denoting the case we are considering. If we define it as a half-density, we will use the notation
\hat{Z}
.

1.4.
Teaser

We illustrate one of the results of the paper (summarizing the content of Sections
4.2
,
11.3
, and
12
, with simplified, adapted notations for this introduction).

The data of the system are a quadratic Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, a vector space
Z
and two derivations:
v\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z

and
\bar{v}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z^{*}
.
The target symplectic manifold is
T^{*}(\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z)
which we identify with

\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z\ni(\bar{q},q)
(using the nondegenerate pairing
\langle\ ,\rangle
on
\mathfrak{g}
).
6
6

6

We write
\bar{q}
for the image of the momentum
p
under the isomorphism
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\otimes Z^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z^{*}
.

The fields are a path
(\bar{q},q)\colon[0,1]\to T^{*}(\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z)
and a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued one-form
e
(i.e., a connection one-form) on
[0,1]
. The action functional, a toy model for nonabelian Chern–Simons theory (see Section
7.2
),
7
7

7

In this case, we have
\mathfrak{g}=\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
and
Z=\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma)
, where
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is a finite-dimensional quadratic Lie algebra, and
\Sigma
is a closed, oriented surface with a choice of complex structure. The derivations
v,\bar{v}
are now the Dolbeault operators
\partial,\bar{\partial}
.
is

S[\bar{q},q,e]=\int_{0}^{1}(\langle\bar{q},\mathrm{d}_{e}q\rangle-\langle\bar{q},v(e)\rangle-\langle\bar{v}(e),q\rangle),

where
\mathrm{d}_{e}
denotes the covariant derivative.

Under the assumption that the exponential map to the simply connected Lie group
G
integrating
\mathfrak{g}
is surjective,
we compute the HJ action—for initial value
q_{a}
of
q
, final value
\bar{q}^{b}
of
\bar{q}
, and a parameter
g=\mathrm{e}^{\xi}\in G
—as

\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\bar{q}^{b};g)=-\langle\bar{q}^{b},{g}^{-1}q_{a}g\rangle-\langle\bar{q}^{b},{g}^{-1}v(g)\rangle-\langle\bar{v}(g)g^{-1},q_{a}\rangle-\text{WZW}(g)

with the Wess–Zumino–Witten term

\text{WZW}(g)=\frac{1}{2}\langle\bar{v}(g)g^{-1},v(g)g^{-1}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\langle\bar{v}(h)h^{-1},[v(h)h^{-1},\mathrm{d}hh^{-1}]\rangle,

where
h=\mathrm{e}^{t\xi}
.

The BV-BFV quantization then provides an integral kernel which, in adddition to the classical coordinates
(q_{a},\bar{q}^{b})
, also depends on “ghost variables”
c_{a},c_{b}\in\mathfrak{g}[1]
and is a half-density on
T^{*}[-1]G\ni(g,g^{+})
:

\hat{Z}(q_{a},c_{a},\bar{q}^{b},c_{b};g,g^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\bar{q}^{b};g)}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle g^{+},gc_{a}-c_{b}g\rangle}\,\mu_{G},

where
\mu_{G}
is the Haar measure.

This integral kernel satisfies the modified quantum master equation (mQME)

(\Omega+\hbar^{2}\Delta)\hat{Z}=0
—and is defined up to the image of
\Omega+\hbar^{2}\Delta
—where
\Delta
is the canonical BV Laplacian on half-densities on
T^{*}[-1]G
and

\begin{split}\Omega&=\left\langle\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{a}},v(c_{a})\right\rangle+\left\langle\bar{v}(c_{a}),q_{a}\right\rangle-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\left\langle[c_{a},c_{a}],\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{a}}\right\rangle\\
&\phantom{=}-\left\langle\bar{q}^{b},v(c_{b})\right\rangle-\left\langle\bar{v}(c_{b}),\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{q}^{b}}\right\rangle-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\left\langle[c_{b},c_{b}],\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{b}}\right\rangle\end{split}

is a quantization of the endpoint BFV action describing the coisotropic reduction by the constraints.

In the abelian case, where we use the multiplicative convention for the group
G
,
we write
g=\mathrm{e}^{T}
, and the HJ action becomes

\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\bar{q}^{b};T)=-\langle\bar{q}^{b},q_{a}\rangle-\langle\bar{q}^{b},v(T)\rangle-\langle\bar{v}(T),q_{a}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\bar{v}(T),v(T)\rangle.

In this case
it is also interesting, as a toy model for 7D Chern–Simons theory with endpoint Hitchin polarization (see Section
7.6
), to change the final Darboux coordinates by some generating function
f(q,Q)
with

\langle\bar{q},\mathrm{d}q\rangle=\langle\bar{Q},\mathrm{d}Q\rangle+\mathrm{d}f
. The HJ action
for initial value
q_{a}
of
q
and final value
Q_{b}
of
Q
is then

\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}^{f}(q_{a},Q_{b};T)=-f(q_{a}+v(T),Q_{b})-\langle\bar{v}(T),q_{a}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\bar{v}(T),v(T)\rangle.

The BV-BFV quantization turns out to be possible when the transformed
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
-valued hamiltonian

\tilde{H}(\bar{Q},Q)=v^{*}(\bar{q}(\bar{Q},Q))+\bar{v}^{*}(q(\bar{Q},Q))
, with
*
denoting transposition,
is linear in
\bar{Q}
. In this case, the integral kernel, with
(T,T^{+})\in T^{*}[-1]\mathfrak{g}
, is

\hat{Z}^{f}(q_{a},c_{a},Q_{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};T)}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{a}^{\alpha}-c_{b}^{\alpha})}\sqrt{\mathrm{d}^{k}T\mathrm{d}^{k}T^{+}},

with
k=\dim\mathfrak{g}
. This integral kernel also satisfies the modified quantum master equation (mQME), with

\Delta
the canonical BV Laplacian on half-densities on
T^{*}[-1]\mathfrak{g}
and

\Omega=\left\langle\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{a}},v(c_{a})\right\rangle+\left\langle\bar{v}(c_{a}),q_{a}\right\rangle-\left\langle c_{b},\tilde{H}\left(-\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial Q_{b}},Q_{b}\right)\right\rangle.

Remark 1.1

(Parameters and quantum corrections)
.

In the examples just presented, there are no quantum corrections in the physical part of the partition function
\hat{Z}
. This statement if of course correct only if we compare the classical and quantum theory in the presence of parameters. The point is that usually one can get rid of (some of) the parameters. Let us discuss for simplicity the case when all parameters can be eliminated. In the classical theory, the constraints are recovered by setting to zero the differential of the HJ action as a function of the parameters
g
or
T
(parametrized by the generalized position variables).
If the Hessian is nondegenerate, we can (locally) solve these equations and write the parameters as functions of the generalized position variables. Inserting this back into the HJ action yields a new, equivalent generating function, now without parameters. At the quantum level, we can instead integrate out the parameters
g
or
T
in the partition function, after setting their odd momenta
g^{+}
or
T^{+}
to zero. In the abelian case with linear polarization (generalized position variables
q_{a}
and
\bar{q}_{b}
), this is equivalent to the classical procedure (apart from an irrelevant constant depending on the determinant of the quadratic form
\langle\bar{v}(T),v(T)\rangle
). In the other cases (abelian case with nonlinear polarization and nonabelian case), the quantum procedure yields nontrivial quantum corrections.

2.
Hamilton–Jacobi for nondegenerate actions

In this section we collect several known facts on the Hamilton–Jacobi action, which will be used in the rest of the paper (for a general reference, see, e.g.,
[
3
, Sections 47-48]
).
We only focus on actions of the form

S[p,q]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-H(p,q)\mathrm{d}t),

where a sum over repeated indices is understood. Here
(p,q)
denotes a point (or a path) in the cotangent bundle

T^{*}M
of a fixed manifold
M
, and
H
is a smooth function on
T^{*}M
.
By abuse of notation, we denote by
\mathrm{d}q^{i}
both the coordinate 1-form on
M
and
its pullback by the map
q\colon[t_{a},t_{b}]\to M
(which then
actually means
\dot{q}^{i}\mathrm{d}t
).
We will later be interested in some generalizations, like adding constraints and working with an infinite-dimensional target.

Recall that the Euler–Lagrange (EL) equations for the above action are Hamilton’s equations

\dot{q}^{i}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\quad\dot{p}_{i}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial q^{i}}.

Next assume that for every
q_{a},q_{b}\in M

these equations have a unique solution, which we will denote by
(p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}})
, satisfying

q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{a})=q_{a}
and
q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{b})=q_{b}
. Then one defines the Hamilton–Jacobi (HJ) action (a.k.a. Hamilton’s principal function)

S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S[p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}}].

Jacobi’s theorem then, in particular, asserts that

(1)

p^{q_{a},q_{b}}_{i}(t_{b})=\frac{\partial S_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial q^{i}_{b}},\quad p^{q_{a},q_{b}}_{i}(t_{a})=-\frac{\partial S_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial q^{i}_{a}}.

In other words,
S_{\text{HJ}}
is a generating function for the graph of the hamiltonian flow of
H
(from
t_{a}
to
t_{b}
), see Appendix
A
(in particular, Remark
A.32
).
Let us briefly review the proof because we will need some generalization thereof in the following.
The variation of the action reads

\delta S=\text{\sl EL}+p_{i}(t_{b})\delta q^{i}(t_{b})-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}(t_{a}),

where
EL
denotes the bulk term responsible for the EL equations. If we insert a solution of the EL equations, the
EL
term vanishes. In particular, inserting the solution
(p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}})
yields

\delta S_{\text{HJ}}=p^{q_{a},q_{b}}_{i}(t_{b})\delta q^{i}_{b}-p^{q_{a},q_{b}}_{i}(t_{a})\delta q_{a}^{i},

which implies (
1
).

Remark 2.1

(Time dependency)
.

Above we have considered the time endpoints
t_{a}
and
t_{b}
as fixed, but one can let them vary as well.
We put the explicit dependence in the notation:

S[p,q](t_{a},t_{b})=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-H(p,q)\mathrm{d}t).

The time-dependent HJ action is then

(2)

S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b};t_{a},t_{b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S[p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}}](t_{a},t_{b}),

where the solution is still required to satisfy
q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{a})=q_{a}
and
q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{b})=q_{b}
. As a result,

(p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}})
also depends on
t_{a}
and
t_{b}
(even though we do not include this in the notation). In addition to the formulae in (
1
), we now also have
8
8

8

Equations (
1
) and (
3
) are also important because they imply that
S_{\text{HJ}}
is a solution of the Hamilton–Jacobi equation.

(3)

\frac{\partial S_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial t_{b}}=-H(p^{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{b}),\quad\frac{\partial S_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial t_{a}}=H(p^{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{a}),q_{a}).

To get these formulae, we have to observe that, e.g., a variation
\delta t_{b}
of
t_{b}
produces not only a variation in the final endpoint of the integral, which yields
(p^{q_{a},q_{b}}_{i}(t_{b})\dot{q}_{q_{a},q_{b}}^{i}(t_{b})-H(p^{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{b}))\delta t_{b}
,
but also an induced variation
-\dot{q}_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{b})\delta t_{b}
of
q
at the final endpoint.

Remark 2.2

(Uniqueness)
.

For simplicity we have assumed that there is a unique solution

(p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}})
satisfying

q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{a})=q_{a}
and
q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{b})=q_{b}
. This might not be true. Generically one may exclude focal points
q_{a},q_{b}
connected by a continuous family of solutions,
9
9

9

For instance, if the target
M
is a Riemannian manifold with metric
g
and we take
H(p,q)=\frac{\|p\|_{g}^{2}}{2m}
, then the solutions to the EL equations are cotangent lifts of parametrized geodesics. If
M
is the sphere with round metric and we take antipodal points
q_{a}
and
q_{b}
, then we have a continuous family of solutions joining them. However, it is enough to perturb one of them, to get a discrete family.
but typically we may expect a discrete, or finite, family to exist. In this case, the above procedure is actually meant to work in a neighborhood of a given nonfocal pair
(q_{a},q_{b})

for a choice of one solution in the discrete set. When one varies
q_{a}
or
q_{b}
(or the time interval),
it is understood that one follows the variation of the chosen solution. The resulting HJ action is then a local generating function. To get the whole graph of the hamiltonian flow of
H
one needs the various, possibly infinitely many, HJ actions associated to the different solutions.

Example 2.3
.

A simple example where uniqueness of solutions with given endpoints is not satisfied is the free particle on
S^{1}
, the unit circle.
10
10

10

The nonuniqueness in this example is due to the fact that
S^{1}
is not simply connected, but it is possible to find examples of nonuniqueness on simply connected configuration spaces as well.
The hamiltonian is simply
H(p,q)=\frac{\,p^{2}}{2m}
. The EL equations are
\dot{p}=0
and
\dot{q}=\frac{p}{m}
. The graph of the hamiltonian flow of
H
is then

L\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left\{(p^{a},q_{a},p^{b},q_{b})\in T^{*}S^{1}\times T^{*}S^{1}\ |\ p^{b}=p^{a},\ q_{b}=q_{a}+\frac{p^{a}}{m}(t_{b}-t_{a})\bmod 2\pi\right\}.

On the other hand, if we fix
q_{a}
and
q_{b}
, we have the solutions

\begin{split}p(t)&=\frac{m(q_{b}-q_{a}+2k\pi)}{t_{b}-t_{a}},\\
q(t)&=q_{a}+(q_{b}-q_{a}+2k\pi)\frac{t-t_{a}}{t_{b}-t_{a}}\bmod 2\pi,\end{split}

for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. It follows that, for a given
k
, the HJ action reads

S_{\text{HJ},k}(q_{a},q_{b})=\frac{1}{2}m\frac{(q_{b}-q_{a}+2k\pi)^{2}}{t_{b}-t_{a}},

which is indeed a generating function for
L
in a neighborhood of
(q_{a}^{0},p_{0},q_{b}^{0},p_{0})
with

p_{0}=\frac{m(q_{b}^{0}-q_{a}^{0}+2k\pi)}{t_{b}-t_{a}}
. To generate the whole
L
, one needs all the
S_{\text{HJ},k}
s.

Remark 2.4

(Semiclassical approximation)
.

In the path integral approach to quantum mechanics (say, on the configuration space
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
), the integral kernel
K(q_{a},q_{b})
for the evolution operator is, formally, given by the path integral
[
26
,
28
,
29
]

(4)

K(q_{a},q_{b})=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}q(t_{a})=q_{a}\\
q(t_{b})=q_{b}\end{subarray}}Dp\,Dq\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S[p,q]}.

Assuming for simplicity that there is a unique solution

(p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}})
satisfying

q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{a})=q_{a}
and
q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{b})=q_{b}
, one usually makes the affine change of variables (translation)

(5)

p\mapsto p^{q_{a},q_{b}}+\hat{p},\quad q\mapsto q_{q_{a},q_{b}}+\hat{q},

getting

K(q_{a},q_{b})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b})}\int_{\hat{q}(t_{a})=\hat{q}(t_{b})=0}D\hat{p}\,D\hat{q}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\hat{S}_{(q_{a},q_{b})}[\hat{p},\hat{q}]}

with

\hat{S}_{(q_{a},q_{b})}[\hat{p},\hat{q}]=S[p^{q_{a},q_{b}}+\hat{p},q_{q_{a},q_{b}}+\hat{q}]-S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b}).

Since
(p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}})
is a solution,
\hat{S}_{(q_{a},q_{b})}
starts with a quadratic term in
(\hat{p},\hat{q})
, so one can define the path integral perturbatively. One then sees that the HJ action yields the semiclassical approximation to
K
. The crucial point here is that the Hessian of
S
at a solution is nondegenerate (one also says that the action or the lagrangian is regular). This does not happen for degenerate actions (e.g., in gauge theories). In this case one has to use some technique to gauge fix the action, but one also has to take into account that the symplectic space of initial conditions is defined via some symplectic reduction. A cohomological way to resolve these issues is to use the BV formalism
[
7
]
in the bulk and the BFV formalism
[
6
,
30
]

on the boundary. In
[
19
]
it was shown how to
reconcile the two formalisms, into what was termed the quantum BV-BFV formalism,
and it was in particular observed that, in general, extending boundary values to a solution of the EL equations is incompatible with the formalism (we will review this in Sections
9.2.3
and
9.2.6
).
The above argument to show that the semiclassical contribution to the integral kernel of the evolution operator is the HJ action is therefore no longer valid.
We will see in Theorem
11.4
that the result still holds, yet the HJ action is recovered not by evaluating the classical action on a solution but via resumming trees in the Feynman diagrams expansion.

Remark 2.5

(Changing the endpoint conditions)
.

Fixing
q
at the endpoints yields the EL equations as critical points for
S
. One may however be interested in choosing different endpoint conditions. In general, they have to correspond to a lagrangian submanifold of
T^{*}M
, not necessarily the zero section, but one has to adapt the one-form
p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}
. For simplicity, we now assume
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
(P_{i},Q^{i})
be another set of Darboux coordinates on
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
and assume (see also Example
A.33
for a more general viewpoint) that we have a generating function
f(q,Q)
for the transfomation: namely,

p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}=P_{i}\mathrm{d}Q^{i}+\mathrm{d}f,

i.e.,

p_{i}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial q^{i}},\quad P_{i}=-\frac{\partial f}{\partial Q^{i}}.

Assume we want to fix
Q
at the final endpoint and
q
at the initial endpoint. We then change the action by a boundary term:

S^{f}[p,q]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-f(q(t_{b}),Q(p(t_{b}),q(t_{b})))+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-H(p,q)\mathrm{d}t).

When we take a variation, we now get

\delta S^{f}=\text{\sl EL}+P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}(t_{b})-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}(t_{a}),

with the same
EL
term as before. Therefore, the solutions of the EL equations are the critical points of
S^{f}

with fixed
q
at the initial endpoint and
Q
at the final endpoint. We define the corresponding HJ action as

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}[p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}],

where
(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}})
is the (assumedly unique) solution ot the EL equations with

q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{a})=q_{a}
and
Q(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}))=Q_{b}
.
Exactly as in the case discussed at the very beginning of this section, inserting a solution into the variation of
S^{f}

kills the
EL
term, so we get

P_{i}(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}})(t_{b})=\frac{\partial S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial Q^{i}_{b}},\quad p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}_{i}(t_{a})=-\frac{\partial S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial q^{i}_{a}}.

In other words,
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is a generating function for the graph of the hamiltonian flow of
H
(from
t_{a}
to
t_{b}
) with respect to the new set of coordinates (see again Remark
A.32
).
The dependency on time can be studied repeating the steps of Remark
2.1
verbatim.

Example 2.6
.

Consider the case when
H=0
, i.e.,
S[p,q]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}
. Since the EL equations have constant solutions, we have
S[p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}]=0
and
q(t_{b})=q_{a}
.
Therefore

(6)

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})=-f(q_{a},Q_{b}).

Remark 2.7
.

For simplicity, we only discuss the change of the final endpoint conditions, but of course the same discussion may be repeated for the initial endpoint and for both endpoints at the same time.

Example 2.8
.

A very common case is when one wants to fix
q
at the initial point and
p
at the final point. This fits in the above construction by choosing
Q=p
and
P=-q
and
f(q,Q)=Q_{i}q^{i}
. In this case, we simply have

S^{f}[p,q]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-p_{i}(t_{b})q^{i}(t_{b})+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-H(p,q)\mathrm{d}t),

\delta S^{f}=\text{\sl EL}-q^{i}(t_{b})\delta p_{i}(t_{b})-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}(t_{a}),

and

(7)

q_{q_{a},p^{b}}^{i}(t_{b})=-\frac{\partial S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial p_{i}^{b}},\quad p^{q_{a},p^{b}}_{i}(t_{a})=-\frac{\partial S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial q^{i}_{a}}.

If we apply this to the Example
2.3
of the free particle on the circle, we get a unique solution for a given
(q_{a},p^{b})
and the HJ action

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b})=-p^{b}q_{a}-\frac{(p^{b})^{2}}{2m}(t_{b}-t_{a}).

If we instead consider the case
H=0
of Example
2.6
, we get, as a particular case of (
6
),

(8)

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b})=-p^{b}q_{a}.

Remark 2.9

(Legendre transform)
.

One can pass from
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b})
to
S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b})
, and back, by the Legendre transform:

S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b})=\lambda^{\text{crit}}_{i}q_{b}^{i}+S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\lambda^{\text{crit}}),

where
\lambda^{\text{crit}}
is the critical point (assumed to be unique) of the variable
p^{b}
for the function
f(p^{b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=p^{b}_{i}q_{b}^{i}+S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b})
:

\frac{\partial f}{\partial p^{b}}=0\text{\ at\ }p^{b}=\lambda^{\text{crit}}.

We will return to this in Section
6.2
.

Remark 2.10
.

In terms of the path-integral computation of the evolution operator, see Remark
2.4
,
changing the endpoint conditions, as discussed in Remark
2.5
, corresponds to assigning different Hilbert space representations, corresponding to different choices of polarization, to the endpoints. For example, fixing
p
at the final endpoint, as in Example
2.8
, corresponds to computing the integral kernel
\hat{K}(q_{a},p^{b})
for the position representation at the initial endpoint and the momentum representation at the final one. This is the Fourier transform of
K(q_{a},q_{b})
with respect to the second argument. One can see a classical track of this in the

p_{i}(t_{b})q^{i}(t_{b})
term in
S^{f}
(and in the Legendre transform of the previous remark).

Example 2.11
.

Consider

\hat{K}(q_{a},p^{b})=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}q(t_{a})=q_{a}\\
p(t_{b})=p^{b}\end{subarray}}Dp\,Dq\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S^{f}[p,q]}.

with
S^{f}[p,q]=-p(t_{b})q(t_{b})+S[p,q]
for the simple action
S[p,q]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p\,\mathrm{d}q
. (Nothing changes if we have many
p
and
q
variables: just think of a hidden scalar product
p\mathrm{d}q=p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}
.) As the EL equations imply that the solutions are constant, the change of variables (
5
) is now

p\mapsto p^{b}+\hat{p},\quad q\mapsto q_{a}+\hat{q},

with
\hat{p}(t_{b})=0
and
\hat{q}(t_{a})=0
.
Since
S^{f}[p,q]=-p^{b}(q_{a}+\hat{q}(t_{b}))+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p^{b}+\hat{p})\mathrm{d}\hat{q}
, we have

\hat{K}(q_{a},p^{b})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}q_{a}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{q}(t_{a})=0\\
\hat{p}(t_{b})=0\end{subarray}}D\hat{p}\,D\hat{q}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\left(-p^{b}\hat{q}(t_{b})+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p^{b}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}\right)}.

This is a Gaussian integral with quadratic term
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}
and no
\hat{p}
-sources, so it simply yields a constant (which we normalize to one), and we get

(9)

\hat{K}(q_{a},p^{b})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}q_{a}},

the integral kernel for the Fourier transform, whose exponent is given by the HJ action (
8
).

Example 2.12

(Formal quantum change of polarization)
.

The computation of Example
2.11
may be generalized to the general case discussed in Remark
2.5
. Namely, we have

\hat{K}^{f}(q_{a},Q_{b})=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}q(t_{a})=q_{a}\\
Q(p(t_{b}),q(t_{b}))=Q_{b}\end{subarray}}Dp\,Dq\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S^{f}[p,q]},

with
S^{f}[p,q]=-f(q(t_{b}),Q(p(t_{b}),q(t_{b})))+S[p,q]
. Again we consider
S[p,q]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p\,\mathrm{d}q
.
The change of variables (
5
) now becomes

p\mapsto p^{0}+\hat{p},\quad q\mapsto q_{a}+\hat{q},

with the constant solution
p^{0}
given by

p^{0}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial q}{(q_{a}+\hat{q}(t_{b}),Q_{b})},

and endpoint conditions
\hat{p}(t_{b})=0
and
\hat{q}(t_{a})=0
.
11
11

11

Note that the endpoint fluctuations of
p
and
q
are constrained by the condition
Q(p(t_{b}),q(t_{b}))=Q_{b}
. We have arranged the change of variables so that, while
p^{0}
may fluctuate at the endpoint,
\hat{p}
does not.
In general this change of variables is no longer affine, since
p^{0}
depends on
\hat{q}
, possibly in a nonlinear way. On the other hand, the Jacobian determinant of the transformation is (formally)
1
(the Jacobian is lower triangular
with “
1
”s on the diagonal).
We now have

\hat{K}^{f}(q_{a},Q_{b})=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{q}(t_{a})=0\\
\hat{p}(t_{b})=0\end{subarray}}D\hat{p}\,D\hat{q}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\left(-f(q_{a}+\hat{q}(t_{b}),Q_{b})+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p^{0}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}\right)},

which is again a Gaussian integral with quadratic term
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}
and no
\hat{p}
-sources, Therefore,

(10)

\hat{K}^{f}(q_{a},Q_{b})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}f(q_{a},Q_{b})},

the exponential of the HJ action (
6
).

Digression 2.13

(Unitarity)
.

The result (
10
)
of the previous Example
2.12
is correct insofar as the starting point is the quantization of the action
S^{f}

as a partition “function.” This has to be corrected if we understand the integral kernel as a half-density in the boundary variables, which is more appropriate for composition.
The idea is that the functional measure is the product over points of the measure
\mathrm{d}p\,\mathrm{d}q/(2\pi\hbar)
(for simplicity we discuss this for the target
T^{*}\mathbb{R}
). At the end points we then retain only the square root of the fixed boundary variable, as the other corresponding square root will come from another integral kernel we want to compose with. For example, in passing from the
q
- to the
p
-representation, the integral kernel half-density will be

\mathbb{K}(q_{a},p^{b})=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q_{a}}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}q_{a}}\;\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}p^{b}}{2\pi\hbar}}

instead of (
9
). Note that
\mathbb{K}
is unitary:

\int_{\{p^{b}\}}\mathbb{K}(q,p^{b})\,\overline{\mathbb{K}}(p^{b},q^{\prime})=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q}\;\left(\int_{\{p^{b}\}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}(q^{\prime}-q)}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}p^{b}}{2\pi\hbar}\right)\;\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q}\,\delta(q^{\prime}-q)\,\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}}.

Unitarity is somehow expected, since reversing the orientation of the interval on which the theory is defined produces

\overline{\mathbb{K}}(p^{b},q^{\prime})
, and the composition of the quantum evolution on an interval with the reversed one should be the identity.
In Example
2.12
, we could follow the same reasoning and expect unitarity (indeed, at the classical level, composition of the generating functions
f(q^{\prime},Q_{b})
and
-f(q,Q_{b})
, see Remark
A.37
, yields a generating function for the identity); however, in general, this is not the case at the quantum level as we will see in a moment. In this example as well
we split the fields in terms of the initial
q
and final
p
variables, but then we expressed the final values in terms of
Q_{b}
. The change of variables we perfomed there entails in particular

(11)

p(t_{b})=\frac{\partial f}{\partial q}{(q_{a}+\hat{q}(t_{b}),Q_{b})}

and therefore

\mathrm{d}p(t_{b})=\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q\,\partial Q}{(q_{a}+\hat{q}(t_{b}),Q_{b})}\,\mathrm{d}Q_{b}+\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q^{2}}{(q_{a}+\hat{q}(t_{b}),Q_{b})}\,(\mathrm{d}q_{a}+\mathrm{d}\hat{q}(t_{b})).

The terms in
\mathrm{d}q_{a}
and
\mathrm{d}\hat{q}(t_{b})
can be neglected, since they already appear in the functional measure. Therefore, we only retain the first term, of which we eventually have to take the square root, getting
the integral kernel half-density

\begin{split}\mathbb{K}^{f}(q_{a},Q_{b})=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q_{a}}\;\Bigg(\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{q}(t_{a})=0\\
\hat{p}(t_{b})=0\end{subarray}}D\hat{p}\,D\hat{q}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\left(-f(q_{a}+\hat{q}(t_{b}),Q_{b})+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p^{0}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}\right)}\\
\sqrt{\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q\,\partial Q}{(q_{a}+\hat{q}(t_{b}),Q_{b})}}\Bigg)\;\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}Q_{b}}{2\pi\hbar}},\end{split}

where we have assumed the nonzero
\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q\,\partial Q}
term to be actually positive.
Again, since this is a Gaussian integral with quadratic term
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}
and no
\hat{p}
-sources, we get

(12)

\mathbb{K}^{f}(q_{a},Q_{b})=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q_{a}}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}f(q_{a},Q_{b})}\sqrt{\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q\,\partial Q}{(q_{a},Q_{b})}}\;\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}Q_{b}}{2\pi\hbar}}

instead of (
10
).
In a neighborhood of the diagonal
q=q^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{K}^{f}
is almost unitary: i.e.,

\int_{\{Q_{b}\}}\mathbb{K}^{f}(q,Q_{b})\,\overline{\mathbb{K}}^{f}(Q_{b},q^{\prime})=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q}\,(1+O(\hbar))\delta(q^{\prime}-q)\,\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}},

where the
O(\hbar)
term is a differential operator. This may be seen by the
(
q
and
q^{\prime}
dependent)
change of variable

\lambda(Q_{b})=\frac{f(q^{\prime},Q_{b})-f(q,Q_{b})}{q^{\prime}-q}

in the
\mathrm{d}Q_{b}
integral.
12
12

12

In some cases,
\mathbb{K}^{f}
is exactly unitary. This happens, e.g., when
f
has product form:
f(q,Q)=\alpha(q)\beta(Q)
. (In particular, this happens when we have a linear symplectomorphism, in which case, moreover, the correction term
\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q\,\partial Q}
is constant.) In this case, we have

\int_{\{Q_{b}\}}\mathbb{K}^{f}(q,Q_{b})\,\overline{\mathbb{K}}^{f}(Q_{b},q^{\prime})=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q}\;\left(\int_{\{Q_{b}\}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\beta(Q_{b})(\alpha(q^{\prime})-\alpha(q))}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}(q)\alpha^{\prime}(q^{\prime})}\beta^{\prime}(Q)\frac{\mathrm{d}Q_{b}}{2\pi\hbar}\right)\;\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}},

where we have assumed the nonzero
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
terms to be actually positive. By the change of variable

\mu=\beta(Q_{b})
, we get

\begin{split}\int_{\{Q_{b}\}}\mathbb{K}^{f}(q,Q_{b})\,\overline{\mathbb{K}}^{f}(Q_{b},q^{\prime})&=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q}\;\left(\int_{\{\mu\}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\mu(\alpha(q^{\prime})-\alpha(q))}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}(q)\alpha^{\prime}(q^{\prime})}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{2\pi\hbar}\right)\;\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}}\\
&=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q}\;\delta(\alpha(q^{\prime})-\alpha(q))\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}(q)\alpha^{\prime}(q^{\prime})}\;\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q}\,\delta(q^{\prime}-q)\,\sqrt{\mathrm{d}q^{\prime}}.\end{split}

Note that (
12
) resembles the starting point of the WKB approximation (see, e.g.,

[
44
, Appendix]
,
[
25
, Section 15]
, and references therein). Indeed, one possible way to get corrections to it to make it unitary is to regard, if possible,
f
as the Hamilton–Jacobi action for some hamiltonian system (on the other hand, the corrections are not uniquely determined, since
the choice of this hamiltonian system is not unique).

3.
Systems with one constraint

Our first generalization is an action of the form
13
13

13

If one requires
e\not=0
everywhere,
this action may also be regarded as the first-order version of one-dimensional gravity, described by the coframe
e
, coupled to matter, described by
q
. Its BV-BFV quantization is studied in
[
22
]
.

S[p,q,e]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-eH(p,q)),

where the new field
e
, the Lagrange multiplier, is a one-form on the interval
[t_{a},t_{b}]
.

We will first consider the case when

e
is different from zero everywhere. This restriction will allow us to deal with endpoint conditions on the
q
s.
Next we will consider the general case, which, however, will force us to pick different endpoint conditions.

3.1.
Nondegenerate Lagrange multiplier

We assume
e
to be different from zero everywhere. A variation of the action yields

\delta S=\text{\sl EL}+p_{i}(t_{b})\delta q^{i}(t_{b})-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}(t_{a})

with the EL equations

\mathrm{d}q^{i}=e\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\quad\mathrm{d}p_{i}=-e\frac{\partial H}{\partial q^{i}},\quad H(p,q)=0.

The last equation, the variation with respect to
e
, is imposed at all times. Note that the endpoint symplectic structures are the same as in the nondegenerate case and depend only on
p
and
q
. This in particular means that no endpoint condition on
e
has to be imposed.

To solve the EL equations, we first define

\tau(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t}e.

The first two sets of EL equations now read

\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{i}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}p_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial q^{i}},

so they are the usual Hamilton equations for the hamiltonian
H
in the time variable
\tau\in[0,T]
, with

T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e
. The last EL equation,
H=0
, is automatically satisfied at any point if we impose it at the initial point (or at the final point). We then have that the endpoint values for
(p,q)
corresponding to solutions form the set

L=\{(p^{a},q_{a},p^{b},q_{b})\in T^{*}M\times T^{*}M\ |\ H(p^{b},q_{b})=0\\
\text{ and }\exists T\in\mathbb{R}_{\not=0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(p^{b},q_{b})=\Phi^{H}_{T}(p^{a},q_{a})\},

where
\Phi^{H}_{\tau}
denotes the hamiltonian flow of
H
at time
\tau
. Note that in this case
L
is not the graph
of a map
T^{*}M\to T^{*}M
, but just a subset of
T^{*}M\times T^{*}M
.
We will call it the evolution relation of the system. (See Remark
A.38
for a more general perspective.)

For a fixed
e
, let
(p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}})
denote the (assumedly unique) solution to the first two sets of EL equations (i.e., we do
not
impose the constraint
H=0
) satisfying

q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{a})=q_{a}
and
q_{q_{a},q_{b}}(t_{b})=q_{b}
.
Since we may rewrite the action as
\int_{0}^{T}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-H(p,q)\mathrm{d}\tau)
, we get that the HJ action is

S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b})[e]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S[p^{q_{a},q_{b}},q_{q_{a},q_{b}},e]=S^{H}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b};0,T),

where the last term denotes the HJ action for the system with hamiltonian
H
over the time interval
[0,T]
.

The first remark is that the HJ action depends on
e
only via its integral
T
; we will then simply write

(13)

\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b};T)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b})[e],\quad\text{with }T=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e,

so

\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b};T)=S^{H}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b};0,T).

The second remark, which follows immediately from (
1
) and (
3
), is that the evolution relation

L

is determined by the equations

p^{b}_{i}=\frac{\partial\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial q^{i}_{b}},\quad p^{a}_{i}=-\frac{\partial\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial q^{i}_{a}},\quad\frac{\partial\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial T}=0.

In other words,
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
is a generalized generating function
14
14

14

In the terminology of
[
9
, Section 4.3]
, the triple
(M\times\mathbb{R}_{\not=0},\pi_{1},\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}})
, where
\pi_{1}
is the projection to the first factor, is a Morse family generating
L
.

for the lagrangian submanifold
L
, see Appendix
A
(in particular, Remark
A.11
for the linear case and Remark
A.27
for the general case).

3.2.
The general case

The assumption
e\not=0
everywhere is only needed to
ensure
that the map
t\mapsto\tau
is a diffeomorphism and hence to relate
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
to the HJ function for the system with hamiltonian
H
.
The existence of a HJ action, however, holds also in the general case where we make no assumptions on
e
, provided we choose the endpoint conditions more carefully.

First observe that the first two sets of EL equations, which we will call the evolution equations,

\mathrm{d}q^{i}=e\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\quad\mathrm{d}p_{i}=-e\frac{\partial H}{\partial q^{i}},

can be uniquely solved, for fixed
e
, in terms of initial conditions
p^{a},q_{a}
. In fact, in the regions (closed intervals) where
e=0
, the solution is constant and on the other regions we can make a change of variables
t\mapsto\tau
as above. Moreover, the function
H
is constant when evaluated on a solution to the evolution equations,
which means that it is enough to impose this constraint at the initial (or final) endpoint to have it satisfied at every time.

Let
L
still denote the evolution relation of the system, i.e., the
set of endpoint values for solutions to the EL equations. Note that, for a given
e=\underline{e}\,\mathrm{d}t
, we have to compute the “flow”
\Phi^{\tilde{H}}_{T}
of
the time-dependent hamiltonian
\tilde{H}=\underline{e}H
up to time
T=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e
.
We then have

L=\{(p^{a},q_{a},p^{b},q_{b})\in T^{*}M\times T^{*}M\ |\ H(p^{b},q_{b})=0\\
\text{ and }\exists e\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(p^{b},q_{b})=\Phi^{\tilde{H}}_{T}(p^{a},q_{a})\}.

Remark 3.1
.

We can have a better geometric description if we assume that the differential of
H
is different from zero at every point of the zero locus
C
of
H
. This ensures that
C
is a submanifold of
T^{*}M
and that the hamiltonian vector field
X^{H}
of
H
restricted to
C
has no zeros, so it defines a distribution of lines. Note that
C
is an example of what is called a coisotropic submanifold and
\Span X^{H}|_{C}
is called its characteristic distribution (see Definition
A.19
and Remark
A.25
for more on this).
In this geometric framework, the evolution relation
L\subset T^{*}M\times T^{*}M
consists of pairs of points on
C
that can be connected by a path along the characteristic distribution. (It is therefore lagrangian by Lemma
A.29
.)

Now observe that fixing
q_{a}
and
q_{b}
is problematic, as for
e=0
we have no solution unless
q_{a}=q_{b}
. For this reason we have to choose different endpoint conditions, as in Remark
2.5
; for instance, we can
fix
p
at one endpoint as in Example
2.8
.

We denote by
(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}},e)

a solution
(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}})
to the evolution equations for the given
e
(but we do
not
impose the constraint
H=0
) satisfying

q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{a})=q_{a}
and
Q(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}))=Q_{b}
.
We then set

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})[e]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}[p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}},e]

with

S^{f}[p,q,e]=-f(q(t_{b}),Q(p(t_{b}),q(t_{b})))+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-eH(p,q)).

We then have

(14)

\begin{split}\delta S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}&=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\delta e\,H(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}})\\
&=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\left(\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\delta e\right)H(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}))\\
&=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\delta T\,H(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b})),\end{split}

where, as above, we set
T=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e
. Note that
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is invariant under variations of
e
with vanishing integral, so we may define
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
like in (
13
):

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};T)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})[e],\quad\text{with }T=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e.

Then the above equation reads

\delta\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\delta T\,H(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b})),

and we see that the equations

P_{i}(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}})(t_{b})=\frac{\partial\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial Q^{i}_{b}},\quad p^{a}_{i}=-\frac{\partial\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial q^{i}_{a}},\quad\frac{\partial\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}}{\partial T}=0

define
L
.

The HJ action, obtained inserting a solution of the evolution equations but ignoring the constraint, is a generalized generating function for the evolution relation determined by all EL equations.

Example 3.2

(Linear case)
.

Consider
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and suppose
H
is linear on
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

H(p,q)=p_{i}v^{i}+w_{i}q^{i},

where the
(v^{i},w_{i})
s are given constants. In this case the evolution equations simply read

\mathrm{d}q^{i}=ev^{i},\quad\mathrm{d}p_{i}=-ew_{i}.

They can be easily solved in terms of the initial conditions, so we get the evolution relation

\displaystyle L=

\displaystyle\{(p^{a},q_{a},p^{b},q_{b})\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\times T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |

\displaystyle|\ \exists T\in\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}p^{b}_{i}v^{i}+w_{i}(q^{i}_{a}+Tv^{i})=0,\ p^{a}=p^{b}+Tw,\ q_{b}=q_{a}+Tv\},

where
T
corresponds to
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e
, and we have evaluated the constraint
H=0
at the final endpoint.
As in Example
2.8
we now fix
q_{a}
and
p^{b}
. The evolution equations can then be easily integrated for the given endpoint conditions as

\begin{split}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}^{i}(t)&=q_{a}^{i}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t}ev^{i}=q_{a}^{i}+\tau(t)v^{i},\\
p^{q_{a},p^{b}}_{i}(t)&=p^{b}_{i}+\int_{t}^{t_{b}}ew_{i}=p^{b}_{i}+(T-\tau(t))w_{i},\end{split}

with
\tau(t)=\int_{t_{a}}^{t}e
. For this choice of endpoint conditions we consider

S^{f}[p,q,e]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-p_{i}(t_{b})q^{i}(t_{b})+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-eH(p,q)).

Now observe that

\displaystyle p^{q_{a},p^{b}}_{i}\mathrm{d}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}^{i}(t)-eH(p^{q_{a},p^{b}},q_{q_{a},p^{b}})

\displaystyle=p^{q_{a},p^{b}}_{i}ev^{i}-ep^{q_{a},p^{b}}_{i}v^{i}-ew_{i}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}^{i}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}\tau w_{i}(q_{a}^{i}+\tau v^{i}),

which implies

\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p^{q_{a},p^{b}}_{i}\mathrm{d}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}^{i}(t)-eH(p^{q_{a},p^{b}},q_{q_{a},p^{b}}))=-Tw_{i}q_{a}^{i}-\frac{T^{2}}{2}w_{i}v^{i}.

On the other hand,

p^{q_{a},p^{b}}_{i}(t_{b})q_{q_{a},p^{b}}^{i}(t_{b})=p^{b}_{i}(q_{a}^{i}+Tv^{i}).

In conclusion, we get

(15)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b};T)=-p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}-T(p^{b}_{i}v^{i}+w_{i}q_{a}^{i})-\frac{T^{2}}{2}w_{i}v^{i},

which is readily seen to be a generalized generating function for
L
.

Remark 3.3
.

Sometimes it is possible to solve for
T
the constraints and then obtain a standard (i.e., without parameters) generating function for the evolution relation
L
. For instance, in the linear case of Example
3.2
,
the constraint is

p^{b}_{i}v^{i}+w_{i}q_{a}^{i}+Tw_{i}v^{i}=0,

which can be solved if
w_{i}v^{i}\not=0
. In this case we get

T=-\frac{p^{b}_{i}v^{i}+w_{i}q_{a}^{i}}{w_{i}v^{i}},

which can be inserted back into
\hat{S}^{f}
to get the standard generating function

\tilde{S}^{f}(q_{a},p^{b})=-p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}+\frac{(p^{b}_{i}v^{i}+w_{i}q_{a}^{i})^{2}}{2w_{i}v^{i}}.

In the one-dimensional case,
n=1
, we get

\tilde{S}^{f}(q_{a},p^{b})=-p^{b}q_{a}+\frac{(p^{b}v+wq_{a})^{2}}{2wv}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{v}{w}(p^{b})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{w}{v}(q_{a})^{2},

which correctly generates the evolution relation

L=\{(p^{a},q_{a},p^{b},q_{b})\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}\times T^{*}\mathbb{R}\ |\ p^{a}v+wq_{a}=0\text{ and }p^{b}v+wq_{b}=0\}.

Note that in this very particular example the evolution plays no role and
L
has the product form

L=L^{\prime}\times L^{\prime}
, with
L^{\prime}=\{(p,q)\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}\ |\ pv+wq=0\}
.

Remark 3.4

(Integrable systems I)
.

The case
n=1

can also be regarded as a one-dimensional integrable system with vanishing hamiltonian. In this case, another expression for the HJ action can be derived as follows. Around a point
(p_{0},q_{0})
where
\mathrm{d}H\neq 0
, by the Carathéodory--Lie--Jacobi theorem
15
15

15

The Carathéodory–Lie–Jacobi theorem says that if
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}
are functions on a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
such that
\{H_{i},H_{j}\}=0
and
\mathrm{d}H_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{d}H_{k}
are linearly independent at some
p\in M
, then there exists a Darboux chart

(q_{i},p^{i})
containing
p
with
q_{1}=H_{1},\ldots,q_{k}=H_{k}
. See, for instance,
[
40
, Theorem 13.4.1]
.
we may extend
H
to a Darboux chart
(P,Q)
with
P=H
(
P
is what is called the action variable and
Q
the corresponding angle variable). Let
f(q,Q)
be the generating function satisfying
p\,\mathrm{d}q=P\,\mathrm{d}Q+\mathrm{d}f
. We may then write

S^{f}[p,q,e]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=-f(q(t_{b}),Q(t_{b}))+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p\,\mathrm{d}q-eH)=-f(q(t_{a}),Q(t_{a}))+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(P\,\mathrm{d}Q-eP),

where
P=P(p,q),Q=Q(p,q)
. The evolution equations now simply read
\mathrm{d}P=0,\mathrm{d}Q=e
. Evaluating on a solution of the evolution equations, the action vanishes and we get

(16)

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};T)=-f(q_{a},Q_{b}-T)

for
(q_{a},Q_{b})
in a neighborhood of
(q_{0},Q(p_{0},q_{0}))
. Alternatively, if
g(q,P)
is the generating function satisfying
p\,\mathrm{d}q={-}Q\,\mathrm{d}P+\mathrm{d}g
, we can express the HJ action with final endpoint condition on
P
by

(17)

S^{g}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},P^{b};T)=-TP^{{b}}-g(q_{a},P^{{b}}).

Notice also that (
17
) is the Legendre transform of (
16
).
We will revisit the case of integrable systems in Remarks
4.5
and
5.1
.

4.
Systems with several constraints

From now on we consider an action of the form
16
16

16

Such an action functional is the classical part of an AKSZ theory
[
2
]
on the interval
[t_{a},t_{b}]
; see Section
8
for a review. See also
[
5
]
for the study of such a theory in the Dirac formalism.

(18)

S[p,q,e]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)),

where the
e^{\alpha}
s (the Lagrange multipliers) are one-forms on the interval
[t_{a},t_{b}]
and the
H_{\alpha}
s are given functions (the constraints). (The sum over
\alpha
, like that over
i
, is now understood.)
The EL equations are

\mathrm{d}q^{i}=e^{\alpha}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial p_{i}},\quad\mathrm{d}p_{i}=-e^{\alpha}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial q^{i}},\quad H_{\alpha}(p,q)=0.

Again we will call the first two sets of equations the evolution equations and the last set the constraints.
Evaluating the constraints on a solution of the evolution equations yields

\mathrm{d}H_{\alpha}=\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial p_{i}}\mathrm{d}p_{i}+\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial q^{i}}\mathrm{d}q^{i}=-\{H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\}e^{\beta},

where
\{\ ,\ \}
denotes the Poisson bracket.
17
17

17

We define the Poisson bracket as

\{f,g\}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial q^{i}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial q^{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial p_{i}}
.

If we assume, as we will do from now on, that the constraints are in involution,
18
18

18

Using Dirac’s terminology, one also says that they are first-class constraints.
i.e., that there are structure functions

f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
such that

(19)

\{H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\}=f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}H_{\gamma},

then the previous equations become

(20)

\mathrm{d}H_{\alpha}=-f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}e^{\beta}H_{\gamma}.

This implies that, if the constraints are imposed at the initial (or final) endpoint, they will be satisfied at every time.

Remark 4.1
.

As in Remark
3.1
, it may be convenient to assume that the differentials of the
H_{\alpha}
s
are linearly independent at every point of the common zero locus
C
of the
H_{\alpha}
s. This ensures that
C
is a submanifold of
T^{*}M
and that the hamiltonian vector fields
X^{H_{\alpha}}
restricted to
C
are linearly independent, so they define a regular distribution. It follows that
C
is also an example of what is called a coisotropic submanifold and
\Span\{X^{H}_{\alpha}|_{C}\}
is called its characteristic distribution (see Definition
A.19
and Remark
A.25
for more on this).
19
19

19

The characteristic distribution is also the same as the kernel of the restriction of the symplectic form to
C
.

As a consequence of (
19
), the characteristic distribution is involutive (and hence, by Frobenius’ theorem, integrable).
In this geometric framework, the evolution relation
L\subset T^{*}M\times T^{*}M
consists of pairs of points on
C
that can be connected by a path along the characteristic distribution and is therefore lagrangian in
\overline{T^{*}M}\times T^{*}M
, where the bar denotes that one takes the opposite symplectic form.

Like in the case with a single constraint, fixing
q_{a}
and
q_{b}
is problematic, as for
e^{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha
we have no solution unless
q_{a}=q_{b}
. Therefore, we choose different endpoint conditions, as in Remark
2.5
; for instance, we can
fix
p
at one endpoint as in Example
2.8
.

We will denote by
(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}},e)

a solution
(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}})
to the evolution equations with the given
e
(but we do
not
impose the constraints
H_{\alpha}=0
) satisfying

q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{a})=q_{a}
and
Q(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}))=Q_{b}
.
We then set

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})[e]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}[p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}},e]

with

S^{f}[p,q,e]=-f(q(t_{b}),Q(p(t_{b}),q(t_{b})))+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)).

By taking a variation, we get

\delta S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\delta e^{\alpha}\,H_{\alpha}(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}).

This shows that
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is indeed a generalized generating function for the evolution relation
L
. However, there is a lot of redundancy because the variations with respect to the
e^{\alpha}(t)
s yield the constraints for every
t\in[t_{a},t_{b}]
, whereas we have observed that it is enough to impose them at the initial (or final) endpoint. This is related to the fact that, thank to (
20
) and upon integration by parts,
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is invariant under a variation of
e
of the form

(21)

\delta e^{\alpha}=\mathrm{d}\gamma^{\alpha}+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}e^{\alpha}\gamma^{\beta},

with the
\gamma
s arbitrary functions on
[t_{a},t_{b}]
that vanish at both endpoints.
We call a transformation as in (
21
) a gauge transformation.
We therefore see that
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
only depends on the
e^{\alpha}(t)
s via their gauge classes. Analogously to the case of equation (
13
), we define

(22)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};[e])\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b},e),\quad\text{with }e\in[e].

In summary, we have the following

Theorem 4.2
.

The generalized HJ action
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
, obtained by inserting into the classical action
S^{f}
the solution of the evolution equations, is a generalized generating functions for the evolution relation, which is defined in terms of all the EL equations (evolution and constrains).

In the next subsections we will discuss more explicitly the gauge classes and how the HJ action depends on them.
We will proceed in increasing order of difficulty. In Section
4.1
, we will consider the strictly involutive case where all the structure functions
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
vanish. This case, which is a straightforward generalization of the single-constraint case,
comprises the case where the constraints
H_{\alpha}
are linear, see Example
4.3
, and will be important for abelian Chern–Simons theory.
Next, in Section
4.2
, we will consider the case when the structure functions are actually constant, so that there is an underlying Lie algebra structure (the
H_{\alpha}
s are in this case the components of an equivariant momentum map). This case will be important for nonabelian Chern–Simons theory. Finally, for completeness, we will treat the general case in Section
4.3
: this case requires knowledge of Lie algebroids and Lie groupoids, see
[
42
]
for a general introduction, but will not be used in the rest of the paper, so it may be safely skipped.

4.1.
The strictly involutive case

Suppose that all the structure functions
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
vanish; i.e.,
\{H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\}=0
for all
\alpha
and
\beta
. This implies that, evaluating on a solution to the evolution equations, we have

\mathrm{d}H_{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha
.
If we define

\tau^{\alpha}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t}e^{\alpha},

we then have
e^{\alpha}=\mathrm{d}\tau^{\alpha}
and
\tau^{\alpha}(t_{a})=0
. Observe that the computation
in (
14
) can be repeated verbatim yielding

\delta S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\delta T^{\alpha}\,H_{\alpha}(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b})),

with
T^{\alpha}=\tau^{\alpha}(t_{b})=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{\alpha}
. Again, this first implies that
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is invariant under variations of the
e^{\alpha}
s with vanishing integral, so we may define
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
as in (
22
) as

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};T)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})[e],\quad\text{with }T^{\alpha}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{\alpha}.

Second, the above computation shows that
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is a generalized generating function for
L
.

Example 4.3

(Linear case)
.

Assume

H_{\alpha}=p_{i}v^{i}_{\alpha}+w_{i\alpha}q^{i}=pv_{\alpha}+w_{\alpha}q,

where the
(v^{i}_{\alpha},w_{i\alpha})
s are given constants and we use the product of row and column vectors to make the notation a bit lighter.
The Poisson bracket of any two constraints is a constant function. The involutivity conditions (
19
) then imply that all these constant functions vanish, so we actually have

\{H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\}=0.

This is equivalent to the conditions

w_{\alpha}v_{\beta}=w_{\beta}v_{\alpha}

for all
\alpha
and
\beta
.
For future convenience we define the (symmetric!) matrix

(23)

A_{\alpha\beta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=w_{\alpha}v_{\beta}=w_{\beta}v_{\alpha}.

We now want to compute
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
explicitly as a function of
q_{a}
and
p^{b}
. First observe that the evolution
equations read

\mathrm{d}q=e^{\alpha}v_{\alpha},\quad\mathrm{d}p=-e^{\alpha}w_{\alpha},

and can be easily integrated for the given endpoint conditions:

\begin{split}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}(t)&=q_{a}+\tau^{\alpha}(t)v_{\alpha},\\
p^{q_{a},p^{b}}(t)&=p^{b}+(T^{\alpha}-\tau^{\alpha}(t))w_{\alpha}.\end{split}

Moreover, we have

\begin{split}p^{q_{a},p^{b}}\mathrm{d}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}(t)-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p^{q_{a},p^{b}},q_{q_{a},p^{b}})&=e^{\alpha}p^{q_{a},p^{b}}v_{\alpha}-e^{\alpha}p^{q_{a},p^{b}}v_{\alpha}-e^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}\\
&=-\mathrm{d}\tau^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}(q_{a}+\tau^{\beta}v_{\beta})=-\mathrm{d}\tau^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}q_{a}-\mathrm{d}\tau^{\alpha}\tau^{\beta}A_{\alpha\beta}\\
&=-\mathrm{d}\tau^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}q_{a}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}(\tau^{\alpha}\tau^{\beta})A_{\alpha\beta}\end{split}

with
A_{\alpha\beta}
the symmetric matrix defined in (
23
). This implies

\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p^{q_{a},p^{b}}\mathrm{d}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}(t)-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p^{q_{a},p^{b}},q_{q_{a},p^{b}}))=-T^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}q_{a}-\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}T^{\beta}A_{\alpha\beta}.

On the other hand,

p^{q_{a},p^{b}}(t_{b})q_{q_{a},p^{b}}(t_{b})=p^{b}(q_{a}+T^{\alpha}v_{\alpha}).

In conclusion, we get

(24)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b},T)=-p^{b}q_{a}-T^{\alpha}(p^{b}v_{\alpha}+w_{\alpha}q_{a})-\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}T^{\beta}A_{\alpha\beta},

which generalizes (
15
) to the the case of several linear constraints.

Remark 4.4

(General change of endpoint conditions)
.

The above example is simple enough for the answer to be computable for a general change of endpoint conditions as in Remark
2.5
. Let
f(q,Q)
be a generating function for the change and fix
q_{a}
and
Q_{b}
. By inspection in the previous computation, we see that to compute
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
we actually only have to solve
q(t)
for the given initial condition
q_{a}
(this is independent of
Q_{b}
), which has already been done above. We then get in general

(25)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};T)=-f(q_{a}+T^{\alpha}v_{\alpha},Q_{b})-T^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}q_{a}-\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}T^{\beta}A_{\alpha\beta}.

Note that this may also be obtained as the composition of the generating functions (
24
)
and (
6
), following Remark
A.36
. Namely, let us write

S_{1}(q_{a},p^{b};T)=-p^{b}q_{a}-T^{\alpha}(p^{b}v_{\alpha}+w_{\alpha}q_{a})-\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}T^{\beta}A_{\alpha\beta},

and

S_{2}(q_{a},Q_{b})=-f(q_{a},Q_{b}).

Let

S_{3}(q_{a},p^{b},\bar{q}_{a},Q_{b};T)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S_{1}(q_{a},p^{b},T)+S_{2}(\bar{q}_{a},Q_{b})+p^{b}\bar{q}_{a}.

The composition of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
is the evaluation of
S_{3}
at its critical point in
(p^{b},\bar{q}_{a})
:

\bar{q}_{a}+\frac{\partial S_{1}}{\partial p^{b}}=0,\quad p^{b}+\frac{\partial S_{2}}{\partial\bar{q}_{a}}=0.

One easily sees that the result is (
25
).

Remark 4.5

(Integrable systems II)
.

As a generalization of Remark
3.4
, we may consider the case where we have exactly
n
constraints
H_{1},\ldots,H_{n}
in strict involution - an
n
-dimensional integrable system with vanishing hamiltonians. Around a point
(p_{0},q_{0})
where
\mathrm{d}H_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{d}H_{n}
are linearly independent, again by the Carathéodory–Jacobi–Lie theorem, see footnote
15
,
we may find a Darboux chart
(P,Q)
with
P_{i}=H_{i}
(the action-angle coordinates). By the same computation as in Remark
3.4
, in this chart the HJ action is expressed as

(26)

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};T)=-f(q_{a},Q_{b}-T)

and

(27)

S^{g}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},P^{b};T)=-T^{i}P_{i}^{b}-{g}(q_{a},P^{b}).

4.2.
The Lie algebra case

We now suppose that all the
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
s are constant (but not necessarily zero). They can be viewed as structure constants of some Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. In turn the
e^{\alpha}
s may be viewed as the components of a
\mathfrak{g}
valued one-form
e
on
[t_{a},t_{b}]
or, equivalently, as a connection one-form. We may also view the
H_{\alpha}
s as the components of a map
H\colon T^{*}M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, where
T^{*}M
is the target symplectic manifold.
The involutivity conditions (
19
) say that
H
is an equivariant momentum map, whereas
(
20
) may be read as the condition
\mathrm{d}_{e}H=0
that
the composition of
H
with a solution to the evolution equations is covariantly constant. We rewrite the action as

S[p,q,e]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-\langle H(p,q),e\rangle),

where
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
denotes the pairing of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
with
\mathfrak{g}
.
Next we define

(28)

h(t)=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t}e}\in G,

where
P
denotes the path ordered product and
G
is the simply connected Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
Note that
h(t_{a})=1
and
e=h^{-1}\mathrm{d}h
.
20
20

20

We use for simplicity the matrix notation. If
G
is not a matrix Lie group, the correct formula is

e(t)=(\mathrm{d}_{1}l_{h})^{-1}\mathrm{d}_{t}h\colon T_{t}I\to\mathfrak{g},

where
1
denotes the unit of
G
and
l_{h}\colon G\to G
denotes the left translation
g\mapsto hg
.

We write

g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=h(t_{b})=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e}.

Finally, we define
\tilde{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Ad_{h}^{*}H
. Observe that, when evaluated on a solution to the evolution equations,
\tilde{H}
is constant. We now repeat the computation as in (
14
):

\begin{split}\delta S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}&=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\langle H(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}),\delta e\rangle\\
&=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\langle\tilde{H}(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}},q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}),\Ad_{h}\delta e\rangle\\
&=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\left\langle\tilde{H}(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b})),\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\mathrm{d}(\delta hh^{-1})\right\rangle\\
&=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\left\langle\tilde{H}(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b})),\delta gg^{-1}\right\rangle\\
&=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\left\langle H(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b})),g^{-1}\delta g\right\rangle.\end{split}

This explicitly shows that
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is invariant under variations of
e
that do not change
g
. As in (
22
), we define

(29)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};g)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})[e],\quad\text{with }g=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e},

and it immediately follows that
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is a generalized generating function for the evolution relation
L
.

Example 4.6

(Biaffine case)
.

Consider a target
T^{*}V=V^{*}\oplus V\ni(p,q)
and constraints that are affine both on
V
and on
V^{*}
; namely,

H_{\alpha}(p,q)=-p_{i}(\rho_{\alpha})^{i}_{j}q^{j}+p_{i}v^{i}_{\alpha}+w_{i\alpha}q^{i}=-p\rho_{\alpha}q+pv_{\alpha}+w_{\alpha}q,

where, for each
\alpha
,
\rho_{\alpha}
is a given endomorphism of
V
,
v_{\alpha}
is a given vector in
V
and
w_{\alpha}
is a given vector in
V^{*}
(we denote the pairing between
V^{*}
and
V
simply by juxtaposition).
The involutivity conditions (
19
) are equivalent to the relations

(30)

\begin{split}[\rho_{\alpha},\rho_{\beta}]&=f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\rho_{\gamma},\\
\rho_{\alpha}v_{\beta}-\rho_{\beta}v_{\alpha}&=f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}v_{\gamma},\\
w_{\alpha}\rho_{\beta}-w_{\beta}\rho_{\alpha}&=f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}w_{\gamma},\\
w_{\beta}v_{\alpha}-w_{\alpha}v_{\beta}&=0.\end{split}

The first relation says that
\rho
is a representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
on
V
, the second and the third that
v\colon\mathfrak{g}\to V
and
w\colon\mathfrak{g}\to V^{*}
are Lie algebra
1
-cocycles,
and the last is what we already saw in Example
4.3
. The
evolution
equations read

\mathrm{d}_{e}q=e^{\alpha}v_{\alpha}=v(e),\quad\mathrm{d}_{e}p=-e^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}=-w(e),

where we introduced the covariant derivatives

\mathrm{d}_{e}q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathrm{d}q+e^{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}q,\quad\mathrm{d}_{e}p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathrm{d}p-e^{\alpha}p\rho_{\alpha}.

Let
R\colon G\to\Aut(V)
denote the representation that integrates
\rho
to the simply connected Lie group
G
.
If we introduce
\tilde{q}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=R_{h(t)}q(t)
with
h
defined in (
28
), then the first evolution relation becomes

\mathrm{d}\tilde{q}=R_{h}v(e),

which has solution, for the the inital condition
\tilde{q}(t_{a})=q(t_{a})=q_{a}
, given by
\tilde{q}(t)=q_{a}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t}R_{h}v(e)
. Therefore,

q_{q_{a},p^{b}}(t)=R_{h(t)}^{-1}\left(q_{a}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t}R_{h}v(e)\right).

Similarly, we get

p^{q_{a},p^{b}}(t)=\bar{R}_{h(t)}^{-1}\left(p^{b}+\int_{t}^{t_{b}}\bar{R}_{h}w(e)\right),

where
\bar{R}_{h}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(R_{h}^{-1})^{*}
is the dual representation on
V^{*}
. For later convenience we introduce the
1
-forms on
G
(valued in
V
and
V^{*}
, respectively)

\alpha(h)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=R_{h}v(e),\quad\beta(h)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bar{R}_{h}w(e).

As a consequence of the second and third relations of (
30
) (and of the fact that the Maurer–Cartan
1
-form
e
is flat), we get that
\alpha
and
\beta
are closed. This implies that
\int_{\gamma}\alpha
and
\int_{\gamma}\beta
are invariant under homotopies of the path
\gamma\colon[t_{a},t_{b}]\to G
with fixed endpoints.
In particular, if
\gamma
is a path joining the identity in
G
to some element
g
, then we define

\Phi(g)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\gamma}\alpha,\quad\Psi(g)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\gamma}\beta.

Note that, since
G
is simply connected, we get well-defined maps
\Phi\colon G\to V
and
\Psi\colon G\to V^{*}
.
21
21

21

One can easily show that
\Phi(g_{1}g_{2})=\Phi(g_{1})+R_{g_{1}}\Phi(g_{2})
and
\Psi(g_{1}g_{2})=\Psi(g_{1})+\bar{R}_{g_{1}}\Psi(g_{2})
; i.e.,
\Phi
and
\Psi
are
1
-cocycles on
G
with values in
V
and
V^{*}
, respectively.
Our formulae give an explicit integration of the Lie algebra
1
-cocycles to the corresponding Lie group
1
-cocycles, which is guaranteed by general arguments, see
[
41
, Lemma 2.13]
.

We now want to compute the HJ action. First observe that

\begin{split}p^{q_{a},p^{b}}\mathrm{d}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p^{q_{a},p^{b}},q_{q_{a},p^{b}})&=p^{q_{a},p^{b}}\mathrm{d}_{e}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}-p^{q_{a},p^{b}}v(e)-w(e)q_{q_{a},p^{b}}\\
&=-w(e)q_{q_{a},p^{b}}=-w(e)R_{h}^{-1}\left(q_{a}+\int_{t_{a}}^{\bullet}R_{h}v(e)\right),\end{split}

so

\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p^{q_{a},p^{b}}\mathrm{d}q_{q_{a},p^{b}}-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p^{q_{a},p^{b}},q_{q_{a},p^{b}}))=-\Psi(g)q_{a}-\text{WZW}(g)

with
g=h(t_{b})
. We introduced the following notation (which will become the WZW term in the case of Chern–Simons theory):

(31)

\text{WZW}(g)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}\beta_{2}\,\alpha_{1}

with
\alpha_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{i}^{*}\alpha
,
\beta_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{i}^{*}\beta
and

\pi_{i}\colon[t_{a},t_{b}]^{2}\to G
,

\pi_{i}(t_{1},t_{2})=\gamma(t_{i})
, where
\gamma
is a(ny) path joining the identity to
g
(e.g., the path
h(t)
). The fact that
\text{WZW}(g)

is invariant under homotopies of
\gamma
with fixed endpoints follows from the fact that
\alpha
and
\beta
are closed and from the fourth relation in (
30
). Finally, we have

p^{q_{a},p^{b}}(t_{b})q_{q_{a},p^{b}}(t_{b})=p^{b}R_{g}^{-1}\left(q_{a}+\Phi(g)\right),

so

(32)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b};g)=-p^{b}R_{g}^{-1}q_{a}-p^{b}R_{g}^{-1}\Phi(g)-\Psi(g)q_{a}-\text{WZW}(g),

which generalizes (
24
) to the case of biaffine constraints.

Remark 4.7

(Exponential map)
.

We now want to rewrite the WZW term (
31
) of the last example to facilitate the comparison with the case of Chern–Simons theory under the assumption that the exponential map
\mathfrak{g}\to G
is surjective. To simplify the notation, we also assume
t_{a}=0
and
t_{b}=1
. In this case, given
g=\mathrm{e}^{\xi}
, we may choose

h(t)=\mathrm{e}^{t\xi}
. It follows that
e=\xi\mathrm{d}t
. We introduce

(33)

\begin{split}W_{2}(g)&\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Psi(g)\Phi(g)=\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}\beta_{2}\,\alpha_{1}+\int_{t_{2}<t_{1}}\beta_{2}\,\alpha_{1},\\
W_{3}(g)&\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{0}^{1}\Psi(h)\rho_{e}\Phi(h)=\int_{t_{1}<t_{2},t_{3}<t_{2}}\beta_{3}\rho_{e_{2}}\alpha_{1}.\end{split}

We have

\begin{split}W_{3}(g)&=\int_{t_{1}<t_{2},t_{3}<t_{2}}w(e_{3})R_{h_{3}}^{-1}\rho_{e_{2}}\alpha_{1}=\int_{t_{1}<t_{2},t_{3}<t_{2}}w(\xi)R_{\mathrm{e}^{t_{3}\xi}}^{-1}\rho(\xi)\mathrm{d}t_{3}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\alpha_{1}\\
&=\int_{t_{1}<t_{2},t_{3}<t_{2}}w(\xi)\rho(\xi)R_{\mathrm{e}^{t_{3}\xi}}^{-1}\mathrm{d}t_{3}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\alpha_{1}=-\int_{t_{1}<t_{2},t_{3}<t_{2}}w(\xi)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t_{3}}R_{\mathrm{e}^{t_{3}\xi}}^{-1}\mathrm{d}t_{3}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\alpha_{1}\\
&=-\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}w(\xi)R_{\mathrm{e}^{t_{2}\xi}}^{-1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\alpha_{1}+\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}w(\xi)\mathrm{d}t_{2}\alpha_{1}\\
&=-\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}\beta_{2}\,\alpha_{1}+\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}w(\xi)\mathrm{d}t_{2}\alpha_{1}.\end{split}

Therefore,

W_{2}(g)-W_{3}(g)=2\text{WZW}(g)+\left(\int_{t_{2}<t_{1}}\beta_{2}\,\alpha_{1}-\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}w(\xi)\mathrm{d}t_{2}\alpha_{1}\right).

We now want to show that the term in brackets vanishes. In fact,

\int_{t_{2}<t_{1}}\beta_{2}\,\alpha_{1}=\int_{t_{2}<t_{1}}w(\xi)R_{\mathrm{e}^{(t_{1}-t_{2})\xi}}v(\xi)\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\underset{r=t_{1}}{\overset{s=t_{1}-t_{2}}{=}}\int_{s<r}w(\xi)R_{\mathrm{e}^{s\xi}}v(\xi)\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

On the other hand,

\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}w(\xi)\mathrm{d}t_{2}\alpha_{1}=\int_{t_{1}<t_{2}}w(\xi)R_{\mathrm{e}^{t_{1}\xi}}v(\xi)\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\underset{r=t_{2}}{\overset{s=t_{1}}{=}}\int_{s<r}w(\xi)R_{\mathrm{e}^{s\xi}}v(\xi)\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

In conclusion,

(34)

\text{WZW}(g)=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}(g)-\frac{1}{2}W_{3}(g).
