Example 4.8

(Adjoint representation)
.

We now specialize the above example and remark to the case when
(V,\rho)=(\mathfrak{g},\mathrm{ad})
is the adjoint representation.
Note that the second relation of (
30
) now says that
v
is a derivation of
\mathfrak{g}
. As such it may be extended to the universal enveloping algebra as a derivation
22
22

22

This is well-defined because, if
v
is a Lie algebra derivation and is extended to the tensor algebra as a derivation, then it preserves the ideal generated by elements of the form
{\xi\otimes\eta-\eta\otimes\xi-[\xi,\eta]}
.
and then to (the image of the exponential map in) the group. We have

v(\mathrm{e}^{\xi})=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{\xi t}v(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\xi(1-t)}\mathrm{d}t.

In this case, for
g=\mathrm{e}^{\xi}
, we get

\Phi(g)=v(g)g^{-1},

where again for notational simplicity we have assumed to have a matrix Lie group. We further assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is a quadratic Lie algebra, i.e., it is equipped with an invariant nondegenerate pairing
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
, which we can use to identify
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
with
\mathfrak{g}
. We will then denote by
\bar{q}
the image of
p
under this isomorphism (and we will then take the target to be
\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
with symplectic structure induced from the pairing) and by
\bar{v}
the composition of
w
with the isomorphism. We can then rewrite (
32
) as

(35)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b};g)=-\langle\bar{q}^{b},{g}^{-1}q_{a}g\rangle-\langle\bar{q}^{b},{g}^{-1}v(g)\rangle-\langle\bar{v}(g)g^{-1},q_{a}\rangle-\text{WZW}(g)

and, using (
33
) and (
34
), the WZW term as

(36)

\text{WZW}(g)=\frac{1}{2}\langle\bar{v}(g)g^{-1},v(g)g^{-1}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\langle\bar{v}(h)h^{-1},[v(h)h^{-1},\mathrm{d}hh^{-1}]\rangle.

Remark 4.9

(A trivial extension)
.

The last example may be generalized to the case when
V=\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z
, with
Z
a trivial representation of
\mathfrak{g}
. We keep denoting the representation by
\mathrm{ad}
:
\mathrm{ad}_{x}(y\otimes z)=[x,y]\otimes z
.
Now

v\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z
is a derivation in the sense that
v([x,y])=\mathrm{ad}_{x}v(y)-\mathrm{ad}_{y}v(x)
for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
.
We identify
V^{*}
with
\mathfrak{g}\otimes Z^{*}
using the nondegenerate pairing on
\mathfrak{g}
and denote by
\bar{q}
and
\bar{v}
the composition of
p
and
w
with this isomorphism. Note that
\bar{v}
is also a derivation in the above sense.
The HJ action is again given by (
35
) and (
36
) with the obvious understanding of notations.

Remark 4.10

(General solution via the exponential map)
.

In the general case, but under the assumption that the exponential map
\mathfrak{g}\to G
is surjective,
the HJ action can also be characterized as follows. As in (
29
), we set
g=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e}
.
We then take
T\in\mathfrak{g}
with
\mathrm{e}^{T}=g
and define

h(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t-t_{a}}{t_{b}-t_{a}}T}P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t}e}.

We have
e=h^{-1}\mathrm{d}h+h^{-1}\frac{T\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}h
, which shows that we can reduce
e
to
e_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{T\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}
by a gauge transformation. Since (
29
) does not depend on which representative of
[e]
we take, we may pick
e_{0}
. With the change of variable
s=\frac{t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}
,
the action then becomes

S[p,q,e_{0}]=\int_{0}^{1}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-\tilde{H}(p,q)\mathrm{d}s),\qquad\tilde{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}={\langle H,T\rangle},

As a result,

(37)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};g)=S_{\text{HJ}}^{f,\tilde{H}}(q_{a},Q_{b};0,1),

where
S_{\text{HJ}}^{f,\tilde{H}}
denotes the HJ action for a system with hamiltonian
\tilde{H}
. This formula is very general, but, unlike the previous ones for the biaffine case, not explicit.

4.3.
The general case

We briefly discuss the general case, where we make no simplifying assumptions on the structure functions
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
, only for completeness, as this will not be needed for the rest of the paper. As a consequence, this section, which requires knowledge of Lie algebroids and Lie groupoids, may be safely skipped by the not interested reader.

We will assume that the differentials of the
H_{\alpha}
s on
their common zero locus
C
are linearly independent. This in particular implies that
C
is a submanifold of
N\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=T^{*}M
and, as observed in Remark
4.1
, that the hamiltonian vector fields
X_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=X^{H_{\alpha}}
restricted to
C
are linearly independent, so they define a regular distribution: the characteristic distribution of the coisotropic submanifold
C
. (See Definition
A.19
and Remark
A.25
.)

The first remark is that the structure functions
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
and the hamiltonian vector fields
X_{\alpha}
of the constraints
H_{\alpha}
define a Lie algebroid
A
over
C
. As a vector bundle,
A
is the trivial product
C\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
k
is the number of constraints. The anchor map
\rho\colon A\to TC
is given by the vector fields
X_{\alpha}
:

\rho(x,u_{\alpha})=X_{\alpha}(x),

where
(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We can expand a section
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)
as

\sigma^{\alpha}u_{\alpha}
, where now the
u_{\alpha}
s are regarded as constant sections of
A
. We then
have
\rho(\sigma)=\sigma^{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
. The Lie bracket is defined as

[\sigma,\tau]^{\gamma}=f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\sigma^{\alpha}\tau^{\beta}+\sigma^{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(\tau^{\gamma})-\tau^{\alpha}X_{\alpha}(\sigma^{\gamma}).

Remark 4.11
.

The Lie algebroid
A
is isomorphic to the characteristic distribution of
C
viewed as a Lie subalgebroid of
TC
. Note that in general the conormal bundle
N^{*}C
of a coisotropic submanifold of a symplectic manifold
N
has a canonical Lie algebroid structure, isomorphic to the characteristic distribution, which makes it into a Lie subalgebroid of
T^{*}N
. In our particular case, since
C
is defined by constraints, its normal bundle, and therefore its conormal bundle, can be trivialized, and this is what have done above.

The Lie algebroid
A\to C
can be extended to the vector bundle
\hat{A}\to N=T^{*}M
with
\hat{A}=N\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
. We can then view the
H_{\alpha}
s as a section
H
of
\hat{A}^{*}
. It is also possible to extend the anchor map and the bracket as defined above, but in general
\hat{A}
will not be a Lie algebroid
(it is, though, when the structure functions are constant).

Next we denote the map
(p,q)\colon I\to T^{*}M
, with
I
the interval
[t_{a},t_{b}]
, as
x\colon I\to N
. The field
e
may be regarded as a section of
T^{*}I\otimes x^{*}\hat{A}
.
The constraints
H_{\alpha}
s composed with
x
define a section of
x^{*}\hat{A}^{*}
, which we keep denoting as
H
. As a consequence,
we may rewrite the action as

S[p,q,e]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-\langle H(p,q),e\rangle),

where
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
denotes the pairing of
\hat{A}^{*}
with
\hat{A}
.
A solution
(x,e)
of the evolution equations is the same as an anchor-preserving morphism
TI\to\hat{A}
.

The generalized HJ action
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is defined as in (
13
) and, by Theorem
4.2
, is a generalized generating function for the evolution relation.

It is possible to give a more transparent expression for
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};[e])
, analogous to the one we have in the Lie algebra case, when
the
x
component of
the assumedly
unique solution of the evolution equations specified by
q_{a}
,
Q_{b}
, and
e
has image in
C
(note that, if
x(t)
is in
C
for some
t
, then
x(t)
is in
C
for every
t
).
In fact, in this case we may view
(x,e)
as
an anchor-preserving morphism
x\colon TI\to A
, which is the same as a Lie algebroid morphism
TI\to A
.
Denote by
\mathcal{G}
the source simply connected Lie groupoid of
A
(i.e., up to isomorphism, the monodromy groupoid of the characteristic distribution).
The Lie algebroid morphism
(x,e)
, with initial condition
x(t_{a})=x_{a}\in C
, may be then uniquely integrated to a Lie groupoid morphism

E\colon I\times I\to\mathcal{G}
. If we denote by
\alpha
and
\beta
the source and target maps of
\mathcal{G}
, then we have, in particular,

x(s)=\alpha(E(s,t))\ \forall t\in I,\qquad x(t)=\beta(E(s,t))\ \forall s\in I.

Define
h(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E(t_{a},t)
. We then have

h(t_{a})=1_{x_{a}},\quad\alpha(h(t))=x_{a}\ \forall t\in I,

where
1_{x}
denotes the unit of
\mathcal{G}
at
x\in N
.
In particular,
h
is a map
I\to\alpha^{-1}(x_{a})
.

We may now recover
e
from
h
, generalizing footnote
20
,
as follows. First consider the linear map

\mathrm{d}_{t}h\colon T_{t}I\to T_{h(t)}\alpha^{-1}(x_{a})
. Next consider the left translation
l_{h}\colon\alpha^{-1}(\beta(h))\to\alpha^{-1}({\alpha(h)})
,
l_{h}g=hg
. Since
h1_{\beta(h)}=h
, we get the linear map

\mathrm{d}_{1_{\beta(h)}}l_{h}\colon A_{\beta(h)}\to T_{h}\alpha^{-1}(\alpha(h)).

Using
\beta(h(t))=x(t)
and
\alpha(h(t))=x_{a}
, we get the linear map

e(t)=(\mathrm{d}_{1_{x(t)}}l_{h})^{-1}\mathrm{d}_{t}h\colon T_{t}I\to A_{x(t)},

which may be shown to be the value at
t
of our section
e
of
T^{*}I\otimes x^{*}\hat{A}
.

The rest of the computation is now like in the Lie algebra case, and we have

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};g)=S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})[e]\quad\text{with }g=h(t_{b})\in\alpha^{-1}(x(t_{a}))\cap\beta^{-1}(x(t_{b})).

Finally, we can take variations with respect to
q_{a}
,
Q_{b}
, and
g
, getting

\delta\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};g)=P_{i}(t_{b})\delta Q^{i}_{b}-p_{i}(t_{a})\delta q^{i}_{a}-\left\langle H(x(t_{b})),(\mathrm{d}_{{1_{x(t_{b})}}}l_{g})^{-1}\delta g\right\rangle,

with
x(t_{b})=(p^{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}),q_{q_{a},Q_{b}}(t_{b}))
. This is consistent with
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
being a generalized generating function for the evolution relation
L
(it is in general a weaker statement, as we are now only allowed to take variations in the class of variables for which the
x
component of a solution lies in
C
).

Remark 4.12
.

The case of constant structure functions, hence corresponding to a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, fits into the general case as the action Lie algebroid
T^{*}M\times\mathfrak{g}

with the action Lie groupoid
T^{*}M\times G
as its integration. In this case, the Lie algebroid and the Lie groupoid are actually defined over the whole of
T^{*}M
and not only over
C
, so the formula for
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};g)
has no restrictions.

5.
Systems with nontrivial evolution and constraints

For completeness, we briefly discuss here the case of systems which, in addition to constraints
H_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1\dots,k
, also have a nontrivial evolution with hamiltonian
H
. Namely, we consider an action of the form

S[p,q,e]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-\mathrm{d}t\,H-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)).

In addition to assuming involutivity of the constraints, as in (
19
), we also assume them to be constants of motion for
H
: namely,
\{H,H_{\alpha}\}=0
for all
\alpha
.

This system can actually be treated as above simply adding
H
to the set of the, now
k+1
, constraints, say, as
H_{0}
. The only difference is that the one-form
e^{0}
instead of being free will be set to be
\mathrm{d}t
.
This is a possible choice, as the Lie group
\tilde{G}
(in the case of structure constants
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
; otherwise, more generally,
the Lie groupoid
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
)
corresponding to the rank-
(k+1)
Lie algebra (Lie algebroid) factorizes as
\mathbb{R}\times G
(
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{G}
), so we can fix
t_{b}-t_{a}
in the first factor.

The HJ action, which serves as a generalized generating function, is then, in the Lie algebra case,

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};g)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})[\mathrm{d}t,e],\quad\text{with }g=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e}.

In the Lie algebroid case, we get, more generally,

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b};g)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b})[\mathrm{d}t,e],

with
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\pi_{\mathcal{G}}(\tilde{h}(t_{b}))\in\alpha^{-1}(x(t_{a}))\cap\beta^{-1}(x(t_{b}))
and
\pi_{\mathcal{G}}
the projection
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathcal{G}
.

5.1.
Classical mechanics

A more conceptual way is to add two new canonically conjugated variables
(E,\mathbb{t})
and consider instead the action

S[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0}]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-E\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}-e^{0}(H-E)-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)).

Note that the constraints
H_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=0\dots,k
, with
H_{0}=H-E
, are still first class. This is then a system as the ones we have studied in the previous sections. One of the evolution equations is now
\mathrm{d}E=0
, which says that eventually
E
will be a constant (the energy). Another important evolution equation is
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}=e^{0}
, which can be used to substitute
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}
to
e^{0}
, so that we may regard
\mathbb{t}
as time. Note that, if we set endpoint conditions
\mathbb{t}(t_{a})=t_{a}
and
\mathbb{t}(t_{b})=t_{b}
, we actually get the time evolution from
t_{a}
to
t_{b}
.

In the following, we focus on the case
H_{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha>0
, i.e.,

S[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0}]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}E\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}-e^{0}(H-E)).

The evolution equations are

\mathrm{d}q^{i}=e^{0}\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{i}},\quad\mathrm{d}p_{i}=-e^{0}\frac{\partial H}{\partial q^{i}},\quad\mathrm{d}E=0,\quad\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}=e^{0},

and the constraint is
H(p,q)=E
. The evolution relation is then

(38)

L=\{(p^{a},E^{a},q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},p^{b},E^{b},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})\ |\ (p,q)_{b}=\phi^{H}_{\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a}}((p,q)_{a}),\ E^{b}=E^{a}=H(p^{a},q_{a})\}

where
\phi^{H}_{T}
denotes the hamiltonian flow of
H
for time
T
.

We now consider endpoint conditions
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},E^{b})
. We thus evaluate the modified action

S^{f}[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=E(t_{b})\mathbb{t}(t_{b})+S[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0}]

on the solution, with components
(\tilde{p},\tilde{q},\tilde{\mathbb{t}})
,
of the evolution equations for fixed
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},E^{b})
and fixed
e^{0}
. The first remark is that
-E\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}+e^{0}E
vanishes on a solution. Therefore,

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},E^{b},e^{0})=E^{b}\,\tilde{\mathbb{t}}(t_{b})+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(\tilde{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\tilde{q}^{i}-\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{t}}\,H(\tilde{p},\tilde{q})).

We can actually pick
e^{0}
constant or at least of constant sign (by a gauge transformation).
With this choice we are sure that the map
t\mapsto\tilde{\mathbb{t}}(t)=\mathbb{t}_{a}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t}e^{0}
is a diffeomorphism. We can then make this change of variable in the integral, getting

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b};T)=E^{b}\,(\mathbb{t}_{a}+T)+\int_{\mathbb{t}_{a}}^{\mathbb{t}_{a}+T}(\check{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\check{q}^{i}-\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{t}}\,H(\check{p},\check{q})),

with
\check{p}(\tilde{\mathbb{t}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\tilde{p}(t(\mathbb{t}))
,
\check{q}(\tilde{\mathbb{t}})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\tilde{q}(t(\mathbb{t}))
and
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{0}
.
But now we recognize in the second term on the right hand side the time-dependent HJ action (
2
) for the hamiltonian
H
from time
\mathbb{t}_{a}
to time
\mathbb{t}_{a}+T
. Therefore, we have

(39)

\begin{split}\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},E^{b};T)&=S_{\text{HJ}}^{H}(q_{a},q_{b};\mathbb{t}_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a}+T)+E^{b}(\mathbb{t}_{a}+T)\\
&=S_{\text{HJ}}^{H}(q_{a},q_{b};0,T)+E^{b}(\mathbb{t}_{a}+T).\end{split}

One can easily verify that
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is a generating function for the evolution relation (
38
).

The HJ action
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
for endpoint conditions
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})

can be obtained from (
39
) by composing, following Remark
A.36
,
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
with the generating function
-E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{b}
of the identity map, and reducing with respect to the intermediate variable
E^{b}
; namely,
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
is the evaluation of

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}-E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{b}
at its critical point in
E^{b}
. We get

(40)

\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})=S_{\text{HJ}}^{H}(q_{a},q_{b};\mathbb{t}_{a},\mathbb{t}_{b}).

One can easily verify that
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
is also a generating function for the evolution relation (
38
).
Observe that the dependency on
T
has disappeared.

Note that we could not have computed
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
directly because fixing the endpoint conditions
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})
yields no solution in general.
Namely, choosing
e^{0}
arbitrarily would lead to no solutions if
\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a}\not=T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{0}
. A way to deal with such a situation will be discussed in Section
6
, see Remark
6.2
. As a result,
T
is fixed by the endpoint conditions and therefore is no longer an independent variable for
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
.

The HJ action for the time-independent, constrained system (a parametrization invariant theory) we considered in this section turns out to be the same as the usual HJ action for a time dependent system describing a hamiltonian evolution. In this case, time is restored via the endpoint conditions.

For simplicity we have obtained this result choosing
e^{0}
constant, so that we could make the change of variable globally.
We might have also worked with a generic
e^{0}
. In this case we would have made changes of variables in all regions where it is different from zero and ignored the other regions. The “time” variable
\tilde{\mathbb{t}}
would have suffered slowing down, speeding up, freezing and even rewinding, but with no change in the final result described
in (
40
)
by
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
.
23
23

23

As the prince observed, “Most people think time is like a river that flows swift and sure in one direction, but I have seen the face of time and I can tell you they are wrong. Time is an ocean in a storm,” yet “what is written in the timeline cannot be changed.”

Remark 5.1

(Integrable systems III)
.

As a generalization of Remarks
3.4
and
4.5
, let us consider the case of an integrable system where we have exactly
n
strictly involutive constraints
H_{1},\ldots,H_{n}
that are integrals of motion for a hamiltonian
H
, i.e., we have
\{H,H_{i}\}=\{H_{i},H_{j}\}=0
for all
i,j
. Around a point
(p_{0},q_{0})
where the differentials
\mathrm{d}H_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{d}H_{n}
are linearly independent, again by the Carathéodory–Jacobi–Lie theorem,
see fotonote
15
,
we may find a Darboux chart
(P_{i},Q^{i})
with
P_{i}=H_{i}
. Changing coordinates from
(p_{i},q^{i})
to
(P_{i},Q^{i})
in the integral we get

S^{g}[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0},e]=-g(q(t_{a}),P(t_{a}))+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(Q^{i}\mathrm{d}P_{i}-E\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}-e^{0}(H-E)-e^{i}{P_{i}}).

Notice that, since the constraints are integrals of motion, in
(P_{i},Q^{i})
coordinates the hamiltonian
H
depends only on
P_{i}
:
0=\{H,H_{i}\}=\{H,P_{i}\}=\partial H/\partial Q_{i}
.
On solutions, we have
\mathrm{d}P_{i}=0
,
\mathrm{d}Q^{i}=e^{i}+e^{0}\frac{\partial H}{\partial P_{i}}
and
\mathrm{d}H=0
. The HJ action then reads

(41)

\hat{S}^{g}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},P^{b},\mathbb{t}_{a},\mathbb{t}_{b};T)=-g(q_{a},P^{b})-(\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a})H(P^{b})-T^{i}P_{i}

in a neighborhood of
(p_{0},q_{0})
.
24
24

24

Here
T
stands for the collection
T^{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{i}
,
i>0
.
The parameter
T^{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{0}
does not appear, as in (
40
), because we fix
\mathbb{t}_{a}
and
\mathbb{t}_{b}
.

We may also compute the HJ action with
Q
endpoint conditions.
We have

S^{f}[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0},e]=-f(q(t_{a}),Q(t_{a}))+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(P_{i}\mathrm{d}Q^{i}-E\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}-e^{0}(H-E)-e^{i}P_{i}).

Solving the evolution equation for
Q^{i}
we obtain the HJ action
25
25

25

As in footnote
24
,

T
stands for the collection
T^{i}
,
i>0
.

(42)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},Q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{a},\mathbb{t}_{b};T)=-f\left(q_{a},Q_{b}-T-(\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a})\frac{\partial H}{\partial P}\right)+(\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a})H(P(q_{a},Q_{b})).

5.2.
The free relativistic particle

The above discussion generalizes easily to the case of a free, relativistic particle. In this case, there is no dynamics but only the constraint
E^{2}=m^{2}+p^{2}
. (For simplicity, we omit indices. If we are not in one dimension,
p^{2}
means

\sum_{i}(p_{i})^{2}
. Similarly, in the action we will simply write
p\mathrm{d}q
instead of
p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}
). Therefore, we consider the action

S[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0}]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\left(p\mathrm{d}q-E\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}-\frac{e^{0}}{2}(p^{2}+m^{2}-E^{2})\right),

where we have introduced the factor
\frac{1}{2}
just for convenience. The evolution equations are simply

\mathrm{d}q=e^{0}p,\quad\mathrm{d}p=0,\quad\mathrm{d}E=0,\quad\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}=e^{0}E,

whereas the constraint is

p^{2}+m^{2}-E^{2}=0.

We first compute the HJ action, without fixing
e^{0}
, for endpoint conditions given by
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},p^{b},E^{b})
. This means the we have to insert a solution of the evolution equations into

S^{f}[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0}]=E(t_{b})\mathbb{t}(t_{b})-p(t_{b})q(t_{b})+S[p,q,E,\mathbb{t},e^{0}].

The first evolution equation implies
p\mathrm{d}q=e^{0}p^{2}
. The second implies that
p(t)=p^{b}
for all
t
, so we actually
have
p\mathrm{d}q=e^{0}(p^{b})^{2}
. The third equation implies
E(t)=E^{b}
for all
t
, which together with the last equation implies

E\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}=e^{0}(E^{b})^{2}
.
Therefore,

S|_{\text{solution}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\frac{e^{0}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}}\,((p^{b})^{2}-m^{2}-(E^{b})^{2})=\frac{T}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}}\,((p^{b})^{2}-m^{2}-(E^{b})^{2}),

where we have set
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{0}
. Solving the evolution equations explicitly yields

q(t_{b})=q_{a}+p^{b}T\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{t}(t_{b})=\mathbb{t}_{a}+E^{b}T,

which implies

p(t_{b})q(t_{b})=p^{b}q_{a}+(p^{b})^{2}T\quad\text{and}\quad E(t_{b})\mathbb{t}(t_{b})=E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{a}+(E^{b})^{2}T

on solutions. We then have

(43)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},p^{b},E^{b};T)=E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{a}-p^{b}q_{a}+\frac{(E^{b})^{2}-(p^{b})^{2}-m^{2}}{2}T,

and one can easily verify that this is a generating funciton for the evolution relation.

We may now easily pass to the HJ action for endpoint conditions
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})
simply composing the generating function
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
with the generating function
p^{b}q_{b}-E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{b}
of the identity map,
and reducing with respect to the intermediate variables
(p^{b},E^{b})
; namely, we evaluate

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}+p^{b}q_{b}-E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{b}
at its critical point in
(p^{b},E^{b})
. Setting the derivatives with respect to
p^{b}
and
E^{b}
to zero yields

p^{b}=\frac{\Delta q}{T}\quad\text{and}\quad E^{b}=\frac{\Delta\mathbb{t}}{T},

where we have set

\Delta q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=q_{b}-q_{a}\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta\mathbb{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=t_{b}-t_{a}.

Inserting, we get

(44)

\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b};T)=\frac{(\Delta q)^{2}-(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}}{2T}-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}T.

We can finally try to reduce with respect to the variable
T
, i.e., to evaluate
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
at its critical point in
T
. Setting the derivative with respect to
T
to zero yields

T^{2}=\frac{(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}-(\Delta q)^{2}}{m^{2}}.

This shows that we can perform this reduction if and only if the endpoint conditions select a timelike trajectory:

(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}>(\Delta q)^{2}
.
26
26

26

This is actually a necessary condition for a solution to exist. In fact, solving the evolution equations for
\mathbb{t}
and
q
yields
\Delta\mathbb{t}=ET
and
\Delta q=pT
, so

(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}-(\Delta q)^{2}=(E^{2}-p^{2})T^{2}=m^{2}T^{2}\geq 0
. The case
(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}=(\Delta q)^{2}
would however imply
T=0
, so
\Delta\mathbb{t}=0
and
\Delta q=0
, which is excluded because the initial and final conditions must be distinct in the HJ setting.
There are two roots in
T
which yield, not unexpectedly,

(45)

S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})=\mp m\sqrt{(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}-(\Delta q)^{2}},

i.e., the Minkowskian length up to the factor
\mp m
. Both choices of sign give a generating function for the evolution relation.
27
27

27

Note that taking derivatives with respect to
\mathbb{t}_{a}
and
\mathbb{t}_{b}
yields

E^{a}=E^{b}=\pm m\frac{\Delta\mathbb{t}}{\sqrt{(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}-(\Delta q)^{2}}}
. In particular, for
\Delta q=0
, we get

E^{a}=E^{b}=\pm m\operatorname{sgn}{\Delta\mathbb{t}}
. For
\Delta\mathbb{t}>0
, it is the plus sign that yields the relation
E=m
for the relativistic particle, the minus sign corresponding to its antiparticle.

Remark 5.2
.

In the massless case
m=0
, equation (
44
) becomes

\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},T)=\frac{(\Delta q)^{2}-(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}}{2T},

so the last step—reduction in the variable
T
—is not possible (the critical point in
T
does not exist for general values of
q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b}
). Still, the vanishing of the derivative in
T
correctly yields the “lightlike displacement” constraint:
(\Delta q)^{2}-(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}=0
.

6.
Generalized generating functions for “bad” endpoint conditions

The presence of constraints is not the only source for the appearance of extra parameters in the generating function for the evolution relation. Another instance occurs when we choose “bad” endpoint conditions that do not ensure existence of a solution.

6.1.
No evolution and no constraints

We discuss a simple example here (more examples, also coupled to the presence of constraints, will appear in the rest of the paper). Consider the action

S[p,q]=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p\dot{q}\,\mathrm{d}t.

The EL equations are simply
\dot{p}=\dot{q}=0
, so the evolution relation is just the diagonal in
T^{*}\mathbb{R}\times T^{*}\mathbb{R}
:

L=\{(p,q,p,q)\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}\times T^{*}\mathbb{R},(p,q)\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}\}.

If we now choose endpoint conditions
q_{a}
and
q_{b}
, we will get no solutions unless
q_{a}=q_{b}
, in which case we get a whole family of solutions, parametrized by
p
solving
\dot{p}=0
.
Nevertheless, there is a generalized generating function for
L
:
28
28

28

This generalized generating function can also be obtained as the composition, in the sense of Remark
A.37
, of the generating functions

\psi_{1}(q_{a},\lambda)=-\lambda q_{a}
and
\psi_{2}(\lambda,q_{b})=\lambda q_{b}
that do and undo a
-\pi
-rotation in phase space.

S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b};\lambda)=\lambda\,(q_{b}-q_{a}).

In fact,
\frac{\partial S_{\text{gen}}}{\partial\lambda}=0
yields the condition
q_{a}=q_{b}
, whereas

p(t_{a})=-\frac{\partial S_{\text{gen}}}{\partial q_{a}}=\lambda
and
p(t_{b})=\frac{\partial S_{\text{gen}}}{\partial q_{b}}=\lambda

imply
p(t_{a})=p(t_{b})
.

This generating function may also be regarded as a sort of Hamilton–Jacobi action, where we impose only one EL equation: namely,
\dot{p}=0
. (The other equation,
\dot{q}=0
, cannot be imposed anyway because generically it has no solutions.) The parameter
\lambda
arises here as the constant value of
p
, so it parametrizes the family of solutions.

We may also obtain the generating function
S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b},\lambda)
by a partial Legendre transform, i.e., as

S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b};\lambda)=\lambda q_{b}+S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\lambda),

where

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b})=-p^{b}q_{a}

is the HJ action for the good choice
(q_{a},p^{b})
of endpoint conditions. Unlike the true Legendre transform (see Remark
2.9
), we do not evaluate at
\lambda^{\text{crit}}
, which in this case does not exist. We will put this observation more in context in Section
6.2
.

The generating function
S_{\text{gen}}
may appear even more naturally from the viewpoint of path integral quantization. In fact, since there is no evolution at all, the evolution operator is the identity operator, so, for the chosen polarizations, its integral kernel is a delta function:

K(q_{a},q_{b})=\delta(q_{a}-q_{b}).

By Fourier transform, we we can also write

K(q_{a},q_{b})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi\hbar}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b},\lambda)}.

One formal way to get this result directly from the path integral is as follows. We start with (
4
) and write
p=\lambda+\hat{p}
, where
\lambda
is a constant (a solution to
\dot{p}=0
). Note that
\lambda
here is still a variable to be integrated out, so we have to make sure that
\hat{p}\colon[t_{a},t_{b}]\to\mathbb{R}
is in a complement to the space of constant maps. For example, we may impose
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\,\mathrm{d}t=0
.
29
29

29

Let
\mathfrak{c}
be the space of constant maps and
\mathfrak{d}
the space of maps
\hat{p}\colon[t_{a},t_{b}]\to\mathbb{R}
with vanishing integral. We have
C^{\infty}([t_{a},t_{b}])=\mathfrak{c}\oplus\mathfrak{d}
.
In fact, if
\lambda
is constant and
(t_{a}-t_{b})\lambda=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\lambda\;\mathrm{d}t=0
, then
\lambda
vanishes, and we have shown that
\mathfrak{c}\cap\mathfrak{d}=0
.
On the other hand, every map
p
can be written as
\lambda+\hat{p}
with

\lambda=\frac{1}{t_{b}-t_{a}}\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p(t)\mathrm{d}t
. It follows immediately that
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\,\mathrm{d}t=0
.

Then we have

S[\lambda+\hat{p},q]=\lambda\,(q_{b}-q_{a})+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\dot{q}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Inserting into (
4
), we get

K(q_{a},q_{b})=\int\mathrm{d}\lambda\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\,\lambda\,(q_{b}-q_{a})}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}q(t_{a})=q_{a}\\
q(t_{b})=q_{b}\\
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\,\mathrm{d}t=0\end{subarray}}D\hat{p}\,Dq\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\dot{q}\,\mathrm{d}t}.

The second integral formally does not depend on
q_{a}
and
q_{b}
: in fact, we can make the affine change of variables

q\mapsto\hat{q}
with

\hat{q}(t)=q(t)-\frac{t-t_{b}}{t_{a}-t_{b}}q_{a}-\frac{t-t_{a}}{t_{b}-t_{a}}q_{b}.

Observe that
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\dot{q}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\dot{\hat{q}}\,\mathrm{d}t
, since
\dot{q}-\dot{\hat{q}}
is constant,
and that
\hat{q}(t_{a})=\hat{q}(t_{b})=0
. Therefore,

\begin{split}K(q_{a},q_{b})&=\int\mathrm{d}\lambda\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\,\lambda\,(q_{b}-q_{a})}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{q}(t_{a})=\hat{q}(t_{b})=0\\
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\,\mathrm{d}t=0\end{subarray}}D\hat{p}\,D\hat{q}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}\dot{\hat{q}}\,\mathrm{d}t}\\
&\propto\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi\hbar}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b},\lambda)}.\end{split}

In this example, one might also think of
\lambda
as parametrizing the vacua of the theory. Instead of integrating over vacua, one may decide to select just one, labeled by
\lambda
, and in this case interpret
S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b},\lambda)

as the corresponding semiclassical contribution, more in line with Hamilton–Jacobi.

Remark 6.1
.

For an analysis of this example in the BV-BFV setting, see
[
19
, Sect. 4.4]

Remark 6.2

(Classical mechanics)
.

We may apply the above considerations to the system with one constraint described in Section
5.1
.
We fix
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})
and let
T=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{0}
. From the evolution equation
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{t}=e^{0}
, we get that there is no solution unless
T=\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a}
. Therefore, we have to introduce a new parameter
\lambda
to fix this condition, and we get

\check{S}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b};T,\lambda)=S_{\text{HJ}}^{H}(q_{a},q_{b};\mathbb{t}_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a}+T)+\lambda\,(T-\mathbb{t}_{b}+\mathbb{t}_{a}).

Of course this generating function may be reduced. Setting the derivative with respect to
\lambda
to zero, we get back
T=\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a}
, which may be inserted into
\check{S}_{\text{HJ}}
yielding
\hat{S}_{\text{HJ}}
as in (
40
).

6.2.
The partial Legendre transform

Suppose our system has a unique solution for endpoint conditions
(q_{a},p^{b})
, so that we have the HJ action

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b})
as in Example
2.8
. We define the partial Legendre transform of
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
as

S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b},\lambda)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\lambda_{i}q_{b}^{i}+S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\lambda).

It follows from (
7
) that
S_{\text{gen}}
is a generalized generating function for the same evolution relation as
S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
is. In fact,
L_{S_{\text{gen}}}
is determined by
S_{\text{gen}}
via the equations

p^{b}_{i}=\frac{\partial S_{\text{gen}}}{\partial q^{i}_{b}},\quad p^{a}_{i}=-\frac{\partial S_{\text{gen}}}{\partial q^{i}_{a}},\quad\frac{\partial S_{\text{gen}}}{\partial\lambda}=0.

Since
\frac{\partial S_{\text{gen}}}{\partial q^{i}_{b}}=\lambda_{i}
, we get
p^{b}=\lambda
. Moreover, by using (
7
), the last equation reads
q_{b}-q_{q_{a},\lambda}(t_{b})=0
and the middle equation yields

p^{a}=p^{q_{a},\lambda}(t_{a})
. Therefore,
L_{S_{\text{gen}}}
consists of endpoint values
(p^{a},q_{a},p^{b},q_{b})

of a solution and is thus the evolution relation.
30
30

30

It may of course happen that the last equation,
q_{b}-q_{q_{a},\lambda}(t_{b})=0
, can be solved for a unique
\lambda
, which we then denote as
\lambda^{\text{crit}}
. We may then insert this value into
S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b},\lambda)
and get a generating function without extra parameters, the true Legendre transform
S_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},q_{b})
as in Remark
2.9
.

The above argument extends immediately to the case with constraints. Namely, suppose we have the HJ action

S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b})[e]
. Then we define its partial Legendre transform as

S_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b},\lambda)[e]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\lambda_{i}q_{b}^{i}+S^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\lambda)[e],

and one immediately verifies, as above, that this is also a generalized generating function for the evolution relation.

Example 6.3

(Partial Legendre transform for linear constraints)
.

Consider the case of a system with linear constraints in strict involution as described in Example
4.3
. We will suppress the target-space index
i
.
If we apply the partial Legendre transform to (
24
), we get

\hat{S}_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b};\lambda,T)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\lambda q_{b}+\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},\lambda;T)=\lambda(q_{b}-q_{a})-T^{\alpha}(\lambda v_{\alpha}+w_{\alpha}q_{a})-\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}T^{\beta}A_{\alpha\beta},

which is also a generating function for the evolution relation. We may change this expression a bit by observing that the derivative with respect to
\lambda
(i.e.,
q_{b}-q_{a}-T^{\alpha}v_{\alpha}
) will have to be set to zero to define the evolution relation. We can insert this relation into
\hat{S}_{\text{gen}}
(without having to solve with respect to
\lambda
). Actually, multiplying the relation with
T^{\beta}w_{\beta}
and using (
23
), we get

T^{\alpha}T^{\beta}A_{\alpha\beta}=T^{\beta}w_{\beta}q_{b}-T^{\beta}w_{\beta}q_{a}.

We can use this to get rid of the quadratic term in the
T
s getting the generalized generating function

(46)

\begin{split}\tilde{S}_{\text{gen}}(q_{a},q_{b},\lambda,T)&=\lambda(q_{b}-q_{a})-T^{\alpha}\lambda v_{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}q_{a}-\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}q_{b}\\
&=\left(\lambda-\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\right)q_{b}-\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\right)q_{a}-T^{\alpha}\lambda v_{\alpha}.\end{split}

7.
Infinite-dimensional targets

For simplicity, up to now we have only discussed examples where the target symplectic manifold is finite-dimensional. The whole discussion can be generalized, almost verbatim, to the case when the target is infinite-dimensional and there are possibly infinitely many constraints. We will touch upon more analytical issues in
Appendix
A
, and in particular in
A.3
. Note that the main problem is that, in principle, the evolution relation might fail to be lagrangian.
31
31

31

This is due to intrinsically analytical problems. See
[
16
, Sect. 5.9]
for an example.

In in this section we will only
be considering infinite-dimensional examples where this problem does not actually arise (mainly thanks to the Hodge decomposition of differential forms).

7.1.
Three-dimensional abelian Chern–Simons theory

As a warm up, we discuss here the case of abelian Chern–Simons theory in three dimensions. The action for a cylinder
I\times\Sigma
, where
I=[t_{a},t_{b}]
is an interval and
\Sigma
is a closed, oriented surface,
is

S[A]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I\times\Sigma}{A}\mathrm{d}{A}

with
A\in\Omega^{1}(I\times\Sigma)
. A variation of the action yields

\delta S=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\delta{A}\mathrm{d}{A}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{t_{b}\}\times\Sigma}{A}\delta{A}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{t_{a}\}\times\Sigma}{A}\delta{A},

from which we read off the EL equations
\mathrm{d}{A}=0
and the boundary symplectic structure:

\mathcal{F}^{\partial}=\Omega^{1}(\Sigma),\quad\omega=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\delta A\delta A.

We may split
A
into its “horizontal” and “vertical” part

A=A_{\Sigma}+A_{I}

and regard
A_{\Sigma}
as a map
I\to\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)=\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
, with target symplectic form

-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\delta A_{\Sigma}\delta A_{\Sigma}
,
and
A_{I}
as a one-form on
I
with values in

\Omega^{0}(\Sigma)
. Splitting also
\mathrm{d}
as
\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}+\mathrm{d}_{I}
, we then have

S^{\prime}[A_{\Sigma},A_{I}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S[A_{\Sigma}+A_{I}]=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\left(\frac{1}{2}A_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}_{I}A_{\Sigma}+A_{I}\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}A_{\Sigma}\right),

from which we see that
A_{\Sigma}
represents the dynamical variables, whereas
A_{I}
represents the Lagrange multipliers. Moreover, we now split the EL equations into the evolution equations

\mathrm{d}_{I}A_{\Sigma}=-\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}A_{I}

and the constraints

\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}A_{\Sigma}=0.

This is a system with linear constraints (in strict involution, since
\mathrm{d}^{2}=0
) that, almost, fits into Example
4.3
. What is missing is a splitting of the symplectic space of fields into
p
and
q
variables (or, more precisely, a splitting in the sense of
Remark
A.6
). We do it in the complexified version

\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\mathbb{C}}=\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathbb{C}
, which splits as
\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma)\oplus\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma)
in terms of a complex structure on
\Sigma
.
32
32

32

This actually corresponds to choosing a complex polarization for the real Chern–Simons theory.

We write accordingly
A_{\Sigma}=A^{1,0}+A^{0,1}
and
\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}=\partial+\bar{\partial}
, so

S^{\prime}[A_{\Sigma},A_{I}]=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\left(\frac{1}{2}A^{1,0}\mathrm{d}_{I}A^{0,1}+\frac{1}{2}A^{0,1}\mathrm{d}_{I}A^{1,0}+A_{I}(\bar{\partial}A^{1,0}+\partial A^{0,1})\right).

We can integrate by parts the second term, so that the action reduces, up to a boundary term, to

S^{\prime\prime}(A^{1,0},A^{0,1},A_{I})=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\left(A^{1,0}\mathrm{d}_{I}A^{0,1}+A_{I}(\bar{\partial}A^{1,0}+\partial A^{0,1})\right),

where we recognize
A^{1,0}
as the
p
variables and
A^{0,1}
as the
q
variables. The variation now, correctly, produces the boundary one-form
\int_{\Sigma}A^{1,0}\delta A^{0,1}
.

We can finally apply the results of Example
4.3
to this case where, up to signs, the matrix
v^{i}_{\alpha}
is now replaced by the operator
\bar{\partial}
and the matrix
w_{i\beta}
is replaced by the operator
\partial
. We write
\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{I}A_{I}\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma)
instead of
T
,
\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{a}
instead of
q_{a}
and
\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{b}
instead of
p^{b}
, so equation (
24
) becomes

(47)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}=\int_{\Sigma}\left(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{b}\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{a}+\sigma(\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{b}+\partial\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{a})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma\right).

Following Example
6.3
we may also get the generating function for the same choice of both endpoint conditions. Namely, extending (
46
) to this case, we get

(48)

\tilde{S}_{\text{gen}}=\int_{\Sigma}\left(\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{b}\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\partial}\sigma\right)-\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{a}\left(\lambda-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\partial}\sigma\right)+\lambda\partial\sigma\right)

with
\lambda\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma)
.

7.2.
Nonabelian Chern–Simons theory

In this case the action is

S[A]=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\left(\frac{1}{2}\langle{A},\mathrm{d}{A}\rangle+\frac{1}{6}\langle{A},[{A},{A}]\rangle\right),

where now
A
takes values in a quadratic Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},\langle\ ,\ \rangle)
.

Proceeding as in the abelian case, we get

S^{\prime\prime}(A^{1,0},A^{0,1},A_{I})=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\left(\langle A^{1,0},\mathrm{d}_{I}A^{0,1}\rangle+\langle A_{I},\bar{\partial}A^{1,0}+\partial A^{0,1}+[A^{0,1},A^{1,0}]\rangle\right),

where we recognize the data of Example
4.8
and Remark
4.9

with
-A^{1,0}
as the
\bar{q}
,
A^{0,1}
as the
q
,
A_{I}
as the
e
variables,

\partial
as the derivation
\bar{v}
, and
\bar{\partial}
as the derivation
v
.
33
33

33

To be more precise, the Lie algebra of
Remark
4.9
is now
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g})
with pointwise Lie bracket induced from that of
\mathfrak{g}
, the trivial representation space
Z
is
\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma)
, and their tensor product, over
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma)
, is

V=\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g})
.
The dual space
V^{*}
is now replaced by
\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g})
with pairing to
V
induced by the paring
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
on
\mathfrak{g}
and integration on
\Sigma
.

We write
\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{a}
instead of
q_{a}
and
-\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{b}
instead of
\bar{q}^{b}
,
so (
35
) becomes

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{b},g^{-1}\,\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{a}g\rangle+\langle\mathsf{A}^{1,0}_{b},g^{-1}\bar{\partial}g\rangle+\langle\mathsf{A}^{0,1}_{a},\partial g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle\Big)+\mathrm{WZW}(g)

with
g=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{I}A_{I}}\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,G)
. For
g=\mathrm{e}^{\xi}
,
\xi\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g})
,
from (
36
) we get

\mathrm{WZW}(g)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\langle\partial g\cdot g^{-1},\bar{\partial}g\cdot g^{-1}\rangle-\frac{1}{12}\int_{\Sigma\times I}\langle\mathrm{d}h\cdot h^{-1},[\mathrm{d}h\cdot h^{-1},\mathrm{d}h\cdot h^{-1}]\rangle,

with
h=\mathrm{e}^{t\xi}
.
Thus, the HJ action of Chern–Simons theory can be identified with a “gauged WZW action” (see for instance
[
33
]
). This points at a deep relationship between these two theories. We will revisit this correspondence in more detail in
[
21
]
.

7.3.
Nonabelian
BF
theory

Consider

BF
theory, defined by the action

(49)

S_{BF}=\int_{M}\langle B,F_{A}\rangle.

Here
M
is an
n
-manifold and the fields are a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued
1
-form
A
(viewed as a connection with curvature
2
-form
F_{A}=\mathrm{d}A+\frac{1}{2}[A,A]
) and a
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
-valued
(n-2)
-form
B
;
34
34

34

More generally,
A
is a connection in a principal
G
-bundle
\mathcal{P}
over
M
and
B
is an
(n-2)
-form valued in the coadjoint bundle of
\mathcal{P}
. In the context of a cylinder
M=I\times\Sigma
, we would take
\mathcal{P}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}
where
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}
is a
G
-bundle over
\Sigma
and
\pi\colon I\times\Sigma\rightarrow\Sigma
is the projection. The results of this subsection and of subsection
7.4
generalize immediately to this setup.

\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of some fixed Lie group
G
.

On an
n
-dimensional cylinder
M=I\times\Sigma
, the action becomes

(50)

S_{BF}=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\langle B_{\Sigma},\mathrm{d}_{I}A_{\Sigma}\rangle+\langle A_{I},\mathrm{d}_{A_{\Sigma}}B_{\Sigma}\rangle+\langle B_{I},F_{A_{\Sigma}}\rangle,

where
\mathrm{d}_{A_{\Sigma}}B_{\Sigma}=\mathrm{d}B_{\Sigma}+[A_{\Sigma},B_{\Sigma}]
is the covariant exterior derivative. Here we expanded the fields according to form degree on
I
:
A=A_{\Sigma}+A_{I}
,
B=B_{\Sigma}+B_{I}
.
Note that
A_{I}
and
B_{I}
in (
50
) are Lagrange multipliers corresponding to the constraints
\mathrm{d}_{A_{\Sigma}}B_{\Sigma}=0
and
F_{A_{\Sigma}}=0
.

Using
A_{a},B_{b}
boundary conditions, the generalized HJ action reads

\hat{S}_{\mathrm{HJ}}(A_{a},B_{b};g,\beta)=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\beta,F_{A_{a}}\rangle-(-1)^{n}\langle B_{b},g^{-1}A_{a}g+g^{-1}d_{\Sigma}g\rangle.

Here the auxiliary fields are
g=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}A_{I}}\in\mathrm{Map}(\Sigma,G)
and
\beta=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\mathrm{Ad}^{*}_{P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t}A_{I}}}(B_{I})\in\Omega^{n-3}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g}^{*})
. The case of
(A_{a},A_{b})
boundary conditions is treated by the partial Legendre transform:

S_{\mathrm{gen}}(A_{a},A_{b};g,\beta,\lambda)=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\beta,F_{A_{a}}\rangle-(-1)^{n}\langle\lambda,g^{-1}A_{a}g+g^{-1}d_{\Sigma}g\rangle+(-1)^{n}\langle\lambda,A_{b}\rangle.

A similar expression appeared in
[
43
]
as a holographic dual of
BF
theory (in the three-dimensional case and with a different—holomorphic—polarization on the boundary).

Remark 7.1

(Relation to Group Quantum Mechanics)
.

Specializing to
n=2
, and assuming
\Sigma
to be connected, we have
\Sigma=S^{1}
. On
\Omega^{\bullet}(S^{1},\mathfrak{g})
we can consider a set of canonical coordinates given by
Q=B
,
P=A-*_{S^{1}}B
, where
*_{S^{1}}
denotes the Hogde star for some choice of Riemannian metric on
S^{1}
. We choose the coordinates
q=A,p=B
at
t_{a}
and
Q,P
at
t_{b}
. The corresponding generating function is
f(A,B)=\int_{S^{1}}\langle B,A\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle B,*_{S^{1}}B\rangle
and the HJ action is given by

(51)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\mathrm{HJ}}(A_{a},B_{b},g)=-\int_{S^{1}}\langle B_{b},g^{-1}A_{a}g+g^{-1}d_{S^{1}}g\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle B_{b},*_{S^{1}}B_{b}\rangle.

One may further specialize by choosing the boundary condition
A_{a}=0
and imposing the equation of motion
B_{b}=-*_{S^{1}}g^{-1}dg
for
B_{b}
. The action then reduces to

(52)

S_{GQM}(g)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{1}}\langle g^{-1}dg,*_{S^{1}}g^{-1}dg\rangle,

the action for a free particle moving in the group
G
, studied for instance in
[
32
]
.
We thus recover a relation between a 2D theory and group quantum mechanics
which could be seen as an instance of holographic correspondence. In the case
G=PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
(corresponding to a model of 2D gravity whose classical solutions are constant negative curvature geometries) it was discussed
in the literature on
\mathrm{AdS}_{2}\mathrm{/CFT}_{1}
correspondence. In
[
48
]
a very interesting subsequent reduction from
S_{GQM}
was considered, in the case
G=PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
, leading to the Schwarzian 1D theory.

7.4.
More examples: 2D Yang–Mills theory and electrodynamics in general dimension

In this section we discuss two examples of systems with nontrivial evolution and constraints: two special cases of Yang–Mills theory on a cylinder. They provide (generalized) examples of the setup of Section
5
.

Consider the first-order formulation of Yang–Mills theory,

(53)

S_{\mathrm{YM}}[A,B]=\int_{M}\langle B,F_{A}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle B,*B\rangle,

where
M
is a pseudo-Riemannian
n
-manifold which we will take to be a cylinder
M=I\times\Sigma
with product metric
g=-(\mathrm{d}t)^{2}+g_{\Sigma}
and
g_{\Sigma}
a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
; here
*
is the associated Hodge star operator.
Note that the action (
53
) is an extension of the
BF
action (
49
) by a metric-dependent term.

Expanding the fields on the cylinder according to form degree along
I
as
A=A_{\Sigma}+A_{I}
,
B=B_{\Sigma}+B_{I}
, we can write the action (
53
) as

(54)

S_{\mathrm{YM}}=\int_{I\times\Sigma}\langle B_{\Sigma},\mathrm{d}_{I}A_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}*B_{\Sigma}\rangle+\langle B_{I},F_{A_{\Sigma}}-\frac{1}{2}*B_{I}\rangle+\langle A_{I},\mathrm{d}_{A_{\Sigma}}B_{\Sigma}\rangle.

The EL equations read

(55a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{I}A_{\Sigma}+\mathrm{d}_{A_{\Sigma}}A_{I}-*B_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(55b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{I}B_{\Sigma}+[A_{I},B_{\Sigma}]+\mathrm{d}_{A_{\Sigma}}B_{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(55c)

\displaystyle F_{A_{\Sigma}}-*B_{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(55d)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{A_{\Sigma}}B_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Here
A_{I}
is a Lagrange multiplier and (
55d
) is the corresponding constraint;
B_{I}
is not a Lagrange multiplier (enters the action quadratically) and although the corresponding equation (
55c
) does not contain time derivatives, it does not impose constraints on the fields in the phase space (which
B_{I}
is not a part of). Therefore, (
55c
) is treated as part of the evolution relations, alongside (
55a
) and (
55b
), for the purpose of computing the HJ action.

Introducing a group-valued variable
h(t)=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t}A_{I}}\in\mathrm{Map}(I\times\Sigma,G)
and performing a change of variables

A_{\Sigma}=h^{-1}\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}h+h^{-1}\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}h,\quad B_{\Sigma}=h^{-1}\widetilde{B}_{\Sigma}h,\quad B_{I}=h^{-1}\widetilde{B}_{I}h

we can rewrite the evolution equations as

(56)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}+(-1)^{n}*_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}_{\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}}*_{\Sigma}F_{\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}}=0,

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{I}=*F_{\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}},\quad\widetilde{B}_{\Sigma}=*_{\Sigma}\partial_{t}\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}.

Thus, essentially, one has to solve the (generally very complicated) nonlinear equation (
56
) and recover the remaining fields using the other two equations. Equation (
56
) becomes linear in two cases: the case
n=2
(as the second term in (
56
) vanishes by a degree reason) and the abelian case.

7.4.1.
2D Yang–Mills theory.

In the two-dimensional case (with
\Sigma=S^{1}
),
we have
B_{I}=0
for a degree reason (and thus
B=B_{\Sigma}
), so we are exactly in the setting of Section
5
. The action (
54
) on
I\times S^{1}
becomes

S_{\mathrm{YM}}=\int_{I\times S^{1}}\langle B,\mathrm{d}_{I}A_{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}*B\rangle+\langle A_{I},\mathrm{d}_{A_{\Sigma}}B\rangle.

In terms of the boundary conditions
(A_{a},B_{b})
, we obtain the following generalized HJ action:

\hat{S}^{f}_{\mathrm{HJ}}(A_{a},B_{b};g)=\int_{\Sigma}-\langle B_{b},g^{-1}A_{a}g+g^{-1}d_{\Sigma}g\rangle-\frac{\tau}{2}\langle B_{b},*_{\Sigma}B_{b}\rangle,

where
\tau=t_{b}-t_{a}
. Here
g=h(t_{b})=P\mathrm{e}^{\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}A_{I}}\in\mathrm{Map}(\Sigma,G)
, the group-valued auxiliary variable for the generalized HJ action. We can also consider
(A_{a},A_{b})
boundary conditions, and then the answer is

S_{\mathrm{gen}}(A_{a},A_{b};g,\lambda)=\int_{\Sigma}-\langle\lambda,g^{-1}A_{a}g+g^{-1}d_{\Sigma}g\rangle-\frac{\tau}{2}\langle\lambda,*_{\Sigma}\lambda\rangle+\langle\lambda,A_{b}\rangle.

Here
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{g}^{*})
is the new auxiliary field.

7.4.2.
Electrodynamics (general dimension).

Next consider Yang–Mills theory (
53
) on a cylinder of general dimension in the abelian case,
G=\mathbb{R}
.
Solving the evolution equations and plugging the result into the action, we find, for boundary conditions
(A_{a}
,
B_{b})
, the following generalized HJ action:
35
35

35

In particular, equation (
56
) reads
\partial_{t}^{2}\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}+(-1)^{n}*_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}*_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}=0
(where the change of variables is
A_{\Sigma}=\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}+\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}\int_{t_{a}}^{t}A_{I}
). It breaks into two equations for the closed and coexact parts of
\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}
:
\partial_{t}^{2}\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{cl}}=0
and
\partial_{t}^{2}\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{coex}}=-\Delta_{\Sigma}A_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{coex}}
. The solution of the boundary problem is then
\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{cl}}=A_{a}^{\mathrm{cl}}+(-1)^{n}*_{\Sigma}B_{b}^{\mathrm{cocl}}\,t
,

\widetilde{A}_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{coex}}=\sum_{\sigma}\chi_{\sigma}\Big(\frac{\sin\omega_{\sigma}t}{\omega_{\sigma}\cos\omega_{\sigma}(t_{b}-t_{a})}B_{b}^{\sigma}+\frac{\cos\omega_{\sigma}(t_{b}-t)}{\cos\omega_{\sigma}(t_{b}-t_{a})}A_{a}^{\sigma}\Big)
.

(57)

\hat{S}_{\mathrm{HJ}}(A_{a},B_{b};\gamma)=\int_{\Sigma}\left(-\frac{\tau}{2}B_{b}^{\mathrm{cocl}}\wedge*_{\Sigma}B_{b}^{\mathrm{cocl}}-A^{\mathrm{cl}}_{a}\wedge B^{\mathrm{cocl}}_{b}-\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}\gamma\wedge B_{b}\right)+\\
+\sum_{\sigma}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\tan\omega_{\sigma}\tau}{\omega_{\sigma}}(B_{b}^{\sigma})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\sigma}\tan\omega_{\sigma}\tau\,(A_{a}^{\sigma})^{2}-\frac{1}{\cos\omega_{\sigma}\tau}A_{a}^{\sigma}B_{b}^{\sigma}\right).

Here:

•

\tau=t_{b}-t_{a}
.

•

\gamma=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}A_{I}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
is an auxiliary field.

•

We introduce
\{\chi_{\sigma}\}
, an orthonormal basis of coexact
1
-forms on
\Sigma
which diagonalize the Laplacian:
\Delta_{\Sigma}\chi_{\sigma}=\omega_{\sigma}^{2}\chi_{\sigma}
. We split the field
A_{a}
, according to the Hodge decomposition, into its closed and coexact parts:

A_{a}=A_{a}^{\mathrm{cl}}+\sum_{\sigma}\chi_{\sigma}\,A_{a}^{\sigma}
.

•

Similarly,
\{*_{\Sigma}\chi_{\sigma}\}
is an orthonormal basis of exact
(n-2)
-forms on
\Sigma
and we have a splitting
B_{b}=B_{b}^{\mathrm{cocl}}+\sum_{\Sigma}*_{\Sigma}\chi_{\sigma}\,B_{b}^{\sigma}
into coclosed and exact parts.

Note that the term
\int_{\Sigma}d_{\Sigma}\gamma\wedge B_{b}
in
\hat{S}_{\mathrm{HJ}}
depends in fact only on the coexact part of
B_{b}
.

We also remark that the summand in the sum over
\sigma
in (
57
) is the HJ action of the harmonic oscillator with frequency
\omega_{\sigma}
. Here the oscillator corresponds to the field
A^{\sigma}(t)
(the coefficient of the expansion of
A_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{coex}}
in the basis
\{\chi_{\sigma}\}
) with corresponding momentum
B^{\sigma}(t)
(the coefficient of the expansion of
B_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{ex}}
in the basis
\{*_{\Sigma}\chi_{\sigma}\}
).

Remark 7.2

(
p
-form electrodynamics)
.

The case of abelian Yang–Mills theory generalizes straightforwardly to “
p
-form electrodynamics”—the theory defined by the action

{S_{p\mathrm{-form}}=\int_{M}B\wedge\mathrm{d}A-\frac{1}{2}B\wedge*B},

where the fields are
A\in\Omega^{p}(M)
,
{B\in\Omega^{n-p-1}(M)}
.
The generalized HJ action on a cylinder is given by an expression identical to (
57
) up to signs:

\hat{S}_{\mathrm{HJ}}(A_{a},B_{b};\gamma)=\int_{\Sigma}\left(-\frac{\tau}{2}B_{b}^{\mathrm{cocl}}\wedge*_{\Sigma}B_{b}^{\mathrm{cocl}}+\epsilon A^{\mathrm{cl}}_{a}\wedge B^{\mathrm{cocl}}_{b}+\epsilon\,\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma}\gamma\wedge B_{b}\right)+\\
+\sum_{\sigma}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\tan\omega_{\sigma}\tau}{\omega_{\sigma}}(B_{b}^{\sigma})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\sigma}\tan\omega_{\sigma}\tau\,(A_{a}^{\sigma})^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{\cos\omega_{\sigma}\tau}A_{a}^{\sigma}B_{b}^{\sigma}\right).

Here
\epsilon=(-1)^{np+n+p}
;
A_{a}^{\sigma}
are the coefficients of the expansion on
A_{a}^{\mathrm{coex}}
in the orthonormal basis
\chi_{\sigma}
of coexact
p
-forms on
\Sigma
;
B_{b}^{\sigma}
are the coefficients of the expansion of
B_{b}^{\mathrm{ex}}
in the orthonormal basis
*_{\Sigma}\chi_{\sigma}
of exact
(n-p-1)
-forms on
\Sigma
; the auxiliary field
\gamma
is a
(p-1)
-form on
\Sigma
. Note that the case
p=0
corresponds to the massless scalar theory which has no constraints (and the field
\gamma
disappears for a degree reason).

7.5.
Higher-dimensional Chern-Simons theory

The results of Section
7.1
are easily generalized to higher-dimensional abelian Chern–Simons theories.
Consider again a cylinder
I\times M
, where
M
is now a
4k+2
-dimensional manifold (
k=0,1,2,\ldots)
with a complex structure and

(58)

S[A]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I\times M}A\mathrm{d}A,

for
A\in\Omega^{2k+1}(M)
. Proceeding as above, we obtain
A=A_{M}+A_{I}
with
A_{M}
a map
I\to\Omega^{2k+1}(M)
and
A_{I}
a one-form on
I
with values in
\Omega^{2k}(M)
, and we have

(59)

S^{\prime}[A_{M},A_{I}]=\int_{I\times M}\frac{1}{2}A_{M}\mathrm{d}_{I}A_{M}+A_{I}\mathrm{d}_{M}A_{M}.

To obtain a splitting of the symplectic space of fields
\Omega^{2k+1}(M)
, we pass to the complexification and employ a decomposition by complex bidegree, namely

(60)

\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\mathbb{C}}=\Omega^{2k+1}(M,\mathbb{C})=\underbrace{\Omega^{2k+1,0}(M)\oplus\cdots\oplus\Omega^{k+1,k}(M)}_{\Omega^{2k+1}_{+}(M)}\oplus\underbrace{\Omega^{k,k+1}(M)\oplus\cdots\oplus\Omega^{0,2k+1}(M)}_{\Omega^{2k+1}_{-}(M)}

and accordingly we write
A_{M}=A_{M}^{+}+A_{M}^{-}
. Plugging this decomposition into (
59
), we obtain, up to a boundary term,

(61)

S^{\prime\prime}(A_{M}^{+},A_{M}^{-},A_{I})=\int_{I\times M}A_{M}^{-}d_{I}A_{M}^{+}+A_{I}(d_{M}A_{M}^{+}+d_{M}A_{M}^{-})

with corresponding boundary 1-form
A_{M}^{-}\delta A_{M}^{+}
. We will choose the endpoint conditions
A_{M}^{+}
on

M\times\{0\}
and
A_{M}^{-}
on
M\times\{1\}
.
36
36

36

The attentive reader will have noticed that for the 3D case
k=0
this gives the opposite boundary conditions than in Section
7.1
. This flips the sign of the last term in (
62
) below.

Notice that for the component of
A_{I}
of Hodge type
(k,k)
, the last term reads
A_{I}^{k,k}(\bar{\partial}A_{M}^{k+1,k}+\partial A_{M}^{k,k+1})
. This gives rise to the only nonvanishing “component” of the matrix
A_{\alpha\beta}
of Example
4.3
, the other components vanishing because of
\mathrm{d}_{M}^{2}=0
. Relabeling
\int A_{I}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sigma\in\Omega^{2k}(M)
we obtain the HJ action

(62)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}=\int_{M}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{b}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{a}+\sigma(d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{a}+d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{-}_{b})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{k,k}\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma^{k,k}.

In applications it is interesting to consider more general coordinates on the space
\Omega^{2k+1}(M)
. Assume that
(A^{Q},A^{P})
is such a set of coordinates and that
f(A^{+}_{M},A^{Q})
is the corresponding generating function. Then, according to Remark
4.4
, the HJ action is

(63)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}=f(\mathsf{A}^{+}_{a}+d^{+}_{M}\sigma,\mathsf{A}^{Q}_{b})+\int_{M}\sigma d_{M}\mathsf{A}^{+}_{a}-\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{k,k}\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma^{k,k},

where
\mathrm{d}_{M}^{+}
is the composition of
\mathrm{d}_{M}
with the projection to
\Omega^{2k+1}_{+}(M)
, and the sign in front of the last term is due to using the
+
-representation on the
a
-boundary.

7.6.
A nonlinear polarization in 7D Chern–Simons theory and the Kodaira–Spencer action

Let us now focus on the 7D case (
k=1
in the section above): i.e.,
M
is now a 6-dimensional manifold with a complex structure. We will now actually assume that it is Kähler,
and fix a reference nonvanishing holomorphic 3-form
\omega_{0}
. Let us fix some notation. We denote by

\Omega^{-p,q}(M)
sections of the bundle
\bigwedge^{p}(T_{\mathbb{C}}M)^{1,0}\wedge\bigwedge^{q}(T^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}M)^{0,1}
, i.e.,
(0,q)
-forms with values in
(p,0)
-vector fields. Contraction with the reference holomorphic 3-form provides a map

\displaystyle\Omega^{-p,q}(M)

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{3-p,q}(M)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto A^{\vee}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\omega_{0}

(we omit symbols for wedge products and contractions). For
A,B,C\in\Omega^{-1,1}(M)
, we further define the operations

(64a)

\displaystyle A^{\vee}\circ B^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(AB)\omega_{0}\in\Omega^{1,2}(M),

(64b)

\displaystyle\langle A^{\vee},B^{\vee},C^{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=A^{\vee}(B^{\vee}\circ C^{\vee})\in\Omega^{3,3}(M),

(64c)

\displaystyle\langle A^{3}\rangle

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{6}}\frac{\langle A^{\vee},A^{\vee},A^{\vee}\rangle}{\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}}\in\Omega^{0}(M),

and similarly for
A,B,C\in\Omega^{1,-1}(M)
by replacing
\omega_{0}
with its conjugate
\bar{\omega}_{0}
.

The symplectic space of boundary fields is
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}=\Omega^{3}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}
. The geometry of this space was studied by Hitchin in
[
35
]
. The main point of interest for us is that any complex 3-form admits a splitting
37
37

37

The superscript
\mathrm{nl}
stands for “nonlinear” and indicates that the map
A_{M}\mapsto(A_{M}^{+,\mathrm{nl}},A_{M}^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
) is nonlinear, as opposed to
A_{M}\mapsto(A_{M}^{+},A_{M}^{-})
, which is linear.

A_{M}=A_{M}^{+,\mathrm{nl}}+A_{M}^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
where
A_{M}^{\pm,\mathrm{nl}}
are decomposable three-forms. Here, a 3-form
A
is called decomposable if around every point there exists a local coframe
\theta_{i}
in which
A=\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}\wedge\theta_{3}
. We will call
A
nondegenerate if
A_{M}^{+,\mathrm{nl}}\wedge A_{M}^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
is everywhere nonvanishing. All three-forms which are not themselves decomposable are nondegenerate and for nondegenerate 3-forms
A^{\pm,\mathrm{nl}}
are uniquely determined by
A

[
35
]
. This splitting defines a polarization
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{nl},-}
on
38
38

38

Strictly speaking, the polarization is defined only on the open subset of nondegenerate forms.

\Omega^{3}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}
by letting
\mathcal{P}_{A_{M}}\subset T_{A_{M}}\Omega^{3}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\Omega^{3}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}
be the subspace spanned by the
A_{M}^{+,\mathrm{nl}}
, whose leaf space is parametrized by
A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}_{M}
. To write down a generating function
f(A_{M}^{+},A_{M}^{-,\mathrm{nl}})
,
we parametrize
A_{M}^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
by

(65)

A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}=\overline{\rho}e^{\overline{\mu}}\overline{\omega}_{0}=\overline{\rho}\left(\overline{\omega_{0}}+\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}+\frac{\overline{\mu}^{2}}{2}\overline{\omega}_{0}+\frac{\overline{\mu}^{3}}{6}\overline{\omega}_{0}\right),

where
\overline{\rho}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
and
\overline{\mu}\in\Omega^{1,-1}(M)
, and
\mu\omega_{0}
should be interpreted as extension of contraction to forms with values in vector fields. An expression for the generating function is then
[
34
]

(66)

f(A^{3,0},A^{2,1},\overline{\rho},\overline{\mu})=\\
=\int_{M}\overline{\rho}(A^{3,0}\overline{\omega}_{0}+A^{2,1}\overline{\mu}\,\overline{\omega}_{0})+\overline{\rho}^{2}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}-\frac{\left\langle\left((A^{2,1}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\mu}^{2}\overline{\omega}_{0})^{\vee}\right)^{3}\right\rangle}{(A^{3,0})^{\vee}-\overline{\rho}\langle\overline{\mu}^{3}\rangle}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

The HJ action is then

(67)

\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma^{1,1}+\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}_{a}^{2,1}+\sigma^{0,2}(\partial\mathsf{A}_{a}^{2,1}+\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a})\\
+f\left(\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{2,0},\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{1,1}+\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0},\overline{\rho},\overline{\mu}\right).

To see the connection to the Kodaira–Spencer action functional introduced in
[
8
]
, take the following quantum state considered in
[
34
]
:

(68)

\psi_{\text{GS}}(\overline{\rho},\overline{\mu})=\delta(\overline{\mu})\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\overline{\rho}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

\psi_{\text{GS}}
is the quantization of the lagrangian
L_{\text{GS}}\subset\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{3}(M)
given by
\overline{\mu}=0,p_{\overline{\rho}}=\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}
(here
p_{\overline{\rho}}
is the canonical momentum coordinate associated to
\overline{\rho}
).
L_{\text{GS}}
has the following generalized generating function, with
\lambda\in\Omega^{-1,1}(M)
an auxiliary parameter:

(69)

S_{\text{GS}}(\overline{\mu},\overline{\rho};\lambda)=\int_{M}(\lambda\overline{\mu}+\overline{\rho})\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

Let
\mathrm{ev}\colon\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{3}(M)\nrightarrow\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{3}(M)
denote
39
39

39

We use the notations of equation (
124
) of Appendix
A.1.4
for canonical relations.
the Chern–Simons evolution relation and interpret
L_{\text{GS}}
as a canonical relation
\{pt\}\nrightarrow\Omega^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. We want to compute the composition
40
40

40

The relation
\mathrm{ev}
goes from
A^{+,\mathrm{l}}
to
A^{-,\mathrm{nl}}
, in the composition we require the transpose relation
\mathrm{ev}^{T}
. This changes the sign of the generating function: the generalized generating function of
\mathrm{ev}^{T}
is
-\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
.

L\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathrm{ev}^{T}\circ L_{\text{GS}}\colon\{pt\}\nrightarrow\Omega^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
. This, following Remark
A.37
, has the generalized generating function
S=-\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}+S_{\text{GS}}
, where
\overline{\mu},\overline{\rho}
now become auxiliary fields. We can immediately solve the constraint
\overline{\mu}=0
to obtain a new generalized generating function for
L
,:

(70)

S^{\prime}=\left[-\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}+S_{\text{GS}}\right]_{\overline{\mu}=0}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma^{1,1}-\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}_{a}^{2,1}-\int_{M}\sigma^{0,2}(\partial\mathsf{A}_{a}^{2,1}+\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a})\\
-\int_{M}\overline{\rho}_{b}(\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{2,0}-\omega_{0})\overline{\omega}_{0}+\int_{M}\frac{\langle((\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{1,1}+\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle}{(\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{2,0})^{\vee}}\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

We now proceed with solving the constraint equations by setting derivatives with respect to auxiliary fields to zero. The equation
\delta S^{\prime}/\delta\overline{\rho}=0
is

(71)

-\frac{\delta S^{\prime}}{\delta\overline{\rho}}=\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{2,0}-\omega_{0}=0

which implies
\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}=-\bar{\partial}\partial\sigma^{2,0}.

The equation
\delta S^{\prime}/\delta\sigma^{0,2}=0
then gives

(72)

-\frac{\delta S^{\prime}}{\delta\sigma^{0,2}}=\partial\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}=\partial(\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0})=0.

Evaluating
S^{\prime}
on the set given by those constraints we obtain

S^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma^{1,1}-\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}_{a}^{2,1}+\int_{M}\langle((\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{1,1}+\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}.

By (
72
) and Hodge decomposition for
\partial
, there are a unique harmonic
(2,1)
-form
x
and a
(1,1)
-form
\beta
such that
x+\partial\beta=\mathsf{A}_{a}^{2,1}+\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0}
. Letting
b=\sigma^{1,1}+\beta
, we have
\bar{\partial}\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}=\bar{\partial}\partial\beta=\partial\bar{\partial}(\sigma^{1,1}-b)
. Plugging this into
S^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain

(73)

S^{\prime\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma^{1,1}-\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma^{1,1}+\int_{M}\sigma^{1,1}\partial\bar{\partial}b+\frac{1}{6}\langle x+\partial b,x+\partial b,x+\partial b\rangle.

After imposing the constraint

\frac{\delta S^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\delta\sigma^{1,1}}=0=-\partial\bar{\partial}\sigma^{1,1}+\partial\bar{\partial}b,

the action becomes

(74)

S_{\text{KS}}(x,b)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}b\partial\bar{\partial}b+\frac{1}{6}\langle x+\partial b,x+\partial b,x+\partial b\rangle,

the action functional for Kodaira–Spencer gravity defined in
[
8
]
.
For any
A\in\Omega^{2,1}(M)
we have

\frac{\delta}{\delta A}\langle(A^{\vee})^{3}\rangle\omega_{0}\overline{\omega}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}A\circ A\in\Omega^{1,2}.

Thus, the equation for
b
finally becomes

(75)

\partial\bar{\partial}b+\frac{1}{2}\partial(x+\partial b)\circ(x+\partial b)=0,

the Kodaira–Spencer equation for
A=x+\partial b
.

Finally, we can characterize the lagrangian
L
as follows:

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\omega_{0}
be a reference holomorphic 3-form on a compact Calabi–Yau manifold
M
. Then the composition
L=\mathrm{ev}^{T}\circ L_{\text{GS}}\subset\Omega^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
of the Chern–Simons evolution relation
\mathrm{ev}
on
M\times I
and the lagrangian
L_{\text{GS}}
defined by (
69
) is given by complex 3-forms with decomposition
A=A^{3,0}+A^{2,1}+A^{1,2}+A^{0,3}
satisfying

(76a)

\displaystyle A^{3,0}

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}-\partial\sigma^{2,0},

(76b)

\displaystyle A^{2,1}

\displaystyle=x+\partial b-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0}-\partial\sigma^{1,1},

(76c)

\displaystyle A^{1,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(x+\partial b)\circ(x+\partial b)-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{1,1}-\partial\sigma^{0,2},

(76d)

\displaystyle A^{0,3}

\displaystyle=c\overline{\omega}_{0}-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{0,2},

where
x
is a harmonic
(2,1)
form and
b\in\Omega^{1,1}
such that
x+\partial b
satisfies the Kodaira–Spencer equation in the form (
75
),
c\in\mathbb{C}
is a constant and
\sigma=\sigma^{2,0}+\sigma^{1,1}+\sigma^{0,2}\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is any 2-form.

Proof.

Equation (
76a
) is (
71
). Equation (
76b
) follows from (
72
) and the definition of
b
. Equation (
76c
) follows from
A^{1,2}=\delta S^{\prime}/\delta A^{2,1}
together with the constraint equation (
71
). Finally, we have

A^{0,3}=\frac{\delta S^{\prime}}{\delta A^{3,0}}=-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{0,2}-\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}-\frac{\langle((\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{1,1}-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle}{((\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{2,0})^{\vee})^{2}}\overline{\omega}_{0},

and again the denominator is 1 by (
71
). Now, the equation

0=\frac{\delta S^{\prime}}{\delta\sigma^{2,0}}=\partial\overline{\rho}\,\overline{\omega}_{0}+\partial\frac{\langle((\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{1,1}-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle}{((\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{2,0})^{\vee})^{2}}\overline{\omega}_{0}\\
+\bar{\partial}\frac{(\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{1,1}-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0})\circ(\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{1,1}-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0})}{((\mathsf{A}^{3,0}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{2,0}))^{2}}\overline{\omega}_{0}

implies, again using (
71
),

\bar{\partial}\partial(\overline{\rho}+\langle((\mathsf{A}^{2,1}_{a}+\partial\sigma^{1,1}-\bar{\partial}\sigma^{2,0})^{\vee})^{3}\rangle)\overline{\omega}_{0}=0,

which means that the expression in brackets is constant.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

It follows from the general discussion of Appendix
A
that
L
is isotropic, but one can also check it directly. To this end, compute the restriction of the symplectic form
\int_{M}\delta A^{3,0}\wedge\delta A^{0,3}+\delta A^{2,1}\wedge\delta A^{1,2}
to
L
. Denoting
B=x+\partial b
, we get

\displaystyle\delta A^{3,0}\wedge\delta A^{0,3}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\partial\bar{\partial}\delta\sigma^{2,0}\delta\sigma^{0,2}

\displaystyle\delta A^{2,1}\wedge\delta A^{1,2}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\delta\sigma^{1,1}\wedge\delta(\bar{\partial}B+\frac{1}{2}B\circ B)+\int_{M}\bar{\partial}\partial\delta\sigma^{2,0}\delta\sigma^{0,2}

(other terms cancel due to
\bar{\partial}^{2}=\partial^{2}=0
or integration by parts).
The terms containing
\sigma^{0,2}\sigma^{2,0}
cancel, while the remaining term is zero by (
75
).

Remark 7.5
.

Given a harmonic
(2,1)
-form
x
, solutions
b\in\Omega^{1,1}(M)
of the Kodaira–Spencer equation are determined only up to a
\partial
-exact term. However, for fixed
x
one can find a unique solution
b_{x}
by demanding that
\bar{\partial}^{*}b_{x}=0
, see
[
47
]
or
[
8
]
, where
\partial^{*}
denotes the formal adjoint of
\partial
with respect to the Kähler metric. Let
\underline{L}=\{(\omega_{0},x+\partial b_{x},\frac{1}{2}(x+\partial b_{x})\circ(x+\partial b_{x}),c\overline{\omega}_{0})\}
with notation as in Propostion
7.3
.
We can then rewrite Proposition
7.3
as saying that

(77)

L=\underline{L}+d\Omega^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)

(
\underline{L}
is a submanifold of
\Omega^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
but not a subspace).
At any point
(y,d\sigma)\in L
, the tangent space is
T_{y}\underline{L}\oplus d\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}(M)
. For
y=(\omega_{0},x+\partial b_{x}+\frac{1}{2}(x+\partial b_{x})\circ(x+\partial b_{x}),c\overline{\omega}_{0})
, the tangent space is

T_{y}\underline{L}=\{(0,\dot{x},x\circ\dot{x},\dot{c}),\dot{x}\in H^{2,1}(M),\dot{c}\in\mathbb{C}\}.

Thus,
L
is actually split lagrangian, with isotropic complement given by
L^{\prime}=H^{3,0}(M)\oplus H^{1,2}(M)\oplus d^{*}\Omega^{4}_{\mathbb{C}}(M).

8.
BFV, AKSZ and BV

The degenerate action (
18
) we have been focusing on so far can be put into BV form, which is suitable for the perturbative quantization (which we will consider in the next sections).

The first step consists in resolving the constraint
manifold
C=\{(p,q)\ |\ H_{\alpha}(p,q)=0\ \forall\alpha\}

in terms of the BFV formalism
[
6
,
30
]
. For this we introduce new odd variables
c^{\alpha}
, one for each constraint,
of ghost number
1
and their momenta
b_{\alpha}
, odd and of ghost number
-1
;
41
41

41

Here odd means a Grassmann variable, whereas even refers to an ordinary variable; ghost number is an additional degree which is helpful for bookkeeping. The original variables
p
and
q
are even and are assigned ghost number zero.
i.e., we extend the symplectic structure to the BFV symplectic structure

\omega_{\text{BFV}}=\mathrm{d}p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}+\mathrm{d}b_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}.

The BFV action is then defined as the odd function of ghost number
1

S_{\text{BFV}}(p,q,b,c)=c^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)+\cdots,

where the dots denote terms in the ghost momenta
b_{\alpha}
such that the Poisson bracket of
S_{\text{BFV}}
with itself vanishes (this is called the classical master equation). The BFV action exists and is uniquely defined up to symplectomorphisms
[
6
,
30
,
46
]
. Note that the linear term in the
b
s depends on the structure functions,

S_{\text{BFV}}(p,q,b,c)=c^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}(p,q)\,b_{\gamma}c^{\alpha}c^{\beta}+\cdots,

and that the higher-order terms are not needed in the Lie algebra case (in which the
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
s are constant).

The next step is the AKSZ construction
[
2
]
, which in this special case takes the following very simple form. We consider the replacements
42
42

42

The “weird” choice of signs is purely conventional. It is done in such a way that
i)
the classical part of the BV action
S_{\text{BV}}
introduced below agrees with (
18
), and
ii)
the BV form
\omega_{\text{BV}}
introduced a bit later has the usual form.

p_{i}\to p_{i}+q^{+}_{i},\quad q^{i}\to q^{i}-p_{+}^{i},\quad c^{\alpha}\to c^{\alpha}-e^{\alpha},\quad b_{\alpha}\to-e^{+}_{\alpha}+c^{+}_{\alpha},

where, in each assignment, the first term on the right hand side is a function on
[t_{a},t_{b}]
with the same Grassmann parity and ghost number as the variable on the left hand side, whereas the second term is a
1
-form on
[t_{a},t_{b}]
with opposite Grassmann parity and with ghost number reduced by
1
. Note, in particular, that we recover the even
1
-forms
e^{\alpha}
of ghost number zero. With this notation we define the BV action as

S_{\text{BV}}[p,q,e,c,p_{+},q^{+},e^{+},c^{+}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\\
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}((p_{i}+q^{+}_{i})\mathrm{d}(q^{i}-p_{+}^{i})+(-e^{+}_{\alpha}+c^{+}_{\alpha})\mathrm{d}(c^{\alpha}-e^{\alpha})+S_{\text{BFV}}(p+q^{+},q-p^{+},-e^{+}+c^{+},c-e))\\
=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-e^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}+S_{\text{BFV}}(p+q^{+},q-p^{+},-e^{+}+c^{+},c-e)),

where only the one-form components get integrated.

Example 8.1
.

In the main cases of interest for this paper, the structure functions
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
are constant. We then get

\displaystyle S_{\text{BFV}}(p,q,b,c)

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,b_{\gamma}c^{\alpha}c^{\beta},

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\text{BV}}[p,q,e,c,p_{+},q^{+},e^{+},c^{+}]&=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\bigg(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-e^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)\\
&\phantom{=}+c^{\alpha}\left(q^{+}_{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial p_{i}}(p,q)-p_{+}^{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial q^{i}}(p,q)\right)\\
&\phantom{=}-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,c^{+}_{\gamma}c^{\alpha}c^{\beta}+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,e^{+}_{\gamma}c^{\alpha}e^{\beta}\bigg).\end{split}

These are the BFV and BV actions that we are going to consider in the next sections.

Let us return to the general case for the remaining part of this section.
The BV action satisfies the following properties:

(1)

It is even and of ghost number zero.

(2)

The terms independent of the antifields (i.e., the fields with a
+
label) are the action (
18
).

(3)

If we ignore boundary terms (or impose periodic boundary conditions for all fields), its Poisson bracket with itself, with respect to the odd symplectic
43
43

43

The nondegeneracy of
\omega_{\text{BV}}
is actually not needed for classical considerations. At the quantum level only a partial nondegeneracy is actually needed, namely the one along the directions for which one considers integration; see more on this in Section
9.2.3
.
form of ghost number
-1

\omega_{\text{BV}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}(\delta q^{+}_{i}\delta q^{i}+\delta p_{+}^{i}\delta p_{i}+\delta e^{+}_{\alpha}\delta e^{\alpha}+\delta c^{+}_{\alpha}\delta c^{\alpha}),

vanishes.

This is the starting point for the BV quantization.

Remark 8.2

(Chern–Simons)
.

If we apply this construction to the Chern–Simons case decribed in the previous section, we get the BV formalism for Chern–Simons theory in
[t_{a},t_{b}]\times\Sigma
. We will return to this in
the
forthcoming paper
[
21
]
.

Remark 8.3
.

The case where in addition to constraints there is also a nontrivial hamiltonian
H
can be treated as at the beginning of Section
5
,
as originally
proposed in
[
24
]
, or, more conceptually, as in Section
5.1
.

9.
An outline of elements of BV-BFV quantization

The BV and BFV structures discussed in Section
8
can be put together, and this may be used towards quantization
[
17
,
19
]
. We briefly review this story in the case at hand assuming a finite-dimensional target. The case of infinite-dimensional targets discussed in Section
7
will be addressed in the forthcoming paper
[
21
]
.

9.1.
The classical BV-BFV setting

As described in Section
8
we have a space
\mathcal{F}
of BV fields
(p,q,e,c,p_{+},q^{+},e^{+},c^{+})
associated to the bulk
[t_{a},t_{b}]
and a space of BFV variables
(p,q,b,c)
associated to each boundary point. As the boundary of
[t_{a},t_{b}]
actually consists of two endpoints (with opposite orientation), we define the space
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
of BFV variables by doubling the
(p,q,b,c)
s:

\mathcal{F}^{\partial}\ni(p^{a},q_{a},b^{a},c_{a},p^{b},q_{b},b^{b},c_{b}).

The BFV symplectic form and the BFV action are now

\omega_{\text{BFV}}=\mathrm{d}p_{i}^{a}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{a}+\mathrm{d}b_{\alpha}^{a}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{a}-\mathrm{d}p_{i}^{b}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{b}-\mathrm{d}b_{\alpha}^{b}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{b}

and

\begin{split}S_{\text{BFV}}&=c^{\alpha}_{a}H_{\alpha}(p^{a},q_{a})-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}(p^{a},q_{a})\,b_{\gamma}^{a}c^{\alpha}_{a}c^{\beta}_{a}+\cdots\\
&\phantom{\ }-c^{\alpha}_{b}H_{\alpha}(p^{b},q_{b})+\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}(p^{b},q_{b})\,b_{\gamma}^{b}c^{\alpha}_{b}c^{\beta}_{b}-\cdots.\end{split}

The bulk setting
(\mathcal{F},S_{\text{BV}},\omega_{\text{BV}})
and the boundary BFV theory
(\mathcal{F}^{\partial},S_{\text{BFV}},\omega_{\text{BFV}})
are related as follows:

(1)

We have a surjective submersion
\pi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
given by

\displaystyle p^{b}

\displaystyle=p(t_{b})

\displaystyle q_{b}

\displaystyle=q(t_{b})

\displaystyle b^{b}

\displaystyle=-e^{+}(t_{b})

\displaystyle c_{b}

\displaystyle=c(t_{b})

\displaystyle p^{a}

\displaystyle=p(t_{a})

\displaystyle q_{a}

\displaystyle=q(t_{a})

\displaystyle b^{a}

\displaystyle=-e^{+}(t_{a})

\displaystyle c_{a}

\displaystyle=c(t_{a})

(2)

If we denote by
Q_{\text{BV}}
the hamiltonian vector field of
S_{\text{BV}}
with respect to
\omega_{\text{BV}}
