, obtained by ignoring boundary terms (or by imposing periodic boundary conditions for all fields), and by
Q_{\text{BFV}}
the hamiltonian vector field of
S_{\text{BFV}}
with respect to
\omega_{\text{BFV}}
we get

(78)

[Q_{\text{BV}},Q_{\text{BV}}]=0,\quad[Q_{\text{BFV}},Q_{\text{BFV}}]=0,\quad\mathrm{d}\pi(Q_{\text{BV}})=Q_{\text{BFV}},

where
\mathrm{d}\pi
denotes the differential of the map
\pi
(the last equation may be equivalently formulated, more algebraically, as the property that
\pi^{*}(Q_{\text{BFV}}h)=Q_{\text{BV}}\pi^{*}h
for every function
h
on
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
).

(3)

If we introduce the potential

\alpha_{\text{BFV}}=p_{i}^{a}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{a}+b_{\alpha}^{a}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{a}-p_{i}^{b}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{b}-b_{\alpha}^{b}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{b}

for the BFV form
\omega_{\text{BFV}}
, then
the modified classical master equation (mCME)

(79)

\iota_{Q_{\text{BV}}}\omega_{\text{BV}}=\delta S_{\text{BV}}+\pi^{*}\alpha_{\text{BFV}}

is satisfied. Here the variation
\delta
of the BV action is computed by taking the boundary terms into account (they are precisely compensated by
\pi^{*}\alpha_{\text{BFV}}
) and is regarded as producing a
1
-form on
\mathcal{F}
.

A simple consequence of the above, see
[
19
]
, is that we also have the equation

(80)

\frac{1}{2}\iota_{Q_{\text{BV}}}\iota_{Q_{\text{BV}}}\omega_{\text{BV}}=\pi^{*}S_{\text{BFV}},

where the left hand side may be read as the correct replacement for the otherwise ill-defined Poisson bracket, induced by
\omega_{\text{BV}}
, of
S_{\text{BV}}
with itself, whereas the right hand side yields the error boundary terms. This equation will play an important role in quantization.

Remark 9.1

(Changing the endpoint conditions)
.

As in the purely classical setting, see Remark
2.5
, we might want to use other variables, say
P
and
Q
, at the final endpoint via a generating function
f(q,Q)
. We can arrange for this by subtracting

f

from the BV action,

S_{\text{BV}}^{f}[p,q,e,c,p_{+},q^{+},e^{+},c^{+}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S_{\text{BV}}[p,q,e,c,p_{+},q^{+},e^{+},c^{+}]-f(q(t_{b}),Q(p(t_{b}),q(t_{b}))),

and at the same time adding
\mathrm{d}f
to the BFV potential,

\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{f}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\alpha_{\text{BFV}}+\mathrm{d}f(q_{b},Q(p^{b},q_{b}))=p_{i}^{a}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{a}+b_{\alpha}^{a}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{a}-P_{i}^{b}\mathrm{d}Q^{i}_{b}-b_{\alpha}^{b}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{b}.

This way we get the wished-for classical part of the symplectic form at the final endpoint, yet preserving the mCME (
79
), now in the form

\iota_{Q_{\text{BV}}}\omega_{\text{BV}}=\delta S_{\text{BV}}^{f}+\pi^{*}\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{f}.

Remark 9.2

(Changing the endpoint field and ghost conditions)
.

We might also want to exchange the role of
b
and
c
at the final endpoint. To do so, we consider the extended generating function

\tilde{f}(q,Q,c,C)=f(q,Q)+cC.

We then get

S_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}[p,q,e,c,p_{+},q^{+},e^{+},c^{+}]=S_{\text{BV}}^{f}[p,q,e,c,p_{+},q^{+},e^{+},c^{+}]+c_{\alpha}(t_{b})e^{+}(t_{b})

and

\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}=p_{i}^{a}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{a}+b_{\alpha}^{a}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{a}-P_{i}^{b}\mathrm{d}Q^{i}_{b}-c^{\alpha}_{b}\mathrm{d}b_{\alpha}^{b}.

The mCME (
79
) now reads

(81)

\iota_{Q_{\text{BV}}}\omega_{\text{BV}}=\delta S_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}+\pi^{*}\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}.

Remark 9.3
.

The induced equation (
80
) holds as it is, since it is independent of the choice of the
1
-form.

9.2.
The quantum BV-BFV setting

In this section, we give a very short overview of the BV-BFV quantization, tailored for the examples of this paper.
For a more general exposition, see
[
19
]
.

9.2.1.
Boundary polarization

For the quantum setting, we first have to choose a polarization in
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
. Concretely, in the case at hand, we
write
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
as
T^{*}\mathcal{B}
, for a suitable lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{B}
, with canonical symplectic form.
We require that the BFV
1
-form of (
79
) should be the canonical
1
-form of the cotangent bundle. This means that the choice of
\mathcal{B}
is related to the choice of boundary polarization. (Recall that,
on the initial endpoint, we choose
q_{a}
and
c_{a}
as coordinates on
\mathcal{B}
; on the final endpoint, we choose
Q_{b}
and
c_{b}
or

Q_{b}
and
b^{b}
—i.e., we work in the setting of Remark
9.1
or
9.2
, and we consider the corresponding
1
-form
\alpha^{f}_{\text{BFV}}
or
\alpha^{\tilde{f}}_{\text{BFV}}
.)

9.2.2.
Splitting

For simplicity, we assume the target (for the
(p,q)
fields) to be
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In this case
\mathcal{B}
is a (graded) vector space, and
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}=\mathcal{B}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{B}
. The crucial point is to write the space of bulk fields as

(82)

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{B},

where
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
is a suitable replacement (regularization) of the space of bulk fields, compatible with the boundary polarization by this splitting. Note that
\mathcal{B}^{*}
is now a subspace of
\mathcal{Y}
. We assume that equations
(
78
) and (
81
), and therefore also (
80
), are satisfied (now working on
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
instead of
\mathcal{F}
and with a suitable replacement
\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}
of
S_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}
).

Example 9.4
.

The
q
-field component of
\mathcal{B}
is
q_{a}
. As
q
-component of
\mathcal{Y}
we can then take the space

C^{\infty}_{0}([t_{a},t_{b}],\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni\hat{q}
of maps that vanish at
t_{a}
. If we define

q(t)=q_{a}+\hat{q}(t),

as we did in Remark
2.4
, then we see that the
q
-component of
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
is the space of smooth maps
[t_{a},t_{b}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, as in
\mathcal{F}
. If on the other hand we set

q(t)=\begin{cases}q_{a}&t=t_{a}\\
\hat{q}(t)&t>t_{a}\end{cases},

then we get, in
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
, maps with a possible discontinuity at
t_{a}
, unlike in
\mathcal{F}
. In this case, the quantum space of bulk fields
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
is slightly different from its classical counterpart
\mathcal{F}
. It turns out that, in general, these discontinuous extensions are more suitable for the BV-BFV formalism, as we explain below (see Section
11
and
12
for examples and also for the correct definition of
\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}
in these cases).

9.2.3.
Good splittings

Given a splitting (
82
), we decompose

Q_{\text{BV}}=Q_{\mathcal{Y}}+Q_{\mathcal{B}}

and

\omega_{\text{BV}}=\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}}+\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{B}}+\omega_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}

according to which (co)tangent space(s) the vector field/
2
-form belongs. We assume
\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}}
to be closed and (weakly) nondegenerate as a
2
-form on
\mathcal{Y}
. The mCME (
81
) now reads, in
\mathcal{Y}
- and
\mathcal{B}
-components,
44
44

44

We write the formulae with generating function
\tilde{f}
. Of course, they hold also with
f
instead.

\displaystyle\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{Y}}}\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}}+\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}

\displaystyle\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{Y}}}\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{B}}+\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\omega_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathcal{B}}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}+\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}},

where we have omitted writing
\pi^{*}
. Note that, by assumption,
\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}
only has
\mathcal{B}
-components.

We say that we have a good splitting if
\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{B}}=0
, for in this case
Q_{\mathcal{Y}}
is the hamiltonian vector field, with respect to
\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}}
, of
\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}
on
\mathcal{Y}
(with everything parametrized by
\mathcal{B}
). We assume from now on that we have a good splitting.
We denote by
(\ ,\ )_{\mathcal{Y}}
the Poisson bracket induced by
\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}}
, so we have

(\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}},\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}})_{\mathcal{Y}}=\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{Y}}}\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{Y}}}\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Y}}=\iota_{Q_{\text{BV}}}\iota_{Q_{\text{BV}}}\omega_{\text{BV}}-\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\omega_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}.

By the induced equation (
80
), we then get

(\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}},\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}})_{\mathcal{Y}}=2S_{BFV}-\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\omega_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}.

If the latter term depends only on the
T^{*}\mathcal{B}
variables, which we assume from now on, we may define a new function

S^{\partial}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S_{BFV}-\frac{1}{2}\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\iota_{Q_{\mathcal{B}}}\omega_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}

on
T^{*}\mathcal{B}
, and we have

(83)

(\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}},\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}})_{\mathcal{Y}}=2S^{\partial}.

Remark 9.5
.

Usually we do not have a good splitting. A way out, as also suggested in Remark 2.33 of
[
19
]
, is to consider a family of splittings such that there is a limit in which the theory exists and corresponds to a good splitting (but of a different space of fields). For instance, one can define a section
s_{\varepsilon}
of the short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{B}\to 0
by multiplying fields in
\mathcal{B}
by a smooth function equal to 1 close to the boundary and with support contained in a collar of length
\varepsilon
. We thus obtain a family of BV-BFV theories

\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}=\left(\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{B},(\iota\oplus s_{\varepsilon})^{*}\omega_{\text{BV}},(\iota\oplus s_{\varepsilon})^{*}S^{\tilde{f}}_{\text{BV}},(\iota\oplus s_{\varepsilon})^{*}Q_{\text{BV}},\pi\circ(\iota\oplus s_{\varepsilon})\right)

over
(\mathcal{F}^{\partial},S_{\text{BFV}},\omega_{BFV})

isomorphic to
(\mathcal{F},\omega_{\text{BV}},S^{\tilde{f}}_{\text{BV}},Q_{\text{BV}},\pi)

for
\varepsilon>0
. The limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
gives a well-defined theory
\mathcal{F}_{0}
on
\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{B}
(one just integrates by parts the terms containing
ds_{\varepsilon}
), but it does not arise from a well-defined section
s
- rather, it corresponds to the discontinuous extension in Example
9.4
.
45
45

45

In this theory the BV form is nondegenerate along
\mathcal{Y}
, but degenerate otherwise. This is all what is needed to define BV quantization.

This theory on
\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{B}
is then the replacement as in (
82
) which is suitable for quantization. The space
\tilde{F}
can be constructed by embedding
\mathcal{F}
in a larger space
\tilde{\tilde{F}}
where the limit
s_{0}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\tilde{\tilde{F}}
exists, and then we let
\tilde{F}=(\iota\oplus s_{0})(\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{B})
. For our purposes one can typically let
\tilde{\tilde{F}}
be smooth functions on the interval with a discontinuity at the endpoints.

9.2.4.
The modified quantum master equation

In the BV setting, one has a second-order differential operator
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}
, the BV Laplacian, acting on functions on
\mathcal{Y}

with the properties

\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}^{2}=0,\quad\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}(fg)=\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}(f)g\pm f\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}g\pm(f,g)_{\mathcal{Y}}.

In the finite-dimensional case, this operator is actually canonically defined on half-densisties
[
37
,
45
]
. It is then transferred to functions upon the choice of a nondegenerate,
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}
-closed, reference half-density. In the infinite-dimensional setting, the definition
of
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}
requires a regularization, which we presently assume to have. Moreover, for simplicity, we assume

\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}=0
. Therefore, we have

\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}=-\frac{1}{2\hbar^{2}}(\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}},\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}})_{\mathcal{Y}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}=-\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}S^{\partial}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}.

The final step consists in finding some operator
\Omega
on the space of functions on
\mathcal{B}
satisfying

(84)

\Omega\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}=S^{\partial}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}.

If we find such an operator, then we get the modified Quantum Master Equation (mQME)

(85)

(\Omega+\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}})\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}=0,

which is a quantization of (
81
).

Note that
\Omega
commutes with
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}
(the former acts on functions on
\mathcal{B}
, the latter on functions on
\mathcal{Y}
), so the operator

(86)

\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}+\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\Omega

squares to zero if and only if
\Omega
does so, which we are going to assume.
The reason for this normalization, in which we prefer to regard
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}
as a modification of
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}
by a contribution due to the boundary,
46
46

46

One might also as well argue that the coboundary operator should be defined as
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Omega+\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}
—i.e., as a modification of
\Omega
by a contribution due to the bulk—stressing that what matters more are the boundary fields, as the bulk fields will eventually be integrated out in the functional integration. One might also argue that, since, without boundary, one has

-\mathrm{i}\hbar\Delta\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}}\mathcal{O}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}}(Q_{\text{BV}}\mathcal{O}-\mathrm{i}\hbar\Delta)
, where on the right hand side we see the quantum modification of the BV operator
Q_{\text{BV}}
, then
-\mathrm{i}\hbar\Delta
is the correct coboundary operator in the bulk, so the correct coboundary operator in the presence of a boundary should be

-\mathrm{i}\hbar\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\Omega
.

will become clear in Section
9.2.7
, where we will address gauge fixing and gauge-fixing independence. The mQME can now be rewritten as

\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}=0.

There are two main settings for the construction of
\Omega
, which we describe in the next two sections. (In principle, there may be more choices available, but we are not aware of them.)

9.2.5.
Case I: No boundary symmetries

Suppose
Q^{\partial}=0
. This implies
Q_{\mathcal{B}}=0
and
S^{\partial}=S_{\text{BFV}}=0
.
In this case, every splitting is good and, moreover, we have
(\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}},\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}})_{\mathcal{Y}}=0
.
Therefore, we obtain the mQME by simply setting
\Omega=0
.

Note, in particular, that we might choose a splitting as in the first part of Example
9.4
for all fields, so that
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}
and
\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}=S_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}
. We may also choose, say for the
q
part, to split
q(t)=q_{0}(t)+\hat{q}(t)
, where
q_{0}
is the solution to the EL equations with the given boundary conditions. We then recover the usual procedure for dealing with a path integral without gauge symmetries, as in equation (
5
) of Remark
2.4
.

9.2.6.
Case II: Discontinuous splitting

A rather general procedure for dealing with the BV-BFV quantization, proposed in
[
19
]
, is based on a discontinuous splitting of the fields, as in the second part of Example
9.4
and in Remark
9.5
. In this case,
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}\not=\mathcal{F}
and
\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}\not=S_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}
. Moreover, the regularization of
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}
requires some care, so we will be rather sketchy here. The main advantage of such a choice is that
\omega_{\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{B}}=\omega_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}}=0
. This is then a good splitting in which
S^{\partial}=S_{\text{BFV}}
and

\delta_{\mathcal{B}}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}=-\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}.

Since
\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}
is by assumption the canonical
1
-form of the cotangent bundle

T^{*}\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{B}\ni(z^{*},z)
, we write
\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}=z^{*}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}z^{\mu}
. Therefore, the last equation becomes

\frac{\partial\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}{\partial z^{\mu}}=-z^{*}_{\mu}.

It then follows that, if we define
\Omega
to be the Schrödinger quantization of
S_{\text{BFV}}
,

(87)

\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=S_{\text{BFV}}\left(\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial z},z\right)

with all the derivatives to the right (standard ordering), then we get

\Omega\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}=S_{\text{BFV}}(z^{*},z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}},

which is (
84
) (recall that we now have
S^{\partial}=S_{\text{BFV}}
).

Remark 9.6
.

A general observation at this point is that
\Omega
is a quantization of
S_{\text{BFV}}
and that the classical master equation
\{S_{\text{BFV}},S_{\text{BFV}}\}=0
is the lowest-order condition for
\Omega^{2}=0
. Note that nothing guarantees that this condition is satisfied at all orders. This actually becomes a consistency condition for the theory. (See more, e.g., in Section
10
).

In this presentation, we treated
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}
rather formally. The correct argument requires a regularization and a careful analysis of the Feynman diagrams, which we present in Appendix
B
, and relies on certain assumptions.

The simplest case when
the above argument turns out to be correct is when the initial and final polarizations are related by a linear transformation (i.e.,
f(q,Q)
is linear in the
q
s and in the
Q
s). This is the setting of
[
19
]
, which we will use in Section
10
(in the particular case
f(q,Q)=Q_{i}q^{i}
as, e.g., in Example
2.8
).

For a more general
f
,
a compatibility conditions between the constraints and
f
is required, see Assumption
12.1
or
B.1
.
In particular,
the above procedure actually works for a system as in Section
4
with the conditions that
i)
all the hamiltonians
H_{\alpha}
are linear in the
p
variables and
ii)
all the hamiltonians
\tilde{H}_{\alpha}
are linear in the
P
variables, where

\tilde{H}_{\alpha}(P,Q)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=H_{\alpha}(p(P,Q),q(P,Q))
, and we use the symplectomorphism induced by
f
(no matter how nonlinear it is; what saves the game here is the linearity of the hamiltonians in the momenta).
Note that this is not an artificial, quirky situation, but it actually occurs in important examples, the main one (although with infinite-dimensional target) being described in Section
7.6
.

9.2.7.
The BV pushforward

One of the properties of the BV formalism is the following. Assume we can write
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{Y}_{2}
as a sum of symplectic subspaces. If
\mathcal{L}
is a lagrangian submanifold of
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
and
\psi
is in the kernel of
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, then the integral
\psi_{1}
of
\psi
over
\mathcal{L}
is in the kernel of
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}+\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\Omega
; moreover, if we deform
\mathcal{L}
, the result
\psi_{1}
will change by a
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}
-exact term.

By the mQME (
85
),
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}}
is
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-closed, so we may apply this procedure,
which is known as the BV pushforward. The result of the pushforward can again be recast in the form
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S_{1}}
, where
S_{1}
is a formal power series in
\hbar
.
The choice of
\mathcal{L}
is called a gauge fixing, and the last property in the previous paragraph is referred to as gauge-fixing independence. With the normalization in (
86
), the argument of

\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}
under a change of gauge fixing is a formal power series in
\hbar
times
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}S_{1}}
, which is appropriate in perturbation theory.

We may, in particular, choose
\mathcal{Y}_{2}=\mathcal{Y}
(assuming we can find a lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
on which the integral of
\psi
converges). In this case,
\psi_{1}
will be a function on
\mathcal{B}
satisfying
\Omega\psi_{1}=0
. Note that in this
case
\Delta^{\Omega}
is just
\Omega/\hbar^{2}
.

Two remarks are here in order. The first is that this procedure is more invariantly defined in terms of half-densitites instead of functions. The second is that there might be lagrangian submanifolds
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
that are not deformations of one another (at least through a path of lagrangian submanifolds on which the integral of
\psi
converges); in this case, the two results of integration might not be
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}
-cohomologous; see, e.g., Example
9.8
below.

Remark 9.7
.

The BV pushforward may of course be iterated. The setting is then the following. We have a fixed space
\mathcal{B}
with a fixed coboundary operator
\Omega
acting on its functions. We then have, for some ordered indices
k
, an odd symplectic space
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
and a
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}}
-closed function
\psi_{k}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\times\mathcal{B}
. The
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}_{k+1}}
-closed function
\psi_{k+1}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{k+1}\times\mathcal{B}
is obtained by BV-pushforward.
On the new spaces
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\times\mathcal{B}
one can recover some derived structures reminiscent of the original one on
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{B}
; see
[
19
, Remark 2.17]
and
[
20
, Proposition 9.1]
.

Example 9.8
.

As a toy example (actually a BV-BFV quantization of Example
3.2
with
n=1
,
v=0
and
w=1
after a first BV pushforward),
we consider
\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}[1]\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}[1]\ni(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b})
,

\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}[-1]\ni(T,T^{+})
,
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial T^{+}\partial T}
, and

\Omega=c_{a}q_{a}-\mathrm{i}\hbar c_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{b}}.

The state

\psi_{1}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b},T,T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(p^{b}q_{a}+Tq_{a}+T^{+}(c_{a}-c_{b}))}

is
\Delta^{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}
-closed. If we consider the lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}=\{T^{+}=0\}
of
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
, we get
47
47

47

We conventionally normalize the measures
\mathrm{d}T
and
\mathrm{d}T^{+}
in order to get nicer looking formulae.

\psi_{2}^{\mathcal{L}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b})=\int_{\mathcal{L}}\psi_{1}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b},T,T^{+})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}T}{2\pi\hbar}\psi_{1}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b},T,0)=\delta(q_{a}),

which is clearly
\Omega
-closed. If, on the other hand, we consider the lagrangian submanifold
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}=\{T=0\}
,
which cannot be deformed to
\mathcal{L}
, we get

\begin{split}\psi_{2}^{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b})&=\int_{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}\psi_{1}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b},T,T^{+})=\int\mathrm{i}\hbar\,{\mathrm{d}T^{+}}\psi_{1}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b},0,T^{+})\\
&=(c_{a}-c_{b})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}q_{a}}=\delta(c_{a}-c_{b})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}q_{a}},\end{split}

which is also
\Omega
-closed but certainly not
\Omega
-cohomologous to
\psi_{2}^{\mathcal{L}}
(the two have different ghost numbers).

9.2.8.
Composition

The space
\mathcal{B}
we consider is actually a product
\mathcal{B}_{a}\times\mathcal{B}_{b}
, with
\mathcal{B}_{a}
having coordinates
q_{a}
and
c_{a}
and
with
\mathcal{B}_{b}
having coordinates
Q_{b}
and
c_{b}
or

Q_{b}
and
b^{b}
. We assume that
\Omega
is local, i.e.,
\Omega=\Omega_{a}-\Omega_{b}
, where
each summand is an operator on the space of functions of the corresponding space. In addition, we assume
\Omega_{a}
and
\Omega_{b}
to be self-adjoint operators on functions on
\mathcal{B}_{a}
and
\mathcal{B}_{b}
, respectively.
48
48

48

This is defined in terms of some measure on
\mathcal{B}_{a}
. A more invariant formulation regards the states as half-densities, so this choice of measure is not necessary.

For instance, see Example
9.8
where we have
\Omega_{a}=c_{a}q_{a}
and
\Omega_{b}=\mathrm{i}\hbar c_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{b}}
. We will see more examples in
Sections
10
and
12.1
.

Suppose we have a
\Delta^{\Omega_{a}-\Omega_{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-closed state
\psi
on
\mathcal{B}_{a}\times\mathcal{B}_{b}\times\mathcal{Y}
and a

\Delta^{\Omega_{b}}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}}
-closed state
\psi_{b}
on
\mathcal{B}_{b}\times\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
. We can then pair
\psi
with
\psi_{b}
integrating over
\mathcal{B}_{b}
(we assume this integration to converge). Then
49
49

49

In the composition integral we assume that there is a measure on
\mathcal{B}_{b}
or that the states are half-densities, see footnote
48
.

\psi_{a}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{b}}\psi\psi_{b}

is a
\Delta^{\Omega_{a}}_{\mathcal{Y}\times\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}}
-closed state on
\mathcal{B}_{a}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
. A further reduction by BV pushforward, as in Section
9.2.7
, is of course possible if we have
\mathcal{Y}\times\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathcal{Y}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}_{2}
.

Example 9.9
.

Take
\psi=\psi_{1}
as in Example
9.8
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
to be a point. The ghost-number-one state

\psi_{b}(p^{b},c_{b})=\phi(p^{b})\delta(c_{b})=\phi(p^{b})c_{b},

where
\phi
is an arbitary function, is obviously
\Omega_{b}
-closed. After pairing, it yields, up to a multiplicative constant that depends on the choice of measure,

\psi_{a}(q_{a},c_{a},T,T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(Tq_{a}+T^{+}c_{a})}\hat{\phi}(q_{a}),

where
\hat{\phi}
is the Fourier transform of
\phi
. Note that

-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}\psi_{a}=c_{a}q_{a}\psi_{a}=\Omega_{a}\psi_{a},

so
\psi_{a}
is
\Delta^{\Omega_{a}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}
-closed. We may now integrate over the lagrangian submanifolds
\mathcal{L}=\{T^{+}=0\}
or
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}=\{T=0\}
getting

\psi_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}(q_{a},c_{a})=\delta(q_{a})\hat{\phi}(0)\quad\text{ and }\quad\psi_{a}^{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}(q_{a},c_{a})=\delta(c_{a})\hat{\phi}(q_{a}),

which are clearly
\Omega_{a}
-closed. If, on the other hand, we consider the ghost-number-zero state

\tilde{\psi}_{b}(p^{b},c_{b})=1,

which is obviously
\Omega_{b}
-closed, then we get, again up to a multiplicative constant,

\tilde{\psi}_{a}(q_{a},c_{a},T,T^{+})=\delta(q_{a})\delta(T^{+})=\delta(q_{a})T^{+},

which is obviously
\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}
- and
\Omega_{a}
-closed, and therefore
\Delta^{\Omega_{a}}_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}
-closed.
By a further BV pushforward as above, we get

\tilde{\psi}_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}(q_{a},c_{a})=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\tilde{\psi}_{a}^{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}(q_{a},c_{a})=\delta(q_{a}),

which are clearly
\Omega_{a}
-closed.

Remark 9.10
.

A generalization of the above is when we have a
\Delta^{\Omega_{a}-\Omega_{b}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-closed state
\psi
on
\mathcal{B}_{a}\times\mathcal{B}_{b}\times\mathcal{Y}
and a

\Delta^{\Omega_{b}-\Omega_{b^{\prime}}}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}}
-closed state
\tilde{\psi}
on
\mathcal{B}_{b}\times\mathcal{B}_{b^{\prime}}\times\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
. We can then pair them on
\mathcal{B}_{b}
, getting a
\Delta^{\Omega_{a}-\Omega_{b^{\prime}}}_{\mathcal{Y}\times\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}}
-closed state

\hat{\psi}=\int_{\mathcal{B}_{b}}\psi\tilde{\psi}

on
\mathcal{B}_{a}\times\mathcal{B}_{b^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
(which could possibly be further reduced by BV pushforwards).
If we interpret the above states
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
as evolution operators corresponding to the intervals

[t_{a},t_{b}]
and
[t_{b},t_{b^{\prime}}]
, respectively, then
\hat{\psi}
can be interpreted as the corresponding evolution operator on
[t_{a},t_{b^{\prime}}]
.

10.
BV-BFV boundary structures for linear polarizations

In this section we prepare for the application of the quantum BV-BFV formalism to the case of one-dimensional systems with constraints, whose classical treatment was the focus of Sections
2
to
6
, with linear polarizations. We discuss here only the quantization of the boundary structure, whereas the quantization of the bulk will be discussed in Section
11
.
We defer the case of nonlinear polarizations to Section
12
and the case of infinite-dimensional targets, discussed at the classical level in Section
7
, to
[
21
]
. In many places, we also make use of deformation quantization, for which we refer to the valuable review
[
27
]
and references therein.

10.1.
Three cases

We will work out some representative cases of linear polarizations. Namely,
we assume that the coordinates in
\mathcal{B}_{b}
are either

Case I:

(p^{b},c_{b})
, or

Case II:

(p^{b},b^{b})
,

with
(q_{a},c_{a})
as the coordinates in
\mathcal{B}_{a}
(see Remark
9.2
).
For completeness, and for later applications, we will also consider:

Case III:

(q_{a},b^{a})
as the coordinates in
\mathcal{B}_{a}
and
(p^{b},c_{b})
as the coordinates in
\mathcal{B}_{b}
.

For the quantization we will consider a discontinuous splitting of the bulk fields (see Example
9.4
).

Finally, we confine ourselves to the case when the structure functions are constant (see Example
8.1
); we then have

\displaystyle S_{\text{BFV}}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}_{a}H_{\alpha}(p^{a},q_{a})-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,b_{\gamma}^{a}c^{\alpha}_{a}c^{\beta}_{a}-c^{\alpha}_{b}H_{\alpha}(p^{b},q_{b})+\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,b_{\gamma}^{b}c^{\alpha}_{b}c^{\beta}_{b},

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\text{BV}}&=\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\bigg(p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}-e^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}-e^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(p,q)+c^{\alpha}\left(q^{+}_{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial p_{i}}(p,q){-}p_{+}^{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial q^{i}}(p,q)\right)\\
&\phantom{=}-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,c^{+}_{\gamma}c^{\alpha}c^{\beta}+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,e^{+}_{\gamma}c^{\alpha}e^{\beta}\bigg).\end{split}

Remark 10.1

(Unimodularity)
.

The BV action satisfies the classical master equation. To ensure
\Delta S_{\text{BV}}=0
, although at a formal level, we assume the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, of which the
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
s are the structure constants, to be unimodular: i.e.,
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\alpha}=0
.

Remark 10.2

(The coboundary operator)
.

In all the cases we consider, the operator
\Omega
is of the form
\Omega_{a}-\Omega_{b}
with
\Omega_{a}
and
\Omega_{b}
acting on different spaces, so
\Omega^{2}=\Omega_{a}^{2}+\Omega_{b}^{2}
and we have
\Omega^{2}=0
if and only if
\Omega_{a}^{2}=0
and
\Omega_{b}^{2}=0
.

10.2.
The boundary structure in Case I

We use
\tilde{f}=f=p_{i}q^{i}
, so

\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}=\alpha_{\text{BFV}}+\mathrm{d}f(q_{b},p^{b})=p_{i}^{a}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{a}+b_{\alpha}^{a}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{a}+q_{b}^{i}\mathrm{d}p^{b}_{i}-b_{\alpha}^{b}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{b}.

We now proceed as in Section
9.2.6
.
If we write
\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}=z^{*}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}z^{\mu}
, we get

p^{a}=q_{a}^{*},\quad b^{a}=c_{a}^{*},\quad q_{b}=(p^{b})^{*},\quad b^{b}=-c_{b}^{*}.

The recipe (
87
) for constructing
\Omega
is to replace
z^{*}_{\mu}
with
\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{\mu}}
in

S_{\text{BFV}}
. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\Omega_{a}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}_{a}H_{\alpha}\left(\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{a}},q_{a}\right)-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,c^{\alpha}_{a}c^{\beta}_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial c^{\gamma}_{a}},

\displaystyle\Omega_{b}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}_{b}H_{\alpha}\left(p^{b},\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{b}}\right)+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,c^{\alpha}_{b}c^{\beta}_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial c^{\gamma}_{b}}.

For example, if the index sets are both
\{1\}
, so
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}=0
, and we take
H_{1}(p,q)=q
, we get the
\Omega
of Example
9.8
.

We can easily see that
\Omega^{2}=0
if and only if we have, at both endpoints, the following quantization of (
19
):

(88)

\left[\widehat{H}_{\alpha},\widehat{H}_{\beta}\right]=\mathrm{i}\hbar f^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}\widehat{H}_{\gamma}.

Here
\widehat{H}_{\alpha}
denotes the corresponding quantization of
H_{\alpha}
at the given endpoint. Equivalently, we may reformulate this as

(89)

[H_{\alpha},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}H_{\beta}]=\mathrm{i}\hbar f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}H_{\gamma},

where
[\phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}\psi]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\phi\star\psi-\psi\star\phi
denotes the commutator with respect to either star product induced from the above quantization (if the condition is satisfied for one star product, it is automatically satisfied also for the other). Namely, at the initial endpoint we have

(90)

\phi\star\psi(p,q)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\tilde{q}^{i}\partial\tilde{p}_{i}}}\phi(\tilde{p},q)\psi(p,\tilde{q})\Big|_{\tilde{p}=p,\ \tilde{q}=q},

whereas at the final endpoint we have

(91)

\phi\star\psi(p,q)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\tilde{q}^{i}\partial\tilde{p}_{i}}}\phi(p,\tilde{q})\psi(\tilde{p},q)\Big|_{\tilde{p}=p,\ \tilde{q}=q}.

In principle, if (
89
) is not satisfied on the nose,
one may look for
\hbar
-deformations of the
H_{\alpha}
s that satisfy it. However, there are in general obstructions to do it. For the purposes of the present paper the following remark is important.

Remark 10.3
.

In both the linear case of Example
4.3
and the biaffine case of Example
4.6
, condition (
89
) is automatically satisfied, so we have
\Omega^{2}=0
.

Digression 10.4
.

Lemma 4.14 of
[
19
]
states that
\Omega^{2}=0
in case of
BF
-like theories (like the theories at hand). The regularization was assumed there to be in terms of the
Fulton–MacPherson–Axelrod–Singer
compactified configurations spaces
[
31
,
4
]
(which is also the case we will consider in the next sections). What was also implicitly assumed there, although unfortunately not made clear, was that the bulk dimension should be larger than
1
.
50
50

50

The proof of that lemma relied in particular on the contribution of the principal faces—i.e., collapses of two points in the bulk—of the boundary of compactified configurations spaces. In
d
dimensions, such faces have dimension
d-1
, whereas propagators in
BF
-like theories are
(d-1)
-forms. For
d>1
, there must then be exactly one propagator between the two collapsing points; the sum over all these contributions is zero as a consequence of the classical master equation. For
d=1
, however, there is no bound on the number of propagators between two collapsing points.
For this reason that lemma does not apply to the present case.
We will briefly discuss in Appendix
B
the correct version of the lemma in the case at hand.
Note that Lemma 4.14 of
[
19
]
also does not apply directly to the case of infinite-dimensional targets that will be treated in
[
21
]
, since the appropriate gauge fixing needed to reduce to the present considerations (the axial gauge fixing) is not compatible with the aforementioned compactification of configuration spaces. Actually, the failure of (
89
) has not only the consequence that the boundary operator
\Omega
does not square to zero, but also that the classical master equation
(S_{\text{BV}},S_{\text{BV}})=0
is not enough for the regularized QME to hold in the bulk. What will save the game in
[
21
]
, where we will consider Chern–Simons theories on cylinders with the axial gauge, is that the theories considered there are examples of the linear or of the biaffine case.

10.3.
The boundary structure in Case II

We use
\tilde{f}=p_{i}q^{i}+c^{\alpha}b_{\alpha}
, so

\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\tilde{f}}=\alpha_{\text{BFV}}+\mathrm{d}\tilde{f}(q_{b},c_{b},p^{b},b^{b})=p_{i}^{a}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{a}+b_{\alpha}^{a}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{a}+q_{b}^{i}\mathrm{d}p^{b}_{i}-c^{\alpha}_{b}\mathrm{d}b_{\alpha}^{b}.

In this case, the prescriptions of Section
9.2.6
yield

p^{a}=q_{a}^{*},\quad b^{a}=c_{a}^{*},\quad q_{b}=(p^{b})^{*},\quad c_{b}=-(b^{b})^{*}

and

\displaystyle\Omega_{a}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}_{a}H_{\alpha}\left(\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{a}},q_{a}\right)-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,c^{\alpha}_{a}c^{\beta}_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial c^{\gamma}_{a}},

\displaystyle\Omega_{b}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\hbar H_{\alpha}\left(p^{b},\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{b}}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\alpha}^{b}}+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,b_{\gamma}^{b}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial b_{\alpha}^{b}\partial b_{\beta}^{b}}.

Again
\Omega^{2}=0
if and only if (
89
) is satisfied at both endpoints.

10.4.
The boundary structure in Case III

In this case have to use the one-form

\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\hat{f}}=p_{i}^{a}\mathrm{d}q^{i}_{a}+c^{\alpha}_{a}\mathrm{d}b_{\alpha}^{a}+q_{b}^{i}\mathrm{d}p^{b}_{i}-b_{\alpha}^{b}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}_{b}.

To achieve this we add
p_{i}q^{i}
at the final endpoint of the BV action, like in Cases I and II, but now we subtract
c^{\alpha}b_{\alpha}
at the initial endpoint. The function
\hat{f}
is now more precisely written as
p^{b}_{i}q^{i}_{b}-c^{\alpha}_{a}b_{\alpha}^{a}
and we have
\alpha_{\text{BFV}}^{\hat{f}}=\alpha_{\text{BFV}}+\mathrm{d}{\hat{f}}
.
In this case, the prescriptions of Section
9.2.6
yield

p^{a}=q_{a}^{*},\quad c_{a}=(b^{a})^{*},\quad q_{b}=(p^{b})^{*},\quad b^{b}=-c_{b}^{*},

and

\displaystyle\Omega_{a}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\hbar H_{\alpha}\left(\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{a}},q_{a}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\alpha}^{a}}+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,b_{\gamma}^{a}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial b_{\alpha}^{a}\partial b_{\beta}^{a}},

\displaystyle\Omega_{b}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}_{b}H_{\alpha}\left(p^{b},\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{b}}\right)+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,c^{\alpha}_{b}c^{\beta}_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial c^{\gamma}_{b}}.

Also in this case
\Omega^{2}=0
if and only if (
89
) is satisfied at both endpoints.

11.
BV-BFV quantization with linear polarizations

In this section we quantize the constrained one-dimensional systems with linear polarizations on the endpoints.
We refer to the three cases whose boundary structure has been discussed in Section
10
(see Section
10.1
for the list of the three case).
We postpone the more complicated Case I and start with Cases II and III.

After that, we will discuss the gluing of intervals and the composition of the corresponding states. We will conclude the section with the quantization of the systems with nontrivial evolution discussed in Section
5
at the classical level: in particular, classical mechanics, Section
5.1
, and the free relativistic particle, Section
5.2
.

11.1.
Quantization in Case II

First we have to subtract the final-endpoint pullback of
\tilde{f}=p_{i}q^{i}+c^{\alpha}b_{\alpha}
from the BV action getting

S_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}=S_{\text{BV}}-p_{i}(t_{b})q^{i}(t_{b})+c^{\alpha}(t_{b})e^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{b}).

Next we pick the discontinuous splitting
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{B}
of Example
9.4
for all bulk fields. Namely, by decorating the elements of
\mathcal{Y}
with a hat, we set

\displaystyle q(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}q_{a}&t=t_{a}\\
\hat{q}(t)&t>t_{a}\end{cases},

\displaystyle\quad c(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}c_{a}&t=t_{a}\\
\hat{c}(t)&t>t_{a}\end{cases},

\displaystyle p(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\hat{p}(t)&t<t_{b}\\
p^{b}&t=t_{b}\end{cases},

\displaystyle\quad e^{+}(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\hat{e}^{+}(t)&t<t_{b}\\
-b^{b}&t=t_{b}\end{cases},

with boundary conditions

\hat{q}(t_{a})=0,\quad\hat{c}(t_{a})=0,\quad\hat{p}(t_{b})=0,\quad\hat{e}^{+}(t_{b})=0.

We decorate the remaining fields, all belonging to
\mathcal{Y}
, also with a hat:
\hat{p}^{+},\hat{q}_{+},\hat{e},\hat{c}^{+}
.
We now formally integrate by parts
51
51

51

See Remark
9.5
.
the terms
p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}
and
e^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}

in
S_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}
,

\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}\to-\hat{p}_{i}(t_{a})q_{a}^{i}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}^{i},\qquad\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}\to\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{a})c_{a}^{\alpha}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha},

in order to get the BV action adapted to this splitting:

\begin{split}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=&-p_{i}^{b}\hat{q}^{i}(t_{b})-\hat{c}^{\alpha}(t_{b})b_{\alpha}^{b}-\hat{p}_{i}(t_{a})q_{a}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{a})c_{a}^{\alpha}\\
&+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\bigg(\hat{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha}-\hat{e}^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(\hat{p},\hat{q})\\
&+\hat{c}^{\alpha}\left(\hat{q}^{+}_{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial p_{i}}(\hat{p},\hat{q})-\hat{p}_{+}^{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial q^{i}}(\hat{p},\hat{q})\right)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{c}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\hat{c}^{\beta}+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\hat{e}^{\beta}\bigg).\end{split}

We finally pick the gauge-fixing lagrangian

(92)

\mathcal{L}=\{\hat{p}^{+}=\hat{q}^{+}=\hat{e}=\hat{c}^{+}=0\},

getting

\begin{split}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}\Big|_{\mathcal{L}}=&-p_{i}^{b}\hat{q}^{i}(t_{b})-\hat{c}^{\alpha}(t_{b})b_{\alpha}^{b}-\hat{p}_{i}(t_{a})q_{a}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{a})c_{a}^{\alpha}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\left(\hat{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\right).\end{split}

The propagators defined by the kinetic terms and compatible with the boundary conditions are

(93)

\langle\hat{q}^{i}(s)\,\hat{p}_{j}(t)\rangle=\mathrm{i}\hbar\delta^{i}_{j}\,\theta(s-t)\quad\text{ and}\quad\langle\hat{c}^{\alpha}(s)\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\beta}(t)\rangle=\mathrm{i}\hbar\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\,\theta(s-t),

where

\theta(t)=\begin{cases}0&\text{for }t<0\\
1&\text{for }t>0\end{cases}

is the Heaviside step function. We can then compute

Z_{\text{II}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathcal{L}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\hat{f}}}

as

(94)

Z_{\text{II}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},b^{b})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}-b^{b}_{\alpha}c_{a}^{\alpha})}.

One can immediately check that
\Omega Z_{\text{II}}=0
.

The striking result is that
Z_{\text{II}}
is extremely simple and does not depend on the constraints
H_{\alpha}
. Still its definition requires the whole BV-BFV machinery and, in particular, condition (
89
). The reason is that we have chosen a clever gauge fixing that simplifies the computation, but we want to be sure that the result is invariant (up to
\Omega
-coboundary terms) under deformations of the gauge-fixing lagrangian (see, in case, the following Digression).

As we discuss in the following Digression, just deforming the gauge fixing, picking
e
different from zero, does not change the result (in
\Delta^{\Omega}
-cohomology). Still one can get a less trivial answer for Case I, as we will dicuss in Section
11.4
. The present “trivial” answer is anyway useful for gluing solutions on different intervals along the lines of Section
9.2.8
. We will see in Section
11.4
how to get a nontrivial answer.

Digression 11.1

(Change of gauge fixing)
.

By the general BV-BFV philosophy—proved in the case at hand in
Appendix
B
—we know that deformations of
\mathcal{L}

produce
\Omega
-cohomologous results. Instead of using this general result,
as a matter of example we explicitly consider a particular case. Let

\mathcal{L}_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left\{\hat{p}^{+}=\hat{q}^{+}=\hat{c}^{+}=0,\ \hat{e}=e_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\frac{u\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}\right\},

where, for simplicity, we assume
u
to be a constant function on
[t_{a},t_{b}]
. We now have

\begin{split}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\tilde{f}}\Big|_{\mathcal{L}_{u}}=&-p_{i}^{b}\hat{q}^{i}(t_{b})-\hat{c}^{\alpha}(t_{b})b_{\alpha}^{b}-\hat{p}_{i}(t_{a})q_{a}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{a})c_{a}^{\alpha}\\
&+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\left(\hat{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha}-e_{0}^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(\hat{p},\hat{q})+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}e_{0}^{\beta}\right).\end{split}

Let us first consider the case when the structure constants vanish. In this case, we compute, following
[
19
, Section 4.4]
,

Z_{\text{II}}^{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathcal{L}_{u}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\hat{f}}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}-b^{b}_{\alpha}c_{a}^{\alpha})}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}u^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}}(p^{b},q_{a}),

where
\mathrm{e}_{\star}
is the star exponential with respect to the initial star product (
90
). We have

\Omega_{a}Z_{\text{II}}^{u}=c^{\alpha}_{a}Z_{\text{II}}^{u}\star H_{\alpha},\quad\Omega_{b}Z_{\text{II}}^{u}=c^{\alpha}_{a}H_{\alpha}\star Z_{\text{II}}^{u},

so the mQME
\Omega Z_{\text{II}}^{u}=0
is satisfied, provided that (
89
), which now simply reads
[H_{\alpha},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\star}}{{,}}H_{\beta}]=0
, holds. Let us now consider a family of gauge fixings as above, parametrized by a path
u(x)
,
x
is some interval, and check gauge-fixing independence under the assumption (
89
). We have

\frac{\partial Z_{\text{II}}^{u}}{\partial x}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\frac{\mathrm{d}u^{\alpha}}{\mathrm{d}x}H_{\alpha}\star Z_{\text{II}}^{u};

that is,

(95)

\frac{\partial Z_{\text{II}}^{u}}{\partial x}=\Delta^{\Omega}\psi

with
\psi=-b_{\alpha}^{b}\frac{\mathrm{d}u^{\alpha}}{\mathrm{d}x}Z_{\text{II}}^{u}
and
\Delta^{\Omega}=\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\Omega
in accordance with (
86
). The general case with nonvanishing structure constants is a bit more complicated. We only report the final computations. As in Section
4.2
, we view the
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
s as structure constants of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
for a certain basis; we then view
objects with an upper Greek index as taking value in
\mathfrak{g}
and objects with a lower Greek index as taking values in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
; finally, we denote by

\langle\ ,\ \rangle
the pairing of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
with
\mathfrak{g}
. We then get

Z_{\text{II}}^{u}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\left(p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}-\langle b^{b},\,\Ad_{\mathrm{e}^{u}}c_{a}\rangle\right)}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,\,u\rangle}(p^{b},q_{a}).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{a}Z_{\text{II}}^{u}

\displaystyle=Z_{\text{II}}^{u}\star\langle H,\,c_{a}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle b^{b},\,\Ad_{\mathrm{e}^{u}}[c_{a},c_{a}]\rangle\,Z_{\text{II}}^{u},

\displaystyle\Omega_{b}Z_{\text{II}}^{u}

\displaystyle=\langle H,\,\Ad_{\mathrm{e}^{u}}c_{a}\rangle\star Z_{\text{II}}^{u}-\frac{1}{2}\langle b^{b},\,\Ad_{\mathrm{e}^{u}}[c_{a},c_{a}]\rangle\,Z_{\text{II}}^{u},

and hence

\displaystyle\Omega Z_{\text{II}}^{u}

\displaystyle=Z_{\text{II}}^{u}\star\left(\langle H,\,c_{a}\rangle-\left\langle\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,\,u\rangle}\star H\star\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,\,u\rangle},\,\Ad_{\mathrm{e}^{u}}c_{a}\right\rangle\right).

Using (
89
), one can then easily prove that

\chi(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\left\langle\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}s\langle H,\,u\rangle}\star H\star\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}s\langle H,\,u\rangle},\,\Ad_{\mathrm{e}^{su}}c_{a}\right\rangle

is constant in
s
, and therefore
\Omega Z_{\text{II}}^{u}=0
. If we consider again a family of gauge fixings parametrized by a path
u(x)
and assume (
89
), we get again (
95
), now with
\psi=-\left\langle b^{b},\,\Ad_{\mathrm{e}^{u}}\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}x}\right\rangle Z_{\text{II}}^{u}
.

11.2.
Quantization in Case III

Case III is very similar to Case II. We proceed very quickly, just outlining the differences. First, the BV action
for the given boundary polarizations is

S_{\text{BV}}^{\hat{f}}=S_{\text{BV}}-p_{i}(t_{b})q^{i}(t_{b})-c^{\alpha}(t_{a})e^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{a}).

In picking the discontinuous extension,
p
and
q
are realized as in Case II, with the same boundary conditions, whereas

(96)

e^{+}(t)=\begin{cases}-b^{a}&t=t_{a}\\
\hat{e}^{+}(t)&t>t_{a}\end{cases},\qquad c(t)=\begin{cases}\hat{c}(t)&t<t_{b}\\
c_{b}&t=t_{b}\end{cases},

with boundary conditions

(97)

\hat{e}^{+}(t_{a})=0,\quad\hat{c}(t_{b})=0.

The BV action adapted to this splitting is then

(98)

\begin{split}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{\hat{f}}=&-p_{i}^{b}\hat{q}^{i}(t_{b})-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{b})c^{\alpha}_{b}-\hat{p}_{i}(t_{a})q_{a}^{i}+\hat{c}^{\alpha}(t_{a})b^{a}_{\alpha}\\
&+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\bigg(\hat{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha}-\hat{e}^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(\hat{p},\hat{q})\\
&+\hat{c}^{\alpha}\left(\hat{q}^{+}_{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial p_{i}}(\hat{p},\hat{q})-\hat{p}_{+}^{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial q^{i}}(\hat{p},\hat{q})\right)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{c}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\hat{c}^{\beta}+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\hat{e}^{\beta}\bigg).\end{split}

We pick again the gauge-fixing lagrangian (
92
). This produces the same
\hat{p}\hat{q}
propagator as in Case II, see (
93
), but now we have

\langle\hat{e}^{+}_{\beta}(s)\,\hat{c}^{\alpha}(t)\rangle=\mathrm{i}\hbar\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\,\theta(s-t).

We then get

Z_{\text{III}}(q_{a},b^{a},p^{b},c_{b})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}-c_{b}^{\alpha}b^{a}_{\alpha})}.

One can immediately check that
\Omega Z_{\text{III}}=0
.

The considerations of Digression
11.1
of course apply also to this case.

11.3.
Quantization in Case I

Finally, we consider Case I. Now we have to subtract the final-endpoint pullback of
f=p_{i}q^{i}
from the BV action getting

S_{\text{BV}}^{f}=S_{\text{BV}}-p_{i}(t_{b})q^{i}(t_{b}).

Since the boundary values
c_{a}
and
c_{b}
are given (as coordinates of
\mathcal{B}
), the fluctuations
\hat{c}
of
the ghost fields
\hat{c}
, in the discontinuous splitting, will have vanishing boundary conditions at both endpoints. On the other hand, there are no endpoint conditions on the fluctuations
\hat{e}^{+}
. Proceeding this way, we would get, as in the previous cases, a term
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha}
in the action. This term is not well suited for the perturbative expansion as
\mathrm{d}
now has a kernel on the space of the
\hat{e}^{+}
fields (namely, the constant ones). Since we compute the functional integral perturbatively, we have to remove this constant term, call it
T^{+}
. More precisely, we are going to proceed as in Section
9.2.7
; namely, in addition to a splitting
\tilde{F}=\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{B}
, we also split
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{Y}_{2}
, where
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
contains the fluctuations (which we will denote with a hat) over which we integrate by perturbation theory, and
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
is a finite-dimensional, odd symplectic space of which the
T^{+}
s are half of the coordinates. The other half will have to be variables
T
which are part of the fields
e
. In general, we will not integrate over
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
, but in some cases this is possible (yet one has to be careful that there are in general nonequivalent choices of gauge-fixing lagrangians: see Example
9.8
and Remark
11.5
).
In accordance with
[
19
]
, we call
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
the space of residual fields.

Explicitly, the spaces
\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{2},\mathcal{B}
are defined as follows. In
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
we have coordinates
(T^{\alpha},T^{+}_{\alpha})
with canonical odd symplectic structure
\delta T^{+}_{\alpha}\,\delta T^{\alpha}
. In
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
we have the hat fields
\hat{p},\hat{q},\hat{e},\hat{c},\hat{p}_{+},\hat{q}^{+},\hat{e}^{+},\hat{c}^{+}
that must obey the following conditions

(99)

\hat{q}(t_{a})=0,\quad\hat{c}(t_{a})=0,\quad\hat{p}(t_{b})=0,\quad\hat{c}(t_{b})=0,\quad\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{e}^{+}\mathrm{d}t=0,\quad\quad\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{e}=0.

Finally,
\mathcal{B}
has coordinates
q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b}
.
We define
\tilde{F}=\mathcal{Y}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{Y}_{2}\oplus\mathcal{B}
via

q(t)=\begin{cases}q_{a}&t=t_{a}\\
\hat{q}(t)&t>t_{a}\end{cases},\qquad p(t)=\begin{cases}\hat{p}(t)&t<t_{b}\\
p^{b}&t=t_{b}\end{cases},\qquad c(t)=\begin{cases}c_{a}&t=t_{a}\\
\hat{c}(t)&t_{a}<t<t_{b}\\
c_{b}&t=t_{b}\end{cases},

e(t)=\frac{T\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}+\hat{e}(t),\quad e^{+}(t)=T^{+}+\hat{e}^{+}(t),

and

p_{+}(t)=\hat{p}_{+}(t),\quad q^{+}(t)=\hat{q}^{+}(t),\quad c^{+}(t)=\hat{c}^{+}(t).

To get the adapted BV action, we use the replacements

\begin{split}\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}p_{i}\mathrm{d}q^{i}&\to-\hat{p}_{i}(t_{a})q_{a}^{i}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}^{i},\\
\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}e^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}c^{\alpha}&\to-T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{b}^{\alpha}-c_{a}^{\alpha})-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{b})c_{b}^{\alpha}+\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{a})c_{a}^{\alpha}+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha},\end{split}

which yield

\begin{split}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{f}&=T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{b}^{\alpha}-c_{a}^{\alpha})-p_{i}^{b}\hat{q}^{i}(t_{b})+\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{b})c_{b}^{\alpha}-\hat{p}_{i}(t_{a})q_{a}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{a})c_{a}^{\alpha}\\
&+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\bigg(\hat{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha}-\frac{T^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}H_{\alpha}(\hat{p},\hat{q})-\hat{e}^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}(\hat{p},\hat{q})\\
&+\hat{c}^{\alpha}\left(\hat{q}^{+}_{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial p_{i}}(\hat{p},\hat{q})-\hat{p}_{+}^{i}\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial q^{i}}(\hat{p},\hat{q})\right)-\frac{1}{2}f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{c}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\hat{c}^{\beta}\\
&-f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,T^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\frac{T^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\frac{T^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}-f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,T^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\hat{e}^{\beta}+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\hat{e}^{\beta}\bigg).\end{split}

On the gauge-fixing lagrangian (
92
) we have

\begin{split}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{f}\Big|_{\mathcal{L}}&=T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{b}^{\alpha}-c_{a}^{\alpha})-p_{i}^{b}\hat{q}^{i}(t_{b})+\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{b})c_{b}^{\alpha}-\hat{p}_{i}(t_{a})q_{a}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}(t_{a})c_{a}^{\alpha}\\
&+\int_{t_{a}}^{t_{b}}\bigg(\hat{p}_{i}\mathrm{d}\hat{q}^{i}-\hat{e}^{+}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\hat{c}^{\alpha}-\frac{T^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}H_{\alpha}(\hat{p},\hat{q})\\
&-f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,T^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\frac{T^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}+f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\gamma}\hat{c}^{\alpha}\frac{T^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}\bigg).\end{split}

The
\hat{p}\hat{q}
propagator is the same as in Case II, see (
93
), but the
\hat{c}\hat{e}^{+}
propagator now has to take into account the conditions (
99
) on
\hat{c}
and
\hat{e}^{+}
. We get

(100)

\langle\hat{c}^{\alpha}(s)\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\beta}(t)\rangle=\mathrm{i}\hbar\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\,(\theta(s-t)-\phi(s))

with

\phi(s)=\frac{s-t_{a}}{t_{b}-t_{a}}.

Note that there are no mixed terms involving the physical fields
\hat{p}\hat{q}
and the ghost fields
\hat{c}\hat{e}^{+}

in
\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{f}\Big|_{\mathcal{L}}
, so the partition function
Z_{\text{I}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\mathcal{L}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}_{\text{BV}}^{f}}
is the product of a “physical part”
Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{phys}}
and a “ghost part”
Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{gh}}
. The physical part is computed following
[
19
, Section 4.4]
:

Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{phys}}(q_{a},p^{b};T)=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}}(p^{b},q_{a}).

Remark 11.2
.

The
\star
-exponential arises from Feynman diagrams consisting of multi-edge linear graphs of arbitrary length. The lowest-order terms are the linear trees. Note that each vertex (either in the bulk or at one endpoint) contributes with a factor
\frac{1}{\hbar}
, whereas each edge contributes with a factor
\hbar
, so a tree of any length yields a global factor
\frac{1}{\hbar}
. Moreover, such trees simply define the (semi)classical evolution operator, i.e., the Hamilton–Jacobi action. Therefore,

(101)

Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{phys}}(q_{a},p^{b};T)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b},\mathrm{e}^{T})+O(\hbar))}

with the HJ action as in (
37
).

Remark 11.3

(Semiclassical expansion)
.

The last formula can also be obtained as follows.
As we have seen, in the Lie algebra case and in the chosen gauge fixing, the physical part completely decouples from the ghost part. Moreover, as in Remark
4.10
,
we may view the gauge-fixed action for the physical part as the action for a mechanical system with hamiltonian
\tilde{H}={\langle H,T\rangle}
(and time variable
s=\frac{t}{t_{b}-t_{a}}
). For such a system we may go back, via a translation in the functional integral, to the usual nondiscontinuous choice of fields,
p(t)=p_{q_{a},p^{b}}(t)+\check{p}(t)
and
q(t)=q_{q_{a},p^{b}}(t)+\check{q}(t)
,
and integrate over the fluctuations
\check{p}
and
\check{q}
, getting the semiclassical expansion (
101
). Note that, on the other hand, the discontinuous extension, besides being the correct one in general for BV-BFV, is also interesting because it produces the
\star
-product representation of the partition function.
52
52

52

Cf. the Clifford-exponential presentation of the nonabelian 1D Chern–Simons partition function in
[
1
]
.

We may summarize the content of equation (
101
) and of the two above remarks in the following

Theorem 11.4
.

The physical part of
the dominant contribution of the perturbative expansion of the BV-BFV partition function, in the polarization of Case I, is the HJ action.

The ghost part is very easy to compute in the abelian case,
f_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}=0
, since the interaction terms vanish and the boundary source terms cannot be coupled to each other:

(102)

Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{gh,abelian}}(c_{a},c_{b};T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{a}^{\alpha}-c_{b}^{\alpha})}.

Therefore,

(103)

Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{abelian}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{\alpha}H_{\alpha}}(p^{b},q_{a})\;\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{a}^{\alpha}-c_{b}^{\alpha})}.

It is very easy in this case to check that the mQME holds. In fact, we have

\Omega_{a}Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{abelian}}=c^{\alpha}_{a}Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{abelian}}\star H_{\alpha},\quad\Omega_{b}Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{abelian}}=c^{\alpha}_{b}H_{\alpha}\star Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{abelian}}.

Therefore, since (
89
) implies in this case that the
H
s
\star
-commute,

\Omega Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{abelian}}=(c^{\alpha}_{a}-c^{\alpha}_{b})H_{\alpha}\star Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{abelian}}.

Since the right hand side is also clearly equal to
-\hbar^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial T^{\alpha}\partial T^{+}_{\alpha}}Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{abelian}}
, the mQME is satisfied.

In the nonabelian case, the ghost part
Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{gh}}
can be computed in terms of Feynman diagrams. In Figure
1
we introduce a notation for the ghost propagator.

\hat{e}^{+}

\hat{c}

F

T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{a}^{\alpha}-c_{b}^{\alpha})

\mathbb{W}
-diagrams

To get a more readable expression, we use again the notations of Section
4.2
, and of the second part of Digression
11.1
, in terms of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, its dual
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and their pairing
\langle\ ,\ \rangle
. We get

(104)

Z_{\text{I}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,T\rangle}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle T^{+},F_{-}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})c_{b}+F_{+}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})c_{a}\rangle}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathbb{W}(T)}.

The series
F_{+},F_{-},\mathbb{W}
can be explicitly computed, although we do not present the details here:

\displaystyle F_{+}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{x}{1-e^{-x}}=\sum_{n\geq 0}(-1)^{n}\frac{B_{n}}{n!}x^{n},

\displaystyle F_{-}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{e^{x}-1}=-\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{B_{n}}{n!}x^{n},

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}(T)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{B_{n}}{n\cdot n!}\,\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})^{n}=\mathrm{tr}\log\frac{\sinh\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{T}}{2}}{\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{T}}{2}},

where the
B_{n}
s are the Bernoulli numbers:
B_{0}=1
,
B_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}
,
B_{2}=\frac{1}{6}
,
B_{3}=0
,
B_{4}=-\frac{1}{30}
,…. The mQME can also be directly verified.

Remark 11.5

(Reduction of the residual fields)
.

We may sometimes perform a further BV pushforward on
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
. One possibility, which leads to a less interesting result as in Case II, consists in choosing the lagrangian
\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}=\{T=0\}
. We get in this case

(105)

Z_{\text{I}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\delta(c_{b}-c_{a}).

More interesting choices consist in taking
\mathcal{L}
to be the conormal bundle, shifted by
-1
, of a submanifold of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. For example, we can take the whole
\mathfrak{g}
as such a submanifold, and therefore
\mathcal{L}=\{T^{+}=0\}
, which yields

(106)

Z_{\text{I}}^{\mathcal{L}}(q_{a},p^{b})=\int_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,T\rangle}\;d^{k}T,

where
k=\dim\mathfrak{g}
and
d^{k}T
is the Lebesgue measure (of course under the assumption that the integral converges).
Note that
Z_{\text{I}}^{\mathcal{L}}
does no longer depend on the ghost variables.

Remark 11.6

(Passing to the group)
.

In the nonabelian case, assuming the exponential map to be surjective,
it is convenient to make a change of variables, setting
g=\psi(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathrm{e}^{T}
. A first advantage of this transformation is that the semiclassical term of the physical part of

Z_{\text{I}}
is brought to the form of Section
4.2
. Since we are in the BV setting, we have to complete this transformation to a symplectomorphism.
We do it by taking the cotangent lift:

\Psi\colon\begin{array}[t]{ccc}T^{*}[-1]\mathfrak{g}&\to&T^{*}[-1]G\\
(T,T^{+})&\mapsto&\left(\mathrm{e}^{T},(\mathrm{d}_{e}l_{\mathrm{e}^{T}})^{*,-1}\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{T}^{*}}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{ad}_{T}^{*}}}T^{+}\right),\end{array}

where
e
denotes the identity element of
G
and

l_{g}
the left multiplication by
g
:

l_{g}(h)=gh
. The first term of the ghost part now simplifies as follows:

\langle T^{+},F_{-}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})c_{b}+F_{+}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})c_{a}\rangle=\langle(\mathrm{d}_{e}l_{g})^{*}g^{+},c_{a}-\Ad_{g}^{-1}c_{b}\rangle.

Finally, we have to observe that, more appropriately for the BV formalism
[
37
,
45
]
, the partition function should be regarded as a half-density:

\hat{Z}_{\text{I}}=Z_{\text{I}}\sqrt{d^{k}Td^{k}T^{+}},

with
k=\dim\mathfrak{g}
,
d^{k}T
the Lebesgue measure, and
d^{k}T^{+}
the standard Berezinian measure.
We have, again under the assumption of unimodularity (see Remark
10.1
),
53
53

53

In the case of a nonunimodular Lie algebra, one may extend the
\hat{c}\hat{e}^{+}
propagator of (
100
) to the diagonal as

\langle\hat{c}^{\alpha}(s)\,\hat{e}^{+}_{\beta}(s)\rangle=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\,\phi(s)
. With this regularization, the tadpoles produce the additional factor
\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\,\mathrm{ad}_{T}}
in
Z_{\text{I}}
. On the other hand, without the assumption of unimodularity, one has

\psi^{*}\mu_{G}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathbb{W}(T)}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\,\mathrm{ad}_{T}}d^{k}T
, so the results discussed in this remark in principle also hold without the assumption of unimodularity.

that

\psi^{*}\mu_{G}=\det\frac{\sinh\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{T}}{2}}{\frac{\mathrm{ad}_{T}}{2}}\ d^{k}T=\mathrm{e}^{\mathbb{W}(T)}d^{k}T,

where
\mu_{G}
is the Haar measure.
Therefore,

\hat{Z}_{\text{I}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b};g,g^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,\log g\rangle}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle(\mathrm{d}_{e}l_{g})^{*}g^{+},c_{a}-\Ad_{g}^{-1}c_{b}\rangle}\;\mu_{G},

where we reinterpret the Haar measure
\mu_{G}
on
G
as a half-density on
T^{*}[-1]G
. We can now “improve”
(
106
) by taking
\mathcal{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{g^{+}=0\}=G
—the zero section of
T^{*}[-1]G
—getting

(107)

Z_{\text{I}}^{\mathcal{L}}(q_{a},p^{b})=\int_{G}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,\log g\rangle}\;d\mu_{G},

which converges for
G
compact.
The integral in (
106
) usually does not converge, as it corresponds to summing infinitely many copies of the integral over
G
(this is an instance of what is known as Gribov’s ambiguity in the physics literature). The “improved” formula (
107
) corresponds to choosing one single domain in
\mathfrak{g}
diffeomorphic to
G
(up to a measure zero subset). A similar procedure was developed in
[
36
]
.

We conclude by discussing two important examples.

Example 11.7

(Linear case)
.

Consider linear constraints as in Example
4.3
. There are then two simplifications. The first is that in the computation of
Z_{I}^{\text{phys}}
we only have linear vertices in
\hat{q}
and
\hat{p}
, so the only connected Feynman graphs have one single edge: connecting two boundary points, or one boundary and a bulk point, or two bulk points. See Figure
5
for our notation for the physical propagator and
Figure
6
for the connected Feynman graphs.

\hat{q}
\hat{p}

Figure 5
.
Physical propagator

,
,
,
p^{b}
q_{a}
p^{b}
T
q_{a}
T
T
T

Figure 6
.
Linear case

These graphs correspond exactly to the terms in (
24
) (this result is of course consistent with Remarks
11.2
and
11.3
.) The second simplification is that the structure functions vanish, so the ghost part is simply the one of equation (
102
). In conclusion, we get

(108)

\begin{split}Z_{\text{I}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})&=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\left(p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}+T^{\alpha}(p^{b}_{i}v_{\alpha}^{i}+w_{\alpha,i}q_{a}^{i})+\frac{1}{2}T^{\alpha}T^{\beta}A_{\alpha\beta}+T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{a}^{\alpha}-c_{b}^{\alpha})\right)}\\
&=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b},T)}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}_{\alpha}\,(c_{a}^{\alpha}-c_{b}^{\alpha})},\end{split}

with
\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}
as in (
24
). This result is the prototype for abelian Chern–Simons theory, see
[
21
, Sections 5.3.1 and 6]
. We may also integrate over the residual fields with the gauge fixing
T^{+}=0
as in (
106
). In general, there will be some linear terms in
T
, corresponding to the kernel of
A
, which produce delta functions. The remaining terms yield a Gaussian integration which produces quadratic terms in the
p,q
variables. This is the quantum analogue of solving some of the constraints. For example, in the case of Remark
3.3
, we get

Z_{\text{I}}^{\mathcal{L}}(q_{a},p^{b})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\tilde{S}^{f}(q_{a},p^{b})}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{v}{w}(p^{b})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{w}{v}(q_{a})^{2}\right)}.

.

and in the

variables, see Example

,

\hat{Z}_{\text{I}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},c_{b};g,g^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\hat{S}^{f}_{\text{HJ}}(q_{a},p^{b},g)}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle(\mathrm{d}_{e}l_{g})^{*}g^{+},c_{a}-\Ad_{g}^{-1}c_{b}\rangle}\,\mu_{G}\\

11.4.
Gluing

Partition functions may be composed as described in Section
9.2.8
. Here we are interested in composing
a partition function from
(q_{a},c_{a})
to
(p^{1},c_{1})
with
a partition function from
(p^{1},c_{1})
to
(q_{2},c_{2})
with
a partition function from
(q_{2},c_{2})
to
(p^{b},b^{b})
, where
(p^{1},c_{1})
and
(q_{2},c_{2})
are some intermediate boundary fields. The first partition function belongs to Case I and the last to Case II. The intermediate one will be discussed below. The result will be a partition function from
(q_{a},c_{a})
to
(p^{b},b^{b})
, so of type II. See
Figure
8
.

(q_{a},c_{a})
(p^{b},b^{b})
Z^{\text{new}}_{\text{II}}
=
(q_{a},c_{a})
(p^{1},c_{1})
Z_{\text{I}}
(q_{2},c_{2})
Z^{\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}_{\text{I'}}
(p^{b},b^{b})
Z_{\text{II}}

Figure 8
.
Composition of the partition function
Z_{\text{II}}^{\text{new}}

The intermediate partition function can be computed along the lines of Case I, with the only difference that we now fix
p
at the initial endpoint and
q
at the final one (instead of the other way around). We are actually interested in the version where we integrate out the residual fields, as we did in Remark
11.5
for Case I. The analogue of (
105
) is now

(110)

Z_{\text{I'}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}(p^{a},c_{a},q_{b},c_{b})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{a}_{i}q_{b}^{i}}\;\delta(c_{b}-c_{a}),

and we refer to this as Case I’, in the trivial gauge fixing.

We now proceed to the computation of the “new” version of Case II as

Z_{\text{II}}^{\text{new}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},b^{b};T,T^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int Z_{\text{I}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{1},c_{1};T,T^{+})\\
Z_{\text{I'}}^{\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}(p^{1},c_{1},q_{2},c_{2})\,Z_{\text{II}}(q_{2},c_{2},p^{b},b^{b})\;\frac{d^{n}p^{1}\,d^{n}q_{2}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\ {d^{k}c_{1}\,d^{k}c_{2}}(-\mathrm{i}\hbar)^{k},

where
n
is the dimension of the target configuration space,
k
is the dimension of the Lie algebra, and we have conveniently normalized the measure. A simple computation yields

Z_{\text{II}}^{\text{new}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},b^{b};T,T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,T\rangle}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle T^{+},F_{+}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})c_{a}\rangle}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathbb{W}(T)}\;\delta(b^{b}+F_{-}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})^{*}T^{+}).

If we now reduce it using the “trivial” gauge-fixing lagrangian
\{T=0\}
, we get exactly
Z_{\text{II}}
as in
(
94
).

If we instead choose the gauge-fixing lagrangian
\{T^{+}=0\}
, possibly passing to group variables, typically we get a less trivial, nonequivalent result.

This shows that the results of Section
11.1
actually correspond to choosing a somewhat trivial gauge fixing, but that more interesting results can be obtained also in this case. We saw in Digression
11.1
that just deforming the gauge fixing, picking
e
different from zero, does not change the result (in
\Delta^{\Omega}
-cohomology). To get interesting results, we should actually split the space
\mathcal{Y}
as
\mathcal{Y}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{Y}_{2}
, make (the analogue of) gauge fixing (
92
) and then choose a nontrivial gauge fixing on
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
. To get a glimpse of (the outcome of) this procedure, take the formula that defined
Z_{\text{II}}^{\text{new}}
but now without integrating over
c_{1}
. We get

\tilde{Z}_{\text{II}}^{\text{new}}(q_{a},c_{a},p^{b},b^{b};T,T^{+},c_{1})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\;\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle H,T\rangle}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle T^{+},F_{-}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})c_{1}+F_{+}(\mathrm{ad}_{T})c_{a}\rangle}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathbb{W}(T)}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\langle b^{b},c_{1}\rangle}.

We should view this as a BV pushforward, where we have set to zero the momenta
c_{1}^{+}
of
c_{1}
.
The residual fields
T,T^{+},c_{1},c_{1}^{+}
arise as part of the fields
e,e^{+},c,c^{+}
.

Remark 11.9
.

The above discussion shows that leaving some residual fields is important not to end up with a trivial answer. It moreover teaches us that, in gluing several intervals together, it is enough to retain residual fields in the partition function associated to one interval, whereas on each of the others we can safely consider a fully reduced, “trivial” partition function. Similar considerations were presented in
[
36
, Remark 3.13]
.

11.5.
Quantum mechanics

The system described in Section
5.1
is a particular case of what we have considered above.
The only, notational, difference is that we have an additional position variable
\mathbb{t}
and its momentum
E
; moreover, they appear in the action with an extra minus sign. Since there is a single hamiltonian,
H-E
, we are in the abelian case of (
103
).
This taken into account, we get

Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},p^{b},E^{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\\
=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{a}-p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i})}\,\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T(H-E)}\!(p^{b},E^{b},q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a})\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}\,(c_{b}-c_{a})}.

Since
E
commutes with the
(p,q)
variables and
H
does not depend on
\mathbb{t}
, we actually have

\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T(H-E)}(p^{b},E^{b},q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}TE^{b}}\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}TH}(p^{b},q_{a}),

so

Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},p^{b},E^{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}E^{b}(\mathbb{t}_{a}+T)}\,\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}TH}\!(p^{b},q_{a})\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}\,(c_{b}-c_{a})},

which is the quantum version of (
39
).

We are also interested in the partition function from
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a})
to
(q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b})
. We can obtain it, following the strategy of Section
11.4
, by composing
a partition function from
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a})
to
(p^{1},E^{1},c_{1})
with
a partition function from
(p^{1},E^{1},c_{1})
to
(q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b})
. The first is Case I,
Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{QM}}
, with a relabeling of the endpoint variables. The second is of type I’, which again we compute with trivial gauge fixing as in (
110
):

(111)

Z_{\text{I'}}^{\text{QM},\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}(p^{a},E^{a},c_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{a}_{i}q_{b}^{i}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}E^{a}\mathbb{t}_{b}}\;\delta(c_{b}-c_{a}).

We then get

\tilde{Z}^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},p^{1},E^{1},c_{1};T,T^{+})\\
Z_{\text{I'}}^{\text{QM},\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}(p^{1},E^{1},c_{1},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b})\;\frac{d^{n}p^{1}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\,\frac{dE^{1}}{2\pi\hbar}\,dc_{1},

that is,

\tilde{Z}^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\int\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{1}_{i}(q_{b}^{i}-q_{a}^{i})}\,\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}TH}\!(p^{1},q_{a})\\
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}\,(c_{b}-c_{a})}\,\delta(T+\mathbb{t}_{a}-\mathbb{t}_{b})\;\frac{d^{n}p^{1}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}.

In this case, using the gauge-fixing lagrangian
\mathcal{L}=\{T^{+}=0\}
,

Z^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int\tilde{Z}^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b};T,0)\;dT,

yields the interesting result

Z^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})=\int\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{1}_{i}(q_{b}^{i}-q_{a}^{i})}\,\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a})H}\!(p^{1},q_{a})\;\frac{d^{n}p^{1}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}},

which is the quantum version of (
40
). To see this more clearly, observe that we have the relation

\langle p|\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T\hat{H}}|q\rangle=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p_{i}q^{i}}\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}TH}\!(p,q)

between the operator formalism and the
\star
-product formalism. Therefore,

Z^{\text{QM}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b})=\int\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}p^{1}_{i}q_{b}^{i}}\,\langle p^{1}|\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a})\hat{H}}|q_{a}\rangle\;\frac{d^{n}p^{1}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}=\langle q_{b}|\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a})\hat{H}}|q_{a}\rangle.

Therefore, we eventually get the standard quantum mechanics evolution with hamiltonian
\hat{H}
and time lapse

\mathbb{t}_{b}-\mathbb{t}_{a}
. In our picture, however, the original theory was parametrization invariant and
(\mathbb{t}_{b},\mathbb{t}_{a})

are endpoint variables (and not fixed time endpoints).

11.6.
The quantum relativistic particle

We can adapt the discussion of Section
11.5
to the case of the relativistic particle introduced in Section
5.2
. Again we have an additional position variable
\mathbb{t}
and its momentum
E
, and we
are in the abelian case, now with hamiltonian
\frac{1}{2}(p^{2}+m^{2}-E^{2})
. Therefore, we have, see (
103
),

Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{QRP}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},p^{b},E^{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\\
=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{a}-p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i})}\,\mathrm{e}_{\star}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\frac{T}{2}(p^{2}+m^{2}-E^{2})}\!(p^{b},E^{b},q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a})\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}\,(c_{b}-c_{a})}.

The simplification now is that the hamiltonian only depends on momentum variables, so its
\star
-exponential is just the usual exponential, and we get

Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{QRP}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},p^{b},E^{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(E^{b}\mathbb{t}_{a}-p^{b}_{i}q_{a}^{i})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\frac{T}{2}((p^{b})^{2}+m^{2}-(E^{b})^{2})}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}\,(c_{b}-c_{a})}.

This shows that the physical part of the partition function, in this polarization, is exactly the exponential of
\mathrm{i}/\hbar
times the HJ action (
43
) with no quantum corrections.

We can obtain the partition function from
(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a})
to
(q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b})
exactly as in Section
11.5
:

\tilde{Z}^{\text{QRP}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int Z_{\text{I}}^{\text{QRP}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},p^{1},E^{1},c_{1};T,T^{+})\\
Z_{\text{I'}}^{\text{QRP},\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}(p^{1},E^{1},c_{1},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b})\;\frac{d^{n}p^{1}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{n}}\,\frac{dE^{1}}{2\pi\hbar}\,dc_{1},

with
Z_{\text{I'}}^{\text{QRP},\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}=Z_{\text{I'}}^{\text{QM},\mathcal{L}^{\text{triv}}}
, see (
111
). We get

\tilde{Z}^{\text{QRP}}(q_{a},\mathbb{t}_{a},c_{a},q_{b},\mathbb{t}_{b},c_{b};T,T^{+})=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}(1-n)}}{(2\pi\hbar T)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\left(\frac{(\Delta q)^{2}-(\Delta\mathbb{t})^{2}}{2T}-\frac{1}{2}m^{2}T\right)}\;\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}T^{+}\,(c_{b}-c_{a})},

with
\Delta q=q_{b}-q_{a}
and
\Delta\mathbb{t}=t_{b}-t_{a}
.
Again we have that, apart from the prefactor
T^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}
, the physical part of the partition function, in this polarization, is the exponential of
\mathrm{i}/\hbar
times the HJ action (
44
).

Finally, we may integrate over the residual fields using the gauge-fixing lagrangian
\mathcal{L}=\{T^{+}=0\}
. This yields
